 |--- DONE ------------------->|   (bank debits)
 |<-- BYE ---------------------|
 time runs downward

Assumptions about transport: a reliable, in-order, connection-oriented byte stream (as TCP gives): no message is lost, duplicated, corrupted or reordered, and the connection is secured (encrypted and authenticated, e.g. TLS) so a PIN cannot be read or forged. If the connection fails before DONE, the bank does not commit the debit.

U1 · Ch1 · P3~6 min

Consider an application that transmits data at a steady rate (for example, the sender generates an N-bit unit of data every k time units, where k is small and fixed). Also, when such an application starts, it will continue running for a relatively long period of time. Answer the following questions, briefly justifying your answer:

  1. Would a packet-switched network or a circuit-switched network be more appropriate for this application? Why?
  2. Suppose that a packet-switched network is used and the only traffic in this network comes from such applications as described above. Furthermore, assume that the sum of the application data rates is less than the capacities of each and every link. Is some form of congestion control needed? Why?
  1. Circuit switching. The application has a steady, predictable rate and long sessions, so a reserved circuit of rate \(N/k\) is fully used (no wasted idle capacity), and the one-time setup cost is amortized over a long session. In return it gets a guaranteed rate and no queuing delay or jitter.
  2. No congestion control is needed. Since the sum of the rates is below every link's capacity, each link can serve all arriving traffic; at worst a small, bounded queue forms when packets arrive at the same instant, but no queue grows without bound and no packets are lost to overflow, so there is nothing to control.
U1 · Ch1 · P6~10 min

This elementary problem begins to explore propagation delay and transmission delay, two central concepts in data networking. Consider two hosts, A and B, connected by a single link of rate R bps. Suppose that the two hosts are separated by m meters, and suppose the propagation speed along the link is s meters/sec. Host A is to send a packet of size L bits to Host B.

  1. Express the propagation delay, \(d_{prop}\), in terms of m and s.
  2. Determine the transmission time of the packet, \(d_{trans}\), in terms of L and R.
  3. Ignoring processing and queuing delays, obtain an expression for the end-to-end delay.
  4. Suppose Host A begins to transmit the packet at time t = 0. At time t = \(d_{trans}\), where is the last bit of the packet?
  5. Suppose \(d_{prop}\) is greater than \(d_{trans}\). At time t = \(d_{trans}\), where is the first bit of the packet?
  6. Suppose \(d_{prop}\) is less than \(d_{trans}\). At time t = \(d_{trans}\), where is the first bit of the packet?
  7. Suppose s = 2.5 × 108, L = 1500 bytes, and R = 10 Mbps. Find the distance m so that \(d_{prop}\) equals \(d_{trans}\).
  1. \(d_{prop} = m/s\) seconds.
  2. \(d_{trans} = L/R\) seconds.
  3. \(d_{end\text{-}end} = m/s + L/R\).
  4. The last bit has just left Host A (it is at the start of the link).
  5. The first bit is in the link, a distance \(s\cdot d_{trans} = sL/R\) metres from A; it has not reached B.
  6. The first bit has already reached Host B (it arrived at time \(d_{prop} < d_{trans}\)).
  7. Set \(m/s = L/R\):
    \[ d_{trans} = \frac{1500\times 8}{10\times10^6} = 1.2\ \text{ms}, \qquad m = s\,\frac{L}{R} = 2.5\times10^8 \times 1.2\times10^{-3} = 3\times10^5\ \text{m} \]
    m = 300 km.
U1 · Ch1 · P7~8 min

In this problem, we consider sending real-time voice from Host A to Host B over a packet-switched network (VoIP). Host A converts analog voice to a digital 64 kbps bit stream on the fly. Host A then groups the bits into 56-byte packets. There is one link between Hosts A and B; its transmission rate is 10 Mbps and its propagation delay is 10 msec. As soon as Host A gathers a packet, it sends it to Host B. As soon as Host B receives an entire packet, it converts the packet's bits to an analog signal. How much time elapses from the time a bit is created (from the original analog signal at Host A) until the bit is decoded (as part of the analog signal at Host B)?

Follow the first bit of a packet. It must wait until the whole packet is gathered, then the packet is transmitted, then it propagates, and B decodes once the whole packet has arrived.

\[ L = 56\times 8 = 448\ \text{bits} \]
\[ d_{packetize} = \frac{448}{64\,000} = 7\ \text{ms}, \quad d_{trans} = \frac{448}{10\times10^6} = 0.0448\ \text{ms}, \quad d_{prop} = 10\ \text{ms} \]
\[ \text{total} = 7 + 0.0448 + 10 = 17.0448\ \text{ms} \]

About 17.04 ms (dominated by packetization and propagation).

U1 · Ch1 · P8~12 min

Suppose users share a 10 Mbps link. Also suppose each user requires 200 kbps when transmitting, but each user transmits only 10 percent of the time. (See the discussion of packet switching versus circuit switching in Section 1.3.)

  1. When circuit switching is used, how many users can be supported?
  2. For the remainder of this problem, suppose packet switching is used. Find the probability that a given user is transmitting.
  3. Suppose there are 120 users. Find the probability that at any given time, exactly n users are transmitting simultaneously. (Hint: Use the binomial distribution.)
  4. Find the probability that there are 51 or more users transmitting simultaneously.
  1. \[ \frac{10\ \text{Mbps}}{200\ \text{kbps}} = 50 \]
    50 users.
  2. p = 0.1.
  3. \[ P(X=n) = \binom{120}{n}(0.1)^n(0.9)^{120-n} \]
    as above, for n = 0, 1, ..., 120.
  4. \[ P(X\ge 51) = \sum_{n=51}^{120}\binom{120}{n}(0.1)^n(0.9)^{120-n} = 1 - \sum_{n=0}^{50}\binom{120}{n}(0.1)^n(0.9)^{120-n} \]
    By hand: the mean is \(120\times0.1 = 12\) and the standard deviation is \(\sqrt{120\times0.1\times0.9} = \sqrt{10.8} \approx 3.29\). Reaching 51 needs \((50.5-12)/3.29 \approx 11.7\) standard deviations above the mean, so the probability is essentially zero. (Exact value \(\approx 2.1\times10^{-20}\).) \(P(X\ge51) \approx 0\) (about \(2\times10^{-20}\)): packet switching can support 120 users where circuit switching supports only 50.
U1 · Ch1 · P9~6 min

Consider the discussion in Section 1.3 of packet switching versus circuit switching in which an example is provided with a 1 Mbps link. Users are generating data at a rate of 100 kbps when busy, but are busy generating data only with probability p = 0.1. Suppose that the 1 Mbps link is replaced by a 1 Gbps link.

  1. What is N, the maximum number of users that can be supported simultaneously under circuit switching?
  2. Now consider packet switching and a user population of M users. Give a formula (in terms of p, M, N) for the probability that more than N users are sending data.
  1. \[ N = \frac{1\ \text{Gbps}}{100\ \text{kbps}} = \frac{10^9}{10^5} \]
    N = 10,000 users.
  2. The number of active users is binomial(M, p):
    \[ P(X > N) = \sum_{n=N+1}^{M}\binom{M}{n}p^n(1-p)^{M-n} = 1-\sum_{n=0}^{N}\binom{M}{n}p^n(1-p)^{M-n} \]
    as above.
U1 · Ch1 · P10~10 min

Consider a packet of length L that begins at end system A and travels over three links to a destination end system. These three links are connected by two packet switches. Let \(d_i\), \(s_i\), and \(R_i\) denote the length, propagation speed, and the transmission rate of link i, for i = 1, 2, 3. The packet switch delays each packet by \(d_{proc}\). Assuming no queuing delays, in terms of \(d_i\), \(s_i\), \(R_i\), (i = 1, 2, 3), and L, what is the total end-to-end delay for the packet? Suppose now the packet is 1,500 bytes, the propagation speed on all three links is 2.5 × 108 m/s, the transmission rates of all three links are 2.5 Mbps, the packet switch processing delay is 3 msec, the length of the first link is 5,000 km, the length of the second link is 4,000 km, and the length of the last link is 1,000 km. For these values, what is the end-to-end delay?

Store-and-forward: the packet is transmitted on each of the three links, propagates on each, and is processed at each of the two switches.

\[ d_{end\text{-}end} = \frac{L}{R_1}+\frac{L}{R_2}+\frac{L}{R_3}+\frac{d_1}{s_1}+\frac{d_2}{s_2}+\frac{d_3}{s_3}+2\,d_{proc} \]

Numbers: \(L = 12\,000\) bits, so each \(L/R = 12\,000/2.5\times10^6 = 4.8\) ms. Propagation: \(5\times10^6/2.5\times10^8 = 20\) ms, \(16\) ms, \(4\) ms.

\[ 3(4.8) + (20+16+4) + 2(3) = 14.4 + 40 + 6 = 60.4\ \text{ms} \]

60.4 ms

U1 · Ch1 · P12~7 min

A packet switch receives a packet and determines the outbound link to which the packet should be forwarded. When the packet arrives, one other packet is halfway done being transmitted on this outbound link and four other packets are waiting to be transmitted. Packets are transmitted in order of arrival. Suppose all packets are 1,500 bytes and the link rate is 2.5 Mbps. What is the queuing delay for the packet? More generally, what is the queuing delay when all packets have length L, the transmission rate is R, x bits of the currently-being-transmitted packet have been transmitted, and n packets are already in the queue?

The packet waits for the remaining half packet plus four full packets: 4.5 packet times.

\[ \frac{L}{R} = \frac{1500\times8}{2.5\times10^6} = 4.8\ \text{ms}, \qquad d_{queue} = 4.5\times4.8 = 21.6\ \text{ms} \]

21.6 ms. In general:

\[ d_{queue} = \frac{nL + (L - x)}{R} \]

\(d_{queue} = (nL + L - x)/R\)

U1 · Ch1 · P13~8 min
  1. Suppose N packets arrive simultaneously to a link at which no packets are currently being transmitted or queued. Each packet is of length L and the link has transmission rate R. What is the average queuing delay for the N packets?
  2. Now suppose that N such packets arrive to the link every LN/R seconds. What is the average queuing delay of a packet?
  1. The k-th packet (k = 1, ..., N) waits for the k-1 packets ahead of it: \((k-1)L/R\).
    \[ \overline{d} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{(k-1)L}{R} = \frac{L}{RN}\cdot\frac{(N-1)N}{2} = \frac{(N-1)L}{2R} \]
    \((N-1)L/(2R)\)
  2. Transmitting a batch takes exactly \(NL/R\), the batch interval, so the link is just empty when the next batch arrives. Every batch sees the situation of part (a): average queuing delay \((N-1)L/(2R)\).
U1 · Ch1 · P19~4 min

Metcalfe's law states the value of a computer network is proportional to the square of the number of connected users of the system. Let n denote the number of users in a computer network. Assuming each user sends one message to each of the other users, how many messages will be sent? Does your answer support Metcalfe's law?

Each of the n users sends one message to each of the other n-1 users:

\[ n(n-1) = n^2 - n \]

n(n-1) messages. For large n this grows as \(n^2\), so yes, it supports Metcalfe's law: the number of possible interactions, a proxy for value, grows with the square of the number of users.

U1 · Ch1 · P20~5 min

Consider the throughput example corresponding to Figure 1.20(b). Now suppose that there are M client-server pairs rather than 10. Denote \(R_s\), \(R_c\), and R for the rates of the server links, client links, and network link. Assume all other links have abundant capacity and that there is no other traffic in the network besides the traffic generated by the M client-server pairs. Derive a general expression for throughput in terms of \(R_s\), \(R_c\), R, and M.

Each of the M downloads crosses its own server link (\(R_s\)), the shared core link, which is split fairly so each gets \(R/M\), and its own client link (\(R_c\)). The throughput is set by the bottleneck:

\[ \text{throughput per pair} = \min\left(R_s,\ R_c,\ \frac{R}{M}\right) \]

\(\min(R_s, R_c, R/M)\) for each pair (total \(M\) times that).

U1 · Ch1 · P21~5 min

Consider Figure 1.19(b). Now suppose that there are M paths between the server and the client. No two paths share any link. Path k (k = 1, ..., M) consists of N links with transmission rates \(R_1^k, R_2^k, \ldots, R_N^k\). If the server can only use one path to send data to the client, what is the maximum throughput that the server can achieve? If the server can use all M paths to send data, what is the maximum throughput that the server can achieve?

The throughput of one path is its slowest link, \(\min_i R_i^k\).

  • One path: choose the best path: \(\displaystyle\max_{k}\ \min\left(R_1^k, R_2^k, \ldots, R_N^k\right)\).
  • All M paths (disjoint, so they add): \(\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{M}\min\left(R_1^k, R_2^k, \ldots, R_N^k\right)\).
U1 · Ch1 · P24~4 min

Suppose you would like to urgently deliver 50 terabytes data from Boston to Los Angeles. You have available a 100 Mbps dedicated link for data transfer. Would you prefer to transmit the data via this link or instead use FedEx overnight delivery? Explain.

\[ t = \frac{50\times10^{12}\times8\ \text{bits}}{100\times10^6\ \text{bps}} = 4\times10^6\ \text{s} \approx 46.3\ \text{days} \]

The link needs about 46 days, while FedEx delivers overnight (about one day), so use FedEx: shipping disks gives roughly 50 TB per day, far more throughput (though with a one-day latency).

U1 · Ch1 · P25~10 min

Suppose two hosts, A and B, are separated by 20,000 kilometers and are connected by a direct link of R = 5 Mbps. Suppose the propagation speed over the link is 2.5 × 108 meters/sec.

  1. Calculate the bandwidth-delay product, \(R\cdot d_{prop}\).
  2. Consider sending a file of 800,000 bits from Host A to Host B. Suppose the file is sent continuously as one large message. What is the maximum number of bits that will be in the link at any given time?
  3. Provide an interpretation of the bandwidth-delay product.
  4. What is the width (in meters) of a bit in the link? Is it longer than a football field?
  5. Derive a general expression for the width of a bit in terms of the propagation speed s, the transmission rate R, and the length of the link m.
  1. \[ d_{prop} = \frac{2\times10^7}{2.5\times10^8} = 0.08\ \text{s}, \qquad R\,d_{prop} = 5\times10^6\times0.08 = 4\times10^5 \]
    400,000 bits
  2. The file (800,000 bits) is longer than what the link holds, so the link fills up: 400,000 bits.
  3. It is the maximum number of bits that can be "in flight" in the link at once, i.e. the link's capacity as a pipe.
  4. \[ w = \frac{20\,000\,000\ \text{m}}{400\,000\ \text{bits}} = 50\ \text{m} \]
    50 m per bit; shorter than a football field (about 91-110 m).
  5. \[ w = \frac{m}{R\cdot m/s} = \frac{s}{R} \]
    \(w = s/R\), independent of m.
U1 · Ch1 · P29~7 min

Suppose there is a 10 Mbps microwave link between a geostationary satellite and its base station on Earth. Every minute the satellite takes a digital photo and sends it to the base station. Assume a propagation speed of 2.4 × 108 meters/sec.

  1. What is the propagation delay of the link?
  2. What is the bandwidth-delay product, \(R\cdot d_{prop}\)?
  3. Let x denote the size of the photo. What is the minimum value of x for the microwave link to be continuously transmitting?

A geostationary satellite orbits about 36,000 km above the Earth.

  1. \[ d_{prop} = \frac{3.6\times10^7}{2.4\times10^8} = 0.15\ \text{s} \]
    150 ms
  2. \[ R\,d_{prop} = 10^7\times0.15 = 1.5\times10^6 \]
    1.5 Mbits (1,500,000 bits)
  3. The link transmits continuously if each photo takes the whole minute to transmit: \(x/R \ge 60\) s.
    \[ x = 10^7\times60 = 6\times10^8\ \text{bits} \]
    x = 6 × 108 bits (600 Mbits, 75 MB)
U1 · Ch1 · P31~12 min

In modern packet-switched networks, including the Internet, the source host segments long, application-layer messages (for example, an image or a music file) into smaller packets and sends the packets into the network. The receiver then reassembles the packets back into the original message. We refer to this process as message segmentation. Figure 1.27 illustrates the end-to-end transport of a message with and without message segmentation. Consider a message that is 106 bits long that is to be sent from source to destination in Figure 1.27. Suppose each link in the figure is 5 Mbps. Ignore propagation, queuing, and processing delays.

  1. Consider sending the message from source to destination without message segmentation. How long does it take to move the message from the source host to the first packet switch? Keeping in mind that each switch uses store-and-forward packet switching, what is the total time to move the message from source host to destination host?
  2. Now suppose that the message is segmented into 100 packets, with each packet being 10,000 bits long. How long does it take to move the first packet from source host to the first switch? When the first packet is being sent from the first switch to the second switch, the second packet is being sent from the source host to the first switch. At what time will the second packet be fully received at the first switch?
  3. How long does it take to move the file from source host to destination host when message segmentation is used? Compare this result with your answer in part (a) and comment.
  4. In addition to reducing delay, what are reasons to use message segmentation?
  5. Discuss the drawbacks of message segmentation.
  1. \[ \frac{10^6}{5\times10^6} = 0.2\ \text{s per link}, \qquad 3\times0.2 = 0.6\ \text{s} \]
    0.2 s to the first switch; 0.6 s end to end.
  2. \[ \frac{10^4}{5\times10^6} = 2\ \text{ms} \]
    First packet at switch 1 after 2 ms; second packet fully received at switch 1 at 4 ms.
  3. The first packet arrives after 3 links = 6 ms; then one packet arrives every 2 ms:
    \[ (3 + 99)\times 2\ \text{ms} = 204\ \text{ms} \]
    0.204 s, about one-third of the 0.6 s without segmentation, because the links work in parallel (pipelining) instead of each waiting for the whole message.
  4. Bit errors cost only one small packet to retransmit rather than the whole message; switches need much smaller buffers; a large message does not block other users' small packets on a link.
  5. Packets must be numbered and reassembled (possibly reordered) at the destination, and every packet carries its own header, so header overhead grows as packets get smaller.
U1 · Ch1 · P33~10 min

Consider sending a large file of F bits from Host A to Host B. There are three links (and two switches) between A and B, and the links are uncongested (that is, no queuing delays). Host A segments the file into segments of S bits each and adds 80 bits of header to each segment, forming packets of L = 80 + S bits. Each link has a transmission rate of R bps. Find the value of S that minimizes the delay of moving the file from Host A to Host B. Disregard propagation delay.

There are \(F/S\) packets. The first packet needs 3 transmission times; each later packet adds one more (pipelining), so the delay is \((F/S + 2)\) packet times:

\[ D(S) = \left(\frac{F}{S}+2\right)\frac{S+80}{R} = \frac{1}{R}\left(F + \frac{80F}{S} + 2S + 160\right) \]
\[ \frac{dD}{dS} = \frac{1}{R}\left(-\frac{80F}{S^2} + 2\right) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; S^2 = 40F \]

The second derivative \(160F/(RS^3) > 0\), so this is a minimum: \(S = \sqrt{40F}\) bits.

U1 · Ch1 · P34~6 min

Skype offers a service that allows you to make a phone call from a PC to an ordinary phone. This means that the voice call must pass through both the Internet and through a telephone network. Discuss how this might be done.

The PC runs a VoIP client that digitizes and compresses the voice and sends it in packets (e.g. over UDP/RTP) across the Internet to a gateway operated by the service, located near the destination's telephone network. The gateway is connected to both networks: it receives the packets, reassembles and decodes the voice, and sends it as a normal digital or analog voice stream into the circuit-switched telephone network, where a circuit is set up to the ordinary phone. Signalling is translated too: the client's call request (dialled number) is turned into telephone call setup, and ringing/answer/hang-up are signalled back. The reverse direction works the same way, so the call is packet-switched over the Internet up to the gateway and circuit-switched from the gateway to the phone.

Unit 2

121 questions

Section 1: Principles of Network Applications

19 leading questions · Textbook Section 2.1, Principles of Network Applications,
U2 · S1 · Q1LQ
Define the term network applications. What are the most used network applications?

A network application is a program that runs on end systems (hosts) at the edge of the network and communicates with other programs over the network by exchanging messages. The network core (routers, switches) does not run application code.

The most used network applications include the World Wide Web, electronic mail, video streaming (Netflix, YouTube), social media, messaging and voice/video calls (Skype, Zoom), peer-to-peer file sharing, online games, and the Domain Name System (DNS) that supports all of them.

U2 · S1 · Q2LQ
What are computer network protocols?

A network protocol defines the format and the order of messages exchanged between two or more communicating entities, and the actions taken when a message is sent, received, or some other event (such as a timeout) occurs.

Examples are the HyperText Transfer Protocol (HTTP), the Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) and the Internet Protocol (IP). Everything on the Internet that involves communication is governed by protocols.

U2 · S1 · Q3LQ
What are the relationships between network applications and application layer protocols?

An application-layer protocol is only one piece of a network application. The application also includes the user interface, the data formats and the program logic.

For example, the Web application consists of browsers, Web servers, the HyperText Markup Language (HTML) document format and the HyperText Transfer Protocol (HTTP). HTTP is the part that defines how browser and server exchange messages. Likewise, e-mail uses the Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP) as its transfer protocol.

U2 · S1 · Q4LQ
What does an application-layer protocol define?

An application-layer protocol defines:

  • the types of messages exchanged (for example request and response messages);
  • the syntax of each message type, meaning its fields and how they are delimited;
  • the semantics of the fields, meaning what the information in them means;
  • the rules for when and how a process sends messages and responds to them.
U2 · S1 · Q5LQ
Where can the specifications of network application layer protocols be found?

Public application-layer protocols are specified in Requests for Comments (RFCs), which are freely available documents. For example, the HyperText Transfer Protocol (HTTP/1.1) is specified in RFC 2616 (now RFC 9110-9112) and the Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP) in RFC 5321.

Some application protocols are proprietary (for example Skype's), and their specifications are not public.

U2 · S1 · Q6LQ
Which organization oversees these specifications?

RFCs are produced by the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF), an open standards body whose working groups develop the Internet protocol standards. The IETF works under the umbrella of the Internet Society (ISOC), with the Internet Architecture Board (IAB) providing oversight.

U2 · S1 · Q7LQ
What is a network application server, and what is a network application client?

In a communication session, the client is the process that initiates contact, and the server is the process that waits to be contacted.

In the client-server architecture, the server is an always-on host with a permanent, well-known IP address that services requests from many clients (for example a Web server). A client, such as a Web browser, may be intermittently connected, may have a dynamic IP address, and clients do not communicate directly with each other.

U2 · S1 · Q8LQ
How are computers on the Internet addressed?

A host on the Internet is identified by its Internet Protocol (IP) address, a 32-bit number in IPv4 (written in dotted-decimal, such as 128.119.245.12) or a 128-bit number in IPv6.

To reach a particular process on that host, the sender also needs a port number. So a process is addressed by the pair (IP address, port number), for example port 80 for a Web server.

U2 · S1 · Q9LQ
How are IP addresses resolved from the literal names of network nodes?

Host names such as www.example.com are translated into IP addresses by the Domain Name System (DNS). The application hands the name to the local DNS client, which sends a query (normally over the User Datagram Protocol (UDP), port 53) to its local DNS server.

The local DNS server answers from its cache or resolves the name through the hierarchy of root, top-level domain (TLD) and authoritative DNS servers, then returns the IP address to the host.

U2 · S1 · Q10LQ
What is a port? What port numbers are used for what popular application-layer protocols?

A port is a 16-bit number that identifies a particular process (socket) on a host, so the transport layer can deliver data to the right application. Well-known ports (0-1023) are reserved for standard services.

ProtocolPort
HTTP (Web)80
HTTPS443
SMTP (mail transfer)25
POP3110
IMAP143
DNS53
FTP (control / data)21 / 20
SSH / Telnet22 / 23

HTTP = HyperText Transfer Protocol (HTTPS = HTTP Secure); SMTP = Simple Mail Transfer Protocol; POP3 = Post Office Protocol version 3; IMAP = Internet Mail Access Protocol; DNS = Domain Name System; FTP = File Transfer Protocol; SSH = Secure Shell.

U2 · S1 · Q11LQ
What is the Web’s application layer protocol?

The Web's application-layer protocol is the HyperText Transfer Protocol (HTTP). It defines how Web clients (browsers) request Web pages and objects from Web servers, and how servers send them back. HTTP runs over the Transmission Control Protocol (TCP), normally on port 80 (port 443 for the secured version, HTTPS).

U2 · S1 · Q12LQ
What application-layer protocols are used for electronic mail?

The Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP) transfers mail from the sender's user agent to the sender's mail server and between mail servers.

Mail access protocols retrieve mail from the recipient's mail server: the Post Office Protocol version 3 (POP3), the Internet Mail Access Protocol (IMAP), and HTTP for Web-based mail such as Gmail.

U2 · S1 · Q13LQ
What is a user agent?

A user agent is the application program that sits between the user and the network, acting on the user's behalf. For the Web the user agent is the browser (Chrome, Firefox); for e-mail it is the mail reader (Outlook, Apple Mail) that lets the user read, compose, reply to and forward messages.

U2 · S1 · Q14LQ
What is a loss-tolerant application? Provide examples.

A loss-tolerant application can tolerate some amount of data loss, which shows up only as small glitches in quality, rather than requiring 100% reliable delivery.

Examples are multimedia applications such as conversational audio/video (Internet phone, video conferencing) and streaming audio/video. In contrast, file transfer, e-mail and Web documents need fully reliable data transfer.

U2 · S1 · Q15LQ
What is bandwidth? What is a bandwidth-sensitive application?

Bandwidth is the rate at which data can be transferred, measured in bits per second (bps); in this context it is the throughput available to an application.

A bandwidth-sensitive application needs a guaranteed minimum throughput to work well. For example, an Internet telephony application that encodes voice at 32 kbps needs about 32 kbps; with less, it must drop quality or becomes unusable. Many multimedia applications are bandwidth-sensitive.

U2 · S1 · Q16LQ
What are elastic applications?

Elastic applications can make use of as much or as little throughput as happens to be available; more bandwidth makes them faster but they still work with less.

Examples are electronic mail, file transfer and Web transfers.

U2 · S1 · Q17LQ
What are real-time network applications? Provide examples.

Real-time (interactive) applications have tight timing constraints: data must arrive within a bounded delay to be useful.

Examples are Internet telephony (VoIP), video conferencing, and multiplayer online games, where end-to-end delays above a few hundred milliseconds make the application unpleasant or unusable.

U2 · S1 · Q18LQ
What services should be provided to application layer protocols by transport layer protocols?

An application could want four kinds of service from a transport protocol:

  • Reliable data transfer: data arrives complete, uncorrupted and in order.
  • Throughput: a guaranteed available rate.
  • Timing: a guaranteed bound on delay.
  • Security: confidentiality, data integrity and end-point authentication.

The Internet's Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) provides reliable transfer (and, with TLS on top, security), but neither TCP nor the User Datagram Protocol (UDP) guarantees throughput or timing.

U2 · S1 · Q19LQ
What applications does TCP support? What applications does UDP support?

The Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) supports applications that need reliable delivery: e-mail (SMTP), remote terminal access (Telnet, SSH), the Web (HTTP), file transfer (FTP) and, today, most streaming multimedia (HTTP/DASH such as Netflix, YouTube).

The User Datagram Protocol (UDP) supports applications that prefer low overhead and delay over reliability: DNS queries, Internet telephony and video conferencing (often over RTP), online games, and network management (SNMP).

Section 2: Network Application - HTTP and the World Wide Web

25 leading questions · Textbook Section 2.2, The Web and HTTP,
U2 · S2 · Q1LQ
What is HTTP? What does it define? What transport protocol does TCP use, and why?

The HyperText Transfer Protocol (HTTP) is the Web's application-layer protocol. It defines the structure of request and response messages and how Web clients request Web pages from servers and how servers transfer them.

HTTP uses the Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) as its transport (the question's "TCP" should read HTTP). TCP provides reliable, in-order byte-stream delivery, so HTTP does not have to deal with lost, reordered or corrupted data; a Web page must arrive complete.

U2 · S2 · Q2LQ
What is the relationship between HTTP and the World Wide Web?

The World Wide Web is an application made of Web pages (HTML files and objects), Web browsers and Web servers. The HyperText Transfer Protocol (HTTP) is the Web's application-layer protocol: the set of rules by which browsers and servers exchange the Web's objects. HTTP is part of the Web, not the whole Web.

U2 · S2 · Q3LQ
What is a Web page? Describe HTML and the function of an HTML file. How is an HTML file made?

A Web page consists of objects, each a file addressable by a single Uniform Resource Locator (URL). Most pages consist of a base HyperText Markup Language (HTML) file plus referenced objects such as JPEG images, scripts, stylesheets and video clips.

HTML is a markup language: tags describe the structure and look of the content and contain the URLs of the referenced objects. The browser reads the base HTML file, then fetches and displays the referenced objects. An HTML file is plain text, written by hand in any text editor, with an HTML editor, or generated by a program on the server.

U2 · S2 · Q4LQ
What are the differences between HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2?
HTTP/1.1HTTP/2
Message formatText (ASCII) messagesBinary framing layer
Objects per connectionSent one after another; a large object blocks those behind it (head-of-line blocking), so browsers open several parallel TCP connectionsObjects split into frames that are interleaved over a single TCP connection
PrioritizationNoneClient can assign weights and dependencies to responses
Server pushNoServer can send objects before they are requested
HeadersSent uncompressed each timeCompressed

The request methods, status codes and header meanings stay the same.

U2 · S2 · Q5LQ
What is a URL? Provide examples.

A Uniform Resource Locator (URL) is the address of a Web object. It has a host name (the server) and a path name (the object on that server), optionally preceded by the scheme and with a port.

Example: in http://www.someSchool.edu/someDepartment/picture.gif the host name is www.someSchool.edu and the path name is /someDepartment/picture.gif. Another example: https://www.athabascau.ca/index.html.

U2 · S2 · Q6LQ
What is a Web browser? What are the most popular browsers used today?

A Web browser is the user agent for the Web: the client side of HTTP. It sends HTTP requests, receives responses, and displays the requested Web page and its objects; it also handles navigation and configuration.

Widely used browsers include Google Chrome (the most widely used), Apple Safari, Microsoft Edge and Mozilla Firefox.

U2 · S2 · Q7LQ
What is a Web server? What Web server software systems are the most popular?

A Web server is the server side of HTTP: an always-on host that stores Web objects, each addressable by a URL, and returns them in response to HTTP requests (listening on port 80 or 443).

Widely used Web server software includes Nginx, the Apache HTTP Server and Microsoft Internet Information Services (IIS).

U2 · S2 · Q8LQ
How does TCP avoid network congestion?

The Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) limits the rate at which a sender transmits using a congestion window (cwnd); the sender may have at most about min(cwnd, receive window) unacknowledged bytes in flight.

The sender infers congestion from loss events (a timeout or three duplicate ACKs). It uses additive increase, multiplicative decrease (AIMD): it grows cwnd by about one segment per round-trip time while there is no loss, and halves it on loss (or drops it to one segment after a timeout and restarts slow start). This keeps senders from overloading the network.

U2 · S2 · Q9LQ
What is slow start?

Slow start is the initial phase of TCP congestion control. The congestion window starts at one maximum segment size (MSS) and increases by one MSS for every acknowledged segment, so it doubles every round-trip time: the sending rate grows exponentially.

Slow start ends when a loss occurs or when cwnd reaches the slow-start threshold (ssthresh), after which TCP enters congestion avoidance (linear growth). Despite its name, it ramps up quickly; it is "slow" only because it starts from a small window.

U2 · S2 · Q10LQ
Why is HTTP called a stateless protocol?

HTTP is stateless because an HTTP server keeps no information about past client requests. If the same client asks for the same object twice in a few seconds, the server simply sends it again; each request is handled independently.

This keeps servers simple and able to handle many clients. When state is needed (logins, shopping carts), it is added on top with cookies.

U2 · S2 · Q11LQ
What are non-persistent connections? What are persistent connections? What is the default connection for HTTP/1.1?

With a non-persistent connection, each request/response pair is sent over a separate TCP connection, which is closed after the server sends the one object. A page with 10 images needs 11 TCP connections.

With a persistent connection, the server leaves the TCP connection open after responding, so subsequent requests and responses between the same client and server (an entire page, or several pages) use the same connection. The server closes it after a period of inactivity.

The default for HTTP/1.1 is persistent connections (with pipelining allowed).

U2 · S2 · Q12LQ
How is round-trip time (RTT) defined?

The round-trip time (RTT) is the time it takes for a small packet to travel from the client to the server and back to the client. It includes the propagation, queuing and processing delays along the path in both directions; transmission time of the small packet is negligible.

A non-persistent HTTP fetch takes about 2 RTT plus the transmission time of the file: one RTT to set up TCP, one RTT for the request and the first bytes of the response.

U2 · S2 · Q13LQ
What are the shortcomings of non-persistent connections?
  • A brand-new TCP connection must be established and maintained for each requested object, so each object costs 2 RTTs (handshake plus request/response).
  • For each connection TCP buffers and variables are allocated in both client and server, which is a significant burden on a Web server serving many clients.
  • Each new connection starts in TCP slow start, so it rarely reaches full speed.
  • Browsers open several parallel connections to compensate, adding more overhead.
U2 · S2 · Q14LQ
What are the disadvantages of a persistent connection without pipelining?

Without pipelining, the client issues a new request only after the previous response has been received. So each referenced object costs one full RTT of waiting, and the connection sits idle while a request travels to the server and the response starts to come back.

The server also keeps the connection open (consuming resources) while it waits for the next request, until a timeout.

U2 · S2 · Q15LQ
In what format are HTTP request messages sent from a user agent such as a Web browser?

HTTP/1.1 request messages are written in ordinary ASCII text, so humans can read them. A request consists of a request line, then zero or more header lines, each ending in a carriage return and line feed, then a blank line (CRLF), then an optional entity body (used with POST).

GET /somedir/page.html HTTP/1.1
Host: www.someschool.edu
Connection: close
User-agent: Mozilla/5.0
Accept-language: fr
U2 · S2 · Q16LQ
What are request lines?

The request line is the first line of an HTTP request message. It has three fields separated by spaces: the method field (GET, POST, HEAD, PUT, DELETE), the URL field (the path of the requested object), and the HTTP version field. Example: GET /somedir/page.html HTTP/1.1.

U2 · S2 · Q17LQ
What are header lines?

Header lines follow the request line (or the status line in a response) and carry extra information as "Name: value" pairs.

Request examples: Host: (the server's host name, needed by caches), Connection: close, User-agent: (browser type), Accept-language:, If-modified-since:, Cookie:. Response examples: Date:, Server:, Last-Modified:, Content-Length:, Content-Type:, Set-cookie:.

U2 · S2 · Q18LQ
What methods can be used by HTTP to send objects?
  • GET: requests an object; form input can be placed in the URL.
  • POST: sends user input (such as form data) to the server in the entity body.
  • HEAD: like GET, but the server returns only the headers, not the object (used for debugging).
  • PUT: uploads an object to a specific path on the server.
  • DELETE: deletes an object on the server.

Objects can be sent to the server with POST (in the body) and PUT; they are retrieved with GET.

U2 · S2 · Q19LQ
What are the differences between the Get and Post methods?

GET requests an object, and its entity body is empty; any user input is appended to the URL as a query string (for example www.somesite.com/animalsearch?monkeys&bananas), so it is visible, limited in length and can be cached or bookmarked.

POST carries user input (form data) in the entity body of the request. The data is not in the URL, can be large, and the response depends on what was posted (POST requests are not normally cached).

U2 · S2 · Q20LQ
In what format are HTTP response messages sent from an HTTP server?

An HTTP response has a status line (protocol version, status code and phrase), then header lines, a blank line, and the entity body containing the requested object.

HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Connection: close
Date: Tue, 18 Aug 2015 15:44:04 GMT
Server: Apache/2.2.3 (CentOS)
Last-Modified: Tue, 18 Aug 2015 15:11:03 GMT
Content-Length: 6821
Content-Type: text/html

(data data data ...)

Common status codes: 200 OK, 301 Moved Permanently, 304 Not Modified, 400 Bad Request, 404 Not Found, 505 HTTP Version Not Supported.

U2 · S2 · Q21LQ
What is authentication? How can a user be authenticated on the Web?

Authentication is verifying the identity of a user (or host) before granting access. On the Web, a user is typically authenticated with a user name and password: the site returns 401 Unauthorized and the browser resends the request with an Authorization: header, or the user fills in a login form that is sent with POST over HTTPS.

After login, the server usually keeps the user authenticated with a cookie (a session ID) that the browser returns on each later request, since HTTP itself is stateless.

U2 · S2 · Q22LQ
What are cookies and what are they used for?

Cookies let a stateless HTTP server keep track of users. There are four components: a Set-cookie: header line in the server's response carrying an identification number; a Cookie: header line in later requests; a cookie file kept by the browser; and a back-end database at the Web site keyed by the ID.

Uses: user sessions and authentication, shopping carts, recommendations and personalization, and remembering preferences. They also allow tracking of users' browsing across sites, which raises privacy concerns.

U2 · S2 · Q23LQ
What is Web caching? Why is Web caching used? Where can a Web cache reside?

A Web cache (proxy server) is a network entity that satisfies HTTP requests on behalf of an origin server. It keeps copies of recently requested objects; on a hit it returns the copy, on a miss it fetches the object from the origin server, stores it and forwards it.

It is used to reduce the response time for client requests (the cache is close and fast), to reduce traffic on an institution's access link (saving money on bandwidth), and to reduce load on origin servers and Internet-wide traffic.

A Web cache typically resides in the institution's or ISP's network (purchased and installed by a university, company or residential ISP), and content distribution networks place caches throughout the Internet. Browsers also have their own local cache.

U2 · S2 · Q24LQ
What is the conditional GET? How does it work? What are the advantages?

A conditional GET lets a cache verify that its copy of an object is still up to date. It is a GET request that includes an If-Modified-Since: header line carrying the Last-Modified date of the cached copy.

If the object has not changed since that date, the server replies 304 Not Modified with an empty body, and the cache serves its copy. If it has changed, the server returns 200 OK with the new object.

Advantages: the cache never serves stale objects, and when the object is unchanged no object data crosses the network, saving bandwidth and transmission time.

U2 · S2 · Q25LQ
Why might cooperative caching be more useful?

In cooperative caching, multiple caches (for example a hierarchy of institutional caches below a national or ISP cache) share their contents: a cache that misses asks its peers or parent before going to the origin server.

It is more useful because the combined client population is larger, so the hit rate is higher: an object requested by a user of one institution can be served from a nearby cache that another institution already filled. This further cuts response time, traffic on expensive long-distance and access links, and load on origin servers.

Section 3: Electronic Mail in the Internet

11 leading questions · Textbook Section 2.3, Electronic Mail in the Internet,
U2 · S3 · Q1LQ
What are the differences between POP and IMAP?

The Post Office Protocol version 3 (POP3) and the Internet Mail Access Protocol (IMAP) both let a user agent retrieve mail from the recipient's mail server, but they differ as follows.

POP3IMAP
Where mail livesDownloaded to the local machine (typically deleted from server: "download-and-delete")Kept on the server
FoldersOnly on the local machineRemote folders on the server, with commands to create folders and move messages
Several devicesPoor: mail ends up on one deviceGood: same view from every device
State across sessionsStateless across sessionsKeeps user state (folders, flags) across sessions
Partial retrievalNoCan fetch only parts of a message (e.g. headers only)
Complexity / portSimple, port 110More complex, port 143
U2 · S3 · Q2LQ
What are user agents and mail servers in electronic mail respectively?

A user agent (such as Outlook, Apple Mail or a Gmail Web page) lets the user read, reply to, forward, save and compose messages. It sends outgoing mail to the user's mail server and retrieves incoming mail from it.

A mail server is the core of the e-mail system. Each recipient has a mailbox on one; the server also keeps an outgoing message queue and uses the Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP) to send messages to, and receive them from, other mail servers.

U2 · S3 · Q3LQ
What does the Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP) do?

The Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP) is the principal application-layer protocol for Internet e-mail. It transfers messages from the sender's mail server to the recipient's mail server (and from a sender's user agent to its mail server), using reliable TCP on port 25. It is a push protocol and requires messages to be in 7-bit ASCII.

U2 · S3 · Q4LQ
What are the basic operations of SMTP?
  1. Alice's user agent sends the message to her mail server, where it is placed in a message queue.
  2. The client side of SMTP on Alice's server opens a TCP connection to port 25 of Bob's mail server.
  3. After the handshake (HELO, MAIL FROM, RCPT TO, DATA), the client sends the message over the connection; the message ends with a line containing only a period, and QUIT closes the connection.
  4. Bob's mail server places the message in Bob's mailbox.
  5. Bob invokes his user agent to read it (using POP3, IMAP or HTTP).

SMTP normally transfers mail directly between the two servers without intermediate mail servers, and uses a persistent connection for multiple messages.

U2 · S3 · Q5LQ
Why does SMTP need a message queue?

The sender's mail server cannot always deliver immediately: the recipient's mail server may be down or unreachable. The message waits in the outgoing message queue and the server retries periodically (for example every 30 minutes); if it still fails after several days, the sender is notified. The queue also lets the sender's user agent hand off mail at once, without waiting for remote delivery.

U2 · S3 · Q6LQ
How would you compare SMTP with HTTP?

Both transfer files between hosts, both use persistent TCP connections, and both have ASCII command/response interactions with status codes.

  • HTTP is mainly a pull protocol (the client pulls objects from the server); SMTP is a push protocol (the sending server pushes the file to the receiving server).
  • SMTP requires every message, including the body, to be 7-bit ASCII (binary data must be encoded); HTTP has no such restriction.
  • HTTP encapsulates each object in its own response message; SMTP places all of a message's objects in one message.
U2 · S3 · Q7LQ
What formats might a mail message have?

A mail message (RFC 5322) consists of header lines (such as From:, To:, Subject:), a blank line, and a body in 7-bit ASCII.

To carry non-ASCII content (other character sets, images, audio, attachments), the Multipurpose Internet Mail Extensions (MIME) add headers such as Content-Type: and Content-Transfer-Encoding: (for example base64), and allow multipart messages. So a message may be plain text, HTML, or multipart with attachments.

U2 · S3 · Q8LQ
What are mail access protocols? How do they differ from a mail transfer protocol, such as SMTP?

Mail access protocols transfer messages from the recipient's mail server to the recipient's user agent: the Post Office Protocol version 3 (POP3), the Internet Mail Access Protocol (IMAP), and HTTP for Web-based e-mail.

They differ from SMTP because SMTP is a push protocol, used between servers and from a sender's agent to its server. The recipient's user agent cannot use SMTP to obtain messages, since obtaining mail is a pull operation; also the user's PC is not always on, so mail must wait on the always-on server until the user pulls it.

U2 · S3 · Q9LQ
What does Post Office Protocol version 3 (POP3) do?

The Post Office Protocol version 3 (POP3) is a simple mail access protocol. The user agent opens a TCP connection to the mail server on port 110, then it proceeds through three phases:

  • Authorization: user name and password (user, pass).
  • Transaction: list, retrieve (retr) and mark messages for deletion (dele).
  • Update: after quit, the server deletes the marked messages.

It works in download-and-delete or download-and-keep mode and keeps no state between sessions.

U2 · S3 · Q10LQ
What does IMAP protocol do?

The Internet Mail Access Protocol (IMAP) is a mail access protocol that keeps messages on the server. It lets the user create remote folders, move messages between them, search them, and fetch only parts of a message (such as headers). Because the server maintains user state across sessions, the user sees the same mailbox from any device. It runs over TCP on port 143.

U2 · S3 · Q11LQ
What are the differences between POP3 and IMAP?

The key differences between the Post Office Protocol version 3 (POP3) and the Internet Mail Access Protocol (IMAP):

POP3IMAP
Where mail livesDownloaded to the local machine (typically deleted from server: "download-and-delete")Kept on the server
FoldersOnly on the local machineRemote folders on the server, with commands to create folders and move messages
Several devicesPoor: mail ends up on one deviceGood: same view from every device
State across sessionsStateless across sessionsKeeps user state (folders, flags) across sessions
Partial retrievalNoCan fetch only parts of a message (e.g. headers only)
Complexity / portSimple, port 110More complex, port 143

Section 4: Learning Objectives

17 leading questions · Textbook Section 2.4, DNS - The Internet’s Directory Service,
U2 · S4 · Q1LQ
What is DNS? What is DNS used for?

The Domain Name System (DNS) is a distributed database implemented in a hierarchy of DNS servers, plus an application-layer protocol that lets hosts query that database. It runs over the User Datagram Protocol (UDP) on port 53.

Its main use is to translate host names (www.example.com) into Internet Protocol (IP) addresses. It also provides host aliasing, mail server aliasing (MX records) and load distribution among replicated servers.

U2 · S4 · Q2LQ
How does the Domain Name System work?

When an application needs an Internet Protocol (IP) address, it calls the DNS client on the host, which sends a query to its local DNS server. If the local server has the answer cached, it replies. Otherwise it queries a root server, which refers it to a top-level domain (TLD) server for the suffix (.com), which refers it to the authoritative server for the domain, which returns the IP address.

The local server caches the mapping (for its time to live) and returns it to the host. Queries and replies are DNS messages carried in User Datagram Protocol (UDP) datagrams.

U2 · S4 · Q3LQ
Why is DNS so important for the Internet community?

Humans remember names, but routers forward packets by IP address. Almost every Internet application (Web, e-mail, streaming) begins with a DNS lookup, so without DNS users could not reach services by name.

DNS also lets operators move a service to a new IP address, alias names, distribute load across replicated servers and let content distribution networks (CDNs) direct users to nearby servers, all without users noticing. It is a core Internet function implemented at the network edge.

U2 · S4 · Q4LQ
In DNS, what is host aliasing? Why is it needed?

Host aliasing means a host with a complicated canonical host name (for example relay1.west-coast.enterprise.com) can have one or more simpler, mnemonic alias names (enterprise.com, www.enterprise.com). DNS returns the canonical name (via a CNAME record) and its IP address for an alias.

It is needed so users can use short, memorable names, and so the organization can change the underlying host without changing the name users know. Mail server aliasing (MX records) works the same way, letting a company use one name for its Web and mail servers.

U2 · S4 · Q5LQ
What is load distribution in DNS?

Busy sites such as cnn.com are replicated over many servers, each with its own IP address. The DNS database holds the whole set of IP addresses for the one canonical name.

When clients query, the DNS server replies with the entire set but rotates the order in each reply. Clients usually use the first address, so requests are spread across the replicated servers.

U2 · S4 · Q6LQ
What problems does a centralized DNS design have?
  • Single point of failure: if the one server crashes, the whole Internet loses name resolution.
  • Traffic volume: one server would have to handle all queries from hundreds of millions of hosts.
  • Distant centralized database: it would be far from most clients, causing long delays.
  • Maintenance: one database would be huge and need constant updates for every new host.

In short, a centralized design does not scale.

U2 · S4 · Q7LQ
Why is a distributed database important for DNS?

Distributing the database across a hierarchy of servers (root, top-level domain, authoritative) makes DNS scale: no server holds all mappings or handles all queries. Each organization maintains its own records on its authoritative servers, failure of one server affects only part of the namespace, servers are replicated and placed near users, and caching at local servers absorbs most queries.

U2 · S4 · Q8LQ
What are root DNS servers? What roles do they play?

Root DNS servers are at the top of the DNS hierarchy. There are 13 logical root servers (named A to M), each replicated, giving over 1,000 server instances worldwide, managed by 12 organizations and coordinated by the Internet Assigned Numbers Authority (IANA).

A root server does not know host addresses; it provides the IP addresses of the top-level domain (TLD) servers for a requested suffix such as .com or .ca.

U2 · S4 · Q9LQ
What is a top-level domain (TLD) server?

A top-level domain (TLD) server is responsible for one top-level domain: generic domains such as com, org, net, edu and gov, or country domains such as ca, uk, fr and jp. For example, Verisign maintains the .com servers. A TLD server provides the IP addresses of the authoritative DNS servers for the domains under it.

U2 · S4 · Q10LQ
What are authoritative DNS servers?

An authoritative DNS server holds the official DNS records for an organization's publicly accessible hosts (such as its Web and mail servers), mapping their names to IP addresses. The organization runs its own (primary and secondary) authoritative servers or pays a service provider to host them. Its answers are authoritative, as opposed to answers from a cache.

U2 · S4 · Q11LQ
What are so-called local DNS servers?

A local DNS server (default name server) belongs to each ISP, university or company, and is not strictly part of the hierarchy. When a host connects, it is told the IP address of its local DNS server (usually by DHCP).

A host sends its DNS queries to the local DNS server, which acts as a proxy: it answers from its cache or forwards the query into the DNS hierarchy and returns the result. It is typically close to the host (a few routers away).

U2 · S4 · Q12LQ
What are recursive queries and iterative queries in DNS?

In a recursive query, the queried server takes on the job of obtaining the mapping on the requester's behalf and returns the final answer. The query from a host to its local DNS server is typically recursive.

In an iterative query, the queried server replies directly with a referral ("I don't know, ask this server") and the requester queries the next server itself. The queries from the local DNS server to the root, TLD and authoritative servers are typically iterative.

In the usual case resolving a name costs 8 DNS messages: 1 recursive query and reply plus 3 iterative query/reply pairs.

U2 · S4 · Q13LQ
Why is DNS caching needed?

When a DNS server receives a reply, it caches the mapping in local memory. Later queries for the same name are answered from the cache without contacting other servers; local DNS servers also cache TLD server addresses, so root servers are often bypassed.

Caching is needed to cut delay for users and to reduce the number of DNS messages across the Internet and the load on root and TLD servers. Cached entries expire after their time to live (TTL), often two days, so mappings that change eventually update.

U2 · S4 · Q14LQ
What are resource records (RRs) in DNS? What is the format of resource record?

DNS servers store resource records (RRs), which provide the hostname-to-IP mappings and related data. Each record is a four-tuple (Name, Value, Type, TTL), where TTL is how long the record may be cached.

TypeNameValue
Ahost nameIPv4 address (AAAA for IPv6)
NSdomain (e.g. foo.com)host name of an authoritative server for the domain
CNAMEalias host namecanonical host name
MXalias host namecanonical name of the mail server
U2 · S4 · Q15LQ
What are DNS messages? What is the format?

DNS has two kinds of messages, query and reply, and both use the same format:

  • Header (12 bytes): a 16-bit identification number that matches replies to queries, flags (query/reply, authoritative, recursion desired, recursion available), and four counts for the sections below.
  • Questions: the name being queried and the query type (A, MX, ...).
  • Answers: the resource records answering the query.
  • Authority: records of other authoritative servers.
  • Additional information: other helpful records (for example the A record of a mail server named in an MX answer).
U2 · S4 · Q16LQ
What can the nslookup command do for you?

The nslookup command-line tool sends a DNS query directly to a DNS server (the local default one or one you name) and displays the reply. It lets you find the IP address of a host name, look up other record types (MX, NS, CNAME, using -type=), do reverse lookups, see whether the answer is authoritative, and troubleshoot DNS problems.

U2 · S4 · Q17LQ
What roles does ICANN do?

The Internet Corporation for Assigned Names and Numbers (ICANN) is the nonprofit organization that coordinates the Internet's unique identifiers. It manages the DNS root zone (through its Internet Assigned Numbers Authority function), oversees the top-level domains and accredits domain-name registrars, and allocates IP address blocks to the regional Internet registries.

Section 5: Peer-to-Peer File Distribution

11 leading questions · Textbook Section 2.5, Peer-to-Peer File Distribution,
U2 · S5 · Q1LQ
What is a peer-to-peer network application?

A peer-to-peer (P2P) application is one in which intermittently connected end hosts, called peers, communicate directly with each other, with minimal or no reliance on always-on dedicated servers. Each peer is both a client and a server: it downloads from and uploads to other peers. Examples are BitTorrent file sharing and some streaming systems.

U2 · S5 · Q2LQ
What are the differences between client-server applications and peer-to-peer applications?
Client-serverPeer-to-peer
Who servesAn always-on server with a fixed, well-known IP address (often a data center)Peers serve each other; no always-on server needed
Client rolesClients talk only to the server, not to each otherEach peer is both client and server
ScalabilityServer capacity must grow with demandSelf-scalable: each new peer adds service capacity
CostExpensive server infrastructure and bandwidthCheap for the provider
ManagementEasy to manage and secureHard: peers are unreliable, churn, change IP addresses
U2 · S5 · Q3LQ
How is P2P used for file distribution?

A server holds a large file that many peers want. In P2P distribution the server sends pieces of the file into the network, and each peer that has received a piece redistributes it to other peers while it continues downloading. So the peers' upload capacity helps the server, and the file spreads quickly. BitTorrent is the most popular P2P file distribution protocol.

U2 · S5 · Q4LQ
Why is it said that P2P architecture is self-scalable?

In P2P, each peer that joins to download the file also brings its own upload capacity and serves other peers. Service demand and service capacity grow together, so the system scales by itself.

The distribution time shows this: \(D_{P2P} \ge NF/(u_s + \sum u_i)\). As N grows the denominator grows too, so the time levels off near F divided by the average peer upload rate, while the client-server time \(NF/u_s\) grows linearly with N.

U2 · S5 · Q5LQ
What is the file distribution problem?

The file distribution problem asks how long it takes to send a copy of one file of F bits from one server to N peers. The distribution time is the time until all N peers have a complete copy. It depends on the server upload rate \(u_s\), the peers' upload rates \(u_i\) and download rates \(d_i\), and it is used to compare client-server and P2P architectures (assuming the Internet core has plenty of bandwidth).

U2 · S5 · Q6LQ
How is distribution time calculated for a P2P file distribution system?

Three limits apply: the server must upload at least one copy (time \(F/u_s\)); the slowest peer must download F bits (time \(F/d_{min}\)); and the system must upload N F bits in total at a combined rate of at most \(u_s + \sum u_i\). The minimum distribution time is the largest of these:

\[ D_{P2P} = \max\left\{ \frac{F}{u_s},\ \frac{F}{d_{min}},\ \frac{NF}{u_s + \sum_{i=1}^{N} u_i} \right\} \]

Compare client-server: \(D_{cs} = \max\{NF/u_s,\ F/d_{min}\}\).

U2 · S5 · Q7LQ
What does the BitTorrent system do?

BitTorrent is a P2P file distribution protocol. The peers sharing a file form a torrent; a tracker records the participating peers. The file is split into 256 KB chunks.

  • A joining peer gets a list of peers from the tracker and opens TCP connections to some of them (its neighbours).
  • It requests missing chunks rarest first, so rare chunks are redistributed quickly.
  • It uploads to the 4 neighbours currently sending to it at the highest rate (tit-for-tat), re-evaluated every 10 seconds, plus one randomly chosen optimistically unchoked peer every 30 seconds so new peers get started and better partners are discovered.

Peers may leave after finishing (selfishly) or stay as seeders.

U2 · S5 · Q8LQ
Where are distributed hash tables (DHTs) used?

A distributed hash table (DHT) is a database of (key, value) pairs spread over millions of peers, with no central server. DHTs are used in P2P systems: the textbook notes they are widely implemented, for example in BitTorrent, where a DHT acts as a distributed tracker that finds the peers in a torrent. They are also used in other P2P file sharing and distributed storage systems.

U2 · S5 · Q9LQ
How does circular DHT work?

Each peer and each key is assigned an n-bit identifier (keys by hashing). A (key, value) pair is stored at the peer whose identifier is the immediate successor of the key (the closest ID equal to or greater than it, wrapping around).

Peers are arranged in a circle, and each peer knows only its successor (and predecessor). A query is passed around the circle until it reaches the responsible peer: on average N/2 messages for N peers. Adding shortcut links reduces this to about \(O(\log N)\) messages with \(O(\log N)\) neighbours per peer. Each peer also tracks its second successor so the circle survives peer churn.

U2 · S5 · Q10LQ
What is an overlay network? How does it work?

An overlay network is a logical network built on top of the physical Internet. Its nodes are the peers, and an edge exists between two peers if they maintain a virtual link (for example a TCP connection or simply knowledge of each other's IP address).

Each overlay edge may pass through many physical routers and links, which the overlay ignores. Peers send messages only to their overlay neighbours, who forward them as needed (as in a circular DHT, or among BitTorrent neighbours).

U2 · S5 · Q11LQ
What does peer churn mean in P2P systems?

Peer churn means peers join and leave a P2P system at any time without warning. The system must keep working: in a circular DHT each peer tracks its first and second successors and pings them periodically; when a successor leaves, the peer makes the second successor its new successor and asks it for its successor. In BitTorrent, peers periodically refresh their neighbour lists from the tracker.

Section 6: Video Streaming and Content Distribution Networks

25 leading questions · Textbook Section 2.6, Video Streaming and Content Distribution Networks,
Streaming Stored Video
U2 · S6 · Q1LQ
What are UDP streaming, HTTP streaming, and Adaptive HTTP streaming?
  • UDP streaming: the server sends video at a rate matching the client's consumption rate, in RTP packets carried over UDP, with no congestion control; a separate control connection (RTSP) handles pause and seek.
  • HTTP streaming: the video is an ordinary file on an HTTP server; the client issues an HTTP GET and the server sends it over TCP as fast as TCP allows, with the client buffering it.
  • Adaptive HTTP streaming (DASH): the video is encoded at several bit rates and split into chunks; the client chooses for each chunk the version that suits its current bandwidth.
U2 · S6 · Q2LQ
Can streaming be done over TCP?

Yes. HTTP streaming and DASH, used by Netflix, YouTube and most services today, run over TCP. TCP's varying rate and retransmissions are absorbed by a client buffer and by prefetching, so playback is smooth as long as the average throughput exceeds the video rate. TCP also passes through firewalls and NATs easily and needs no special media servers.

U2 · S6 · Q3LQ
What is client buffering? Why is it needed?

Client buffering means the client stores arriving video in an application buffer and starts playback only after a short initial delay, then plays from the buffer at the constant playout rate.

It is needed because network delay and throughput vary (jitter): the buffer absorbs these variations so playback continues smoothly while the network temporarily slows down, as long as the average throughput is high enough.

U2 · S6 · Q4LQ
How does UDP streaming work? What properties does UDP streaming have?

The server transmits video at a constant rate that matches the client's consumption rate, wrapping chunks in Real-time Transport Protocol (RTP) packets sent over UDP. The client keeps a small buffer (a few seconds) to absorb jitter.

Properties: no congestion control or retransmission, so the rate is steady but losses are not repaired; low start-up delay; it needs a media control server (RTSP) that keeps per-client state, which raises cost; and UDP traffic is often blocked by firewalls. It cannot adapt to bandwidth changes, so it stalls if available bandwidth drops below the video rate.

U2 · S6 · Q5LQ
Why does UDP streaming need a parallel control connection?

UDP streaming sends the media itself as a stream of UDP packets that carry no commands. Interactive actions such as play, pause, reposition (seek) and stop must be signalled from client to server separately, so a parallel control connection is used, typically with the Real-Time Streaming Protocol (RTSP). The server must keep this client state.

U2 · S6 · Q6LQ
What protocols are used for UDP streaming?
  • Real-time Transport Protocol (RTP): wraps media chunks with payload type, sequence number and timestamp, over UDP.
  • RTP Control Protocol (RTCP): carries reception statistics (loss, jitter) alongside RTP.
  • Real-Time Streaming Protocol (RTSP): the control connection for play, pause and reposition.
  • User Datagram Protocol (UDP) as the transport.
U2 · S6 · Q7LQ
What is HTTP streaming? How does it work?

In HTTP streaming the video is stored on an HTTP server as an ordinary file with a URL. The client opens a TCP connection and sends an HTTP GET; the server sends the file as fast as TCP congestion and flow control allow. Bytes collect in the client application buffer, and once it holds a threshold amount, playback starts; the client periodically grabs frames, decompresses and displays them while more data keeps arriving.

U2 · S6 · Q8LQ
What are multimedia network applications using HTTP streaming?

Most major streaming services use HTTP streaming (in its adaptive form, DASH or HLS): YouTube, Netflix, Amazon Prime Video, Disney+, Hulu, Twitch, and on-demand news or sports video sites. It is popular because it traverses firewalls and NATs, needs no media control server, and works with ordinary Web caches and CDNs.

U2 · S6 · Q9LQ
What is prefetching video? Why is it needed?

Prefetching means downloading video at a rate higher than the consumption (playout) rate, storing frames in the client buffer before they are needed.

It is needed so that the buffer builds a reserve: when TCP throughput temporarily drops (congestion, retransmission), playback can continue from the prefetched frames without freezing. It happens naturally in HTTP streaming, since TCP sends as fast as possible.

U2 · S6 · Q10LQ
HTTP streaming runs over TCP and utilizes both the client application buffer and TCP buffers. How do the two collaborate?

The server writes video into its TCP send buffer; TCP delivers it into the client's TCP receive buffer; the client application reads bytes from there into its application buffer, from which the player takes frames at the playout rate.

If the client application buffer fills (the user paused, or the download rate exceeds the playout rate), the client stops reading the TCP receive buffer. That buffer fills, TCP flow control throttles the server, the server's TCP send buffer fills and the server's writes block. So the buffers form a chain that automatically limits the server to the rate at which the client consumes video.

U2 · S6 · Q11LQ
How is early termination handled in HTTP streaming?

If the user stops watching early, any video already prefetched but not yet played was downloaded for nothing, wasting bandwidth and server resources. Systems limit this by prefetching only a moderate amount (a limited client buffer size, so TCP flow control stops the download) and by closing the TCP connection when the user leaves.

U2 · S6 · Q12LQ
How is repositioning video handled in HTTP streaming?

When the user jumps to a future point in the video, the client sends a new HTTP request with the HTTP byte-range header (Range:) specifying the byte offset where it wants the server to start sending. The server then sends from that point. Any prefetched data beyond the old position that is discarded is wasted.

U2 · S6 · Q13LQ
What is adaptive HTTP streaming? What is dynamic adaptive streaming over HTTP (DASH)

Plain HTTP streaming sends the same encoding to every client regardless of its bandwidth. Adaptive HTTP streaming fixes this by encoding the video into several versions at different bit rates and quality levels.

Dynamic Adaptive Streaming over HTTP (DASH) is the standard for this: each version is split into chunks of a few seconds, each with its own URL, and the client dynamically chooses, chunk by chunk, the version whose bit rate fits its currently available bandwidth.

U2 · S6 · Q14LQ
How does DASH work? What advantages does DASH have?

Each version of the video is stored on the HTTP server under a different URL, and the server keeps a manifest file listing the URL and bit rate of each version. The client first fetches the manifest, then requests chunks one at a time with HTTP GETs. While downloading, it measures the received bandwidth and runs a rate-determination algorithm to pick the highest-quality version it can sustain for the next chunk, given the bandwidth and its buffer level.

Advantages: smooth playback as conditions change, the best quality each client can afford, clients with different access rates are served from the same content, it uses ordinary HTTP servers, caches and CDNs, and it traverses firewalls.

U2 · S6 · Q15LQ
What are content distribution networks (CDNs)?

A content distribution network (CDN) manages servers in many geographically distributed locations, stores copies of videos and other Web content on them, and redirects each user request to a server location that will provide the best user experience.

A CDN may be private (owned by the content provider, such as Google's for YouTube or Netflix Open Connect) or third-party (such as Akamai or Limelight, serving many content providers). CDNs avoid the single point of failure, the long and congested paths, and the repeated sending of popular content that a single huge data center would suffer.

U2 · S6 · Q16LQ
A CDN often consists of many servers. How would these servers be placed?

CDN servers are placed in clusters, following one of two philosophies:

  • Enter deep: many small clusters deployed inside access ISPs all over the world, close to users (Akamai).
  • Bring home: fewer, larger clusters placed in Internet exchange points (IXPs) (Limelight and many others).
U2 · S6 · Q17LQ
What is the so-called enter deep philosophy for server placement within content distribution networks? How are servers placed according to enter deep?

The enter deep philosophy, pioneered by Akamai, places server clusters deep into the access networks of ISPs, deploying clusters in access ISPs all over the world (thousands of locations).

The goal is to get close to end users, reducing the delay and number of links and routers between user and server, and so improving delay and throughput. The drawback is that maintaining and managing so many highly distributed clusters is difficult.

U2 · S6 · Q18LQ
What is the so-called bring home philosophy for server placement within content distribution networks? How are servers placed according to bring home?

The bring home philosophy brings the ISPs "home" by building large clusters at a smaller number of key sites (tens of locations), typically in Internet exchange points (IXPs), rather than inside access ISPs.

Compared with enter deep, it gives lower maintenance and management overhead, possibly at the expense of higher delay and lower throughput to end users.

U2 · S6 · Q19LQ
How do CDNs work?

The CDN replicates content across its clusters, usually by a pull strategy: when a cluster gets a request for content it does not have, it retrieves it from a central repository or another cluster and stores a copy (evicting less popular content when storage fills), much like a Web cache. When a user requests content, the CDN intercepts the request (usually through DNS), determines a suitable cluster for that client, and redirects the request to a server in that cluster.

U2 · S6 · Q20LQ
How do CDNs take advantage of DNS to intercept and redirect requests?

The content provider's Web page gives the video a URL whose host name belongs to the provider (for example video.netcinema.com). When the user's local DNS server resolves it, the provider's authoritative DNS server returns not an IP address but a host name in the CDN's domain (via a CNAME). The local DNS server then queries the CDN's authoritative DNS server, which chooses a suitable CDN server for this client and returns that server's IP address. The client's request thus goes to the CDN server instead of the provider's server.

U2 · S6 · Q21LQ
What six steps do CDNs use to take advantage of DNS to intercept and redirect requests?

Example: NetCinema uses the third-party CDN KingCDN.

  1. The user visits the Web page at NetCinema and clicks a video link, http://video.netcinema.com/6Y7B23V.
  2. The user's host sends a DNS query for video.netcinema.com.
  3. The user's local DNS server (LDNS) relays the query to NetCinema's authoritative DNS server, which returns a host name in KingCDN's domain, such as a1105.kingcdn.com.
  4. The LDNS sends a query for a1105.kingcdn.com, which goes to KingCDN's own DNS servers; they return the IP address of a KingCDN content server (chosen for this client).
  5. The LDNS forwards that IP address to the user's host.
  6. The client opens a TCP connection to that KingCDN server and sends an HTTP GET for the video (with DASH, first the manifest, then chunks).
U2 · S6 · Q22LQ
What are the cluster selection strategies for CDNs to assign a client?
  • Geographically closest: assign the client to the cluster geographically closest to its local DNS server, using a commercial geo-location database. Simple, but the closest cluster may not be closest in network path length or delay, the LDNS may be far from the client, and it ignores changing delay and bandwidth.
  • Real-time measurements: measure delay and loss between clusters and clients (or their LDNS) periodically, and assign each client the best-performing cluster.
  • IP anycast: let Internet routing choose the closest cluster (see LQ 24).
U2 · S6 · Q23LQ
How do CDNs measure delay and loss performance for a client?

A CDN can have each of its clusters periodically send probes, such as ping messages or DNS queries, to the local DNS servers of clients around the world, and record delay and loss. A drawback is that many LDNSs are configured not to respond to such probes.

Alternatively, the CDN can measure passively, using the characteristics of recent and ongoing traffic between clients and CDN servers (for example TCP round-trip times seen on handshakes), or send a small fraction of a client's DNS queries to different clusters and compare the resulting performance.

U2 · S6 · Q24LQ
How do some CDNs use IP anycast to match clients with CDN servers?

With IP anycast, every CDN cluster is assigned the same IP address, and each cluster's router advertises that address into the Internet using the Border Gateway Protocol (BGP). Routers then see multiple routes to the same address and choose the "best" (closest by BGP) route.

A client's request to that address is therefore routed automatically to the nearest cluster in routing terms, with no DNS tricks. A drawback is that BGP route changes mid-connection can send packets of one TCP connection to a different cluster.

U2 · S6 · Q25LQ
What strategies are used by Netflix, YouTube, and Kankan, respectively, to assign clients to CDN servers?
ServiceHow clients are assigned
NetflixIts own private CDN (Open Connect) with server racks in IXPs and inside residential ISPs. It pushes videos to the CDN servers during off-peak hours rather than pulling on cache misses. Netflix's software running on Amazon's cloud tells the client which CDN server to use; the client then streams with DASH. No DNS redirect is used.
YouTubeGoogle's private CDN with clusters in IXPs and access ISPs. Uses pull caching and DNS redirect, usually to the cluster with the lowest RTT to the client, sometimes to a more distant cluster to balance load. Uses HTTP streaming with a few versions chosen by the user rather than full DASH.
KankanUses P2P delivery instead of (or alongside) a CDN: a client contacts a tracker for a list of peers holding the video and downloads chunks from many peers in parallel (BitTorrent-like), with CDN servers used as a fallback or hybrid source, cutting server costs.
