U2 · Ch2 · P3~4 min

Consider an HTTP client that wants to retrieve a Web document at a given URL. The IP address of the HTTP server is initially unknown. What transport and application-layer protocols besides HTTP are needed in this scenario?

Application layer: the Domain Name System (DNS) is needed to translate the host name in the URL into the server's IP address (in addition to HTTP itself).

Transport layer: the User Datagram Protocol (UDP) carries the DNS query and reply (port 53), and the Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) carries the HTTP request and response (port 80).

So besides HTTP: DNS (application) over UDP, and TCP (transport) for HTTP.

U2 · Ch2 · P7~5 min

Suppose within your Web browser you click on a link to obtain a Web page. The IP address for the associated URL is not cached in your local host, so a DNS lookup is necessary to obtain the IP address. Suppose that n DNS servers are visited before your host receives the IP address from DNS; the successive visits incur an RTT of RTT1, . . . , RTTn. Further suppose that the Web page associated with the link contains exactly one object, consisting of a small amount of HTML text. Let RTT0 denote the RTT between the local host and the server containing the object. Assuming zero transmission time of the object, how much time elapses from when the client clicks on the link until the client receives the object?

The DNS lookup takes the sum of the n round trips to the DNS servers:

\[ T_{DNS} = RTT_1 + RTT_2 + \dots + RTT_n \]

Fetching the object then needs one RTT0 to set up the TCP connection (SYN, SYNACK) and one RTT0 for the HTTP request and the response (the request rides with the third handshake segment). The object's transmission time is zero.

\[ T = RTT_1 + \dots + RTT_n + 2\,RTT_0 \]

Total elapsed time: \(2RTT_0 + RTT_1 + RTT_2 + \dots + RTT_n\).

U2 · Ch2 · P10~15 min

Consider a short, 10-meter link, over which a sender can transmit at a rate of 150 bits/sec in both directions. Suppose that packets containing data are 100,000 bits long, and packets containing only control (e.g., ACK or handshaking) are 200 bits long. Assume that N parallel connections each get 1/N of the link bandwidth. Now consider the HTTP protocol, and suppose that each downloaded object is 100 Kbits long, and that the initial downloaded object contains 10 referenced objects from the same sender. Would parallel downloads via parallel instances of non-persistent HTTP make sense in this case? Now consider persistent HTTP. Do you expect significant gains over the non-persistent case? Justify and explain your answer.

Setup. Propagation delay over 10 m is about \(10/(2\times10^8) = 5\times10^{-8}\) s, negligible. All time is transmission time:

\[ \text{control: } \frac{200}{150} = 1.333\text{ s}, \qquad \text{data: } \frac{100{,}000}{150} = 666.67\text{ s} \]

A non-persistent fetch of one object needs 3 control packets (SYN, SYNACK, ACK/request) plus 1 data packet: \(3(1.333) + 666.67 = 670.67\) s at the full 150 bps.

Parallel non-persistent. The base object must come first (alone, full rate): 670.67 s. Then the 10 referenced objects are fetched over 10 parallel connections, each at 150/10 = 15 bps:

\[ \frac{3(200) + 100{,}000}{15} = 6706.67\text{ s} \quad\Rightarrow\quad T_{par} = 670.67 + 6706.67 = 7377.33\text{ s} \]

Serial non-persistent (one at a time) gives the same: \(11 \times 670.67 = 7377.33\) s. So parallel downloads give no gain (7377.33 s either way): the link is bandwidth-bound, and splitting it among 10 connections only makes each 10 times slower.

Persistent. Handshake once (3 control packets) plus base data, then for each of the 10 objects one request (control) and one data packet:

\[ T_{pers} = 670.67 + 10\left(\frac{200}{150} + \frac{100{,}000}{150}\right) = 670.67 + 6680 = 7350.67\text{ s} \]

Persistent HTTP saves only \(7377.33 - 7350.67 = 26.67\) s (20 handshake packets), about 0.4%. No significant gain: almost all the time is spent transmitting the 100,000-bit data packets, and persistent connections only remove handshakes and RTTs, which are negligible on this short, slow link.

U2 · Ch2 · P11~12 min

Consider the scenario introduced in the previous problem (a 150 bps link, 100,000-bit data packets, 200-bit control packets, N parallel connections each get 1/N of the link bandwidth, a base object referencing 10 objects). Now suppose that the link is shared by Bob with four other users. Bob uses parallel instances of non-persistent HTTP, and the other four users use non-persistent HTTP without parallel downloads.

  1. Do Bob's parallel connections help him get Web pages more quickly? Why or why not?
  2. If all five users open five parallel instances of non-persistent HTTP, then would Bob's parallel connections still be beneficial? Why or why not?

(a) Bandwidth is shared per connection. While Bob fetches his 10 referenced objects he has 10 connections and the others have 4, so 14 connections share 150 bps:

\[ \text{Bob's share} = \frac{10}{14}(150) = 107.14\text{ bps}, \qquad \text{each other user} = \frac{150}{14} = 10.71\text{ bps} \]

Each of Bob's connections gets 10.71 bps and carries 100,600 bits, so all 10 objects arrive after \(100{,}600 / 10.714 = 9389.33\) s. Fetching them one at a time with a single connection (1 of 5 connections, 30 bps) would take \(10 \times 100{,}600/30 = 33{,}533.33\) s.

Yes, parallel connections help Bob (about 3.6 times faster), because he grabs a larger share of the shared link at the expense of the other four users.

(b) With 25 connections in total, Bob's 5 get \(5/25 \times 150 = 30\) bps, exactly the 1/5 share each user would get with one connection each. No advantage over everyone using one connection. However, Bob still needs his parallel connections defensively: if he alone used one connection he would get only \(150/21 = 7.14\) bps. So he should keep them, but they give him no gain relative to the others.

U2 · Ch2 · P13~4 min

Consider sending over HTTP/2 a Web page that consists of one video clip, and five images. Suppose that the video clip is transported as 2000 frames, and each image has three frames.

  1. If all the video frames are sent first without interleaving, how many "frame times" are needed until all five images are sent?
  2. If frames are interleaved, how many frame times are needed until all five images are sent.

(a) All 2000 video frames go first, then the 5 images of 3 frames each:

\[ 2000 + 5 \times 3 = 2015 \text{ frame times} \]

2015 frame times.

(b) Round-robin interleaving sends one frame of each of the 6 objects per round (video, I1, I2, I3, I4, I5). Each image needs 3 frames, so the images finish after 3 rounds:

\[ 3 \text{ rounds} \times 6 \text{ frames per round} = 18 \text{ frame times} \]

18 frame times (15 image frames plus 3 video frames slipped in), about 1997 frame times sooner than in (a).

U2 · Ch2 · P14~3 min

Consider the Web page in problem 13 (one video clip of 2000 frames and five images of three frames each, sent over HTTP/2). Now HTTP/2 prioritization is employed. Suppose all the images are given priority over the video clip, and that the first image is given priority over the second image, the second image over the third image, and so on. How many frame times will be needed until the second image is sent?

With strict priority, the server sends the highest-priority object's frames first. Image 1's 3 frames go in slots 1-3, then image 2's 3 frames in slots 4-6. No video frames are sent before the images.

\[ 3 + 3 = 6 \text{ frame times} \]

The second image is completely sent after 6 frame times.

U2 · Ch2 · P20~6 min

Suppose you can access the caches in the local DNS servers of your department. Can you propose a way to roughly determine the Web servers (outside your department) that are most popular among the users in your department? Explain.

Yes. Periodically take snapshots of the local DNS caches (for example every few minutes). Each cached entry for an external Web server name means some user in the department recently resolved that name, typically in order to visit the site.

Count, over many snapshots, how often each external host name appears in the caches (a name that keeps reappearing after its entry expires is being requested again). The Web servers that appear most frequently are the most popular. Sorting by that count gives a rough popularity ranking of external Web servers.

It is only rough: one cache entry serves many users until its time to live (TTL) expires, so the cache shows that a site was visited, not how many times, and browsers and hosts have their own caches.

U2 · Ch2 · P21~5 min

Suppose that your department has a local DNS server for all computers in the department. You are an ordinary user (i.e., not a network/system administrator). Can you determine if an external Web site was likely accessed from a computer in your department a couple of seconds ago? Explain.

Yes. Use a tool such as dig or nslookup to query the local DNS server for the Web site's host name, and measure the query time.

If the site was accessed a couple of seconds ago, the mapping is in the local DNS server's cache, so the reply comes back almost immediately (a few milliseconds) and is marked non-authoritative. If it was not accessed, the server must query the root, top-level domain and authoritative servers, so the reply takes much longer (tens to hundreds of milliseconds).

So a very short query time means the site was likely accessed recently from the department. (One can also send a non-recursive query: a cached server answers, an uncached one only gives a referral.)

U2 · Ch2 · P22~15 min

Consider distributing a file of F = 20 Gbits to N peers. The server has an upload rate of us = 30 Mbps, and each peer has a download rate of di = 2 Mbps and an upload rate of u. For N = 10, 100, and 1,000 and u = 300 Kbps, 700 Kbps, and 2 Mbps, prepare a chart giving the minimum distribution time for each of the combinations of N and u for both client-server distribution and P2P distribution.

Formulas. \(F = 20\times10^9\) bits, \(u_s = 30\times10^6\) bps, \(d_{min} = 2\times10^6\) bps.

\[ D_{cs} = \max\left\{\frac{NF}{u_s},\ \frac{F}{d_{min}}\right\}, \qquad D_{P2P} = \max\left\{\frac{F}{u_s},\ \frac{F}{d_{min}},\ \frac{NF}{u_s + Nu}\right\} \]

Constants: \(F/u_s = 666.7\) s, \(F/d_{min} = 10{,}000\) s, \(NF/u_s = 6{,}666.7,\ 66{,}666.7,\ 666{,}666.7\) s for N = 10, 100, 1000.

Client-server does not depend on u: N = 10 gives max(6,666.7, 10,000) = 10,000 s; N = 100 gives 66,666.7 s; N = 1000 gives 666,666.7 s.

P2P, the third term \(NF/(u_s+Nu)\):

  • N = 10: \(2\times10^{11}/(3.3\times10^7) = 6{,}060.6\), \(/(3.7\times10^7) = 5{,}405.4\), \(/(5\times10^7) = 4{,}000\) s; all below 10,000, so D = 10,000 s.
  • N = 100: \(2\times10^{12}/(6\times10^7) = 33{,}333.3\), \(/(10^8) = 20{,}000\), \(/(2.3\times10^8) = 8{,}695.7 \to 10{,}000\) s.
  • N = 1000: \(2\times10^{13}/(3.3\times10^8) = 60{,}606.1\), \(/(7.3\times10^8) = 27{,}397.3\), \(/(2.03\times10^9) = 9{,}852.2 \to 10{,}000\) s.

Minimum distribution times (seconds):

SchemeNu = 300 Kbpsu = 700 Kbpsu = 2 Mbps
Client-server1010,00010,00010,000
Client-server10066,666.766,666.766,666.7
Client-server1,000666,666.7666,666.7666,666.7
P2P1010,00010,00010,000
P2P10033,333.320,00010,000
P2P1,00060,606.127,397.310,000

Client-server grows linearly with N (10,000 to 666,666.7 s); P2P stays between 10,000 and 60,606.1 s, never below the \(F/d_{min} = 10{,}000\) s floor.

U2 · Ch2 · P23~10 min

Consider distributing a file of F bits to N peers using a client-server architecture. Assume a fluid model where the server can simultaneously transmit to multiple peers, transmitting to each peer at different rates, as long as the combined rate does not exceed us.

  1. Suppose that us/N ≤ dmin. Specify a distribution scheme that has a distribution time of NF/us.
  2. Suppose that us/N ≥ dmin. Specify a distribution scheme that has a distribution time of F/dmin.
  3. Conclude that the minimum distribution time is in general given by max{NF/us, F/dmin}.

(a) The server splits its upload rate equally and sends the file to all N peers in parallel, each at rate \(u_s/N\). Since \(u_s/N \le d_{min} \le d_i\), every peer can receive at that rate. Each peer receives F bits at rate \(u_s/N\), so all finish at

\[ T = \frac{F}{u_s/N} = \frac{NF}{u_s} \]

Distribution time NF/us.

(b) The server sends to every peer in parallel at rate \(d_{min}\). The combined rate is \(N d_{min} \le u_s\), so the server can sustain it, and every peer can receive at \(d_{min}\). All peers finish at

\[ T = \frac{F}{d_{min}} \]

Distribution time F/dmin.

(c) Lower bounds: the server must upload NF bits at rate at most \(u_s\), so \(D \ge NF/u_s\); the slowest peer must download F bits at rate at most \(d_{min}\), so \(D \ge F/d_{min}\). Hence \(D \ge \max\{NF/u_s, F/d_{min}\}\).

When \(u_s/N \le d_{min}\), \(NF/u_s \ge F/d_{min}\) and the scheme in (a) achieves the max; when \(u_s/N \ge d_{min}\), \(F/d_{min} \ge NF/u_s\) and scheme (b) achieves it. Since the bound is always achieved:

\[ D_{cs} = \max\left\{\frac{NF}{u_s},\ \frac{F}{d_{min}}\right\} \]

The minimum client-server distribution time is max{NF/us, F/dmin}.

U2 · Ch2 · P24~15 min

Consider distributing a file of F bits to N peers using a P2P architecture. Assume a fluid model. For simplicity assume that dmin is very large, so that peer download bandwidth is never a bottleneck.

  1. Suppose that us ≤ (us + u1 + . . . + uN)/N. Specify a distribution scheme that has a distribution time of F/us.
  2. Suppose that us ≥ (us + u1 + . . . + uN)/N. Specify a distribution scheme that has a distribution time of NF/(us + u1 + . . . + uN).
  3. Conclude that the minimum distribution time is in general given by max{F/us, NF/(us + u1 + . . . + uN)}.

Let \(u = u_1 + \dots + u_N\). The server divides the file into N parts, part i of size \(F u_i / u\) (proportional to peer i's upload rate).

(a) Condition: \(u_s \le (u_s+u)/N\), equivalently \(u_s(N-1) \le u\). The server sends part i to peer i at rate \(r_i = u_s u_i / u\); these rates add up to \(u_s\). Peer i forwards each bit of its part to the other N-1 peers as it arrives, at rate \((N-1) r_i = (N-1)u_s u_i/u \le u_i\), which it can sustain because \((N-1)u_s \le u\). Every peer therefore receives bits at the aggregate rate

\[ r_i + \sum_{j\ne i} r_j = u_s \]

so every peer has the whole file after F/us.

(b) Condition: \(u_s \ge (u_s+u)/N\), i.e. \(u_s \ge u/(N-1)\). The server sends part i to peer i at rate \(r_i = u_i/(N-1)\), and peer i relays it to the other N-1 peers at rate \(u_i\) (its full upload). The server uses \(\sum r_i = u/(N-1)\), leaving \(r_{extra} = u_s - u/(N-1) \ge 0\); it splits this leftover equally and sends extra bits at rate \(r_{extra}/N\) directly to every peer. Each peer then receives at rate

\[ \sum_j \frac{u_j}{N-1} + \frac{u_s - u/(N-1)}{N} = \frac{u}{N-1} + \frac{u_s}{N} - \frac{u}{N(N-1)} = \frac{u_s + u}{N} \]

so every peer is done after NF/(us + u1 + ... + uN).

(c) Lower bounds: the server must upload at least one copy, \(D \ge F/u_s\); the whole system must upload NF bits at combined rate at most \(u_s + u\), \(D \ge NF/(u_s+u)\). In case (a) \(F/u_s \ge NF/(u_s+u)\) and scheme (a) achieves it; in case (b) the second term is larger and scheme (b) achieves it. Hence

\[ D_{P2P} = \max\left\{\frac{F}{u_s},\ \frac{NF}{u_s + u_1 + \dots + u_N}\right\} \]

The minimum P2P distribution time is max{F/us, NF/(us + Σui)}.

U2 · Ch2 · P25~3 min

Consider an overlay network with N active peers, with each pair of peers having an active TCP connection. Additionally, suppose that the TCP connections pass through a total of M routers. How many nodes and edges are there in the corresponding overlay network?

The overlay network contains only the peers as nodes; routers belong to the underlying physical network and are not overlay nodes, so M does not matter. Each TCP connection between a pair of peers is one overlay edge, and every pair is connected:

\[ \text{edges} = \binom{N}{2} = \frac{N(N-1)}{2} \]

N nodes and N(N-1)/2 edges.

U2 · Ch2 · P26~6 min

Suppose Bob joins a BitTorrent torrent, but he does not want to upload any data to any other peers (so called free-riding).

  1. Bob claims that he can receive a complete copy of the file that is shared by the swarm. Is Bob's claim possible? Why or why not?
  2. Bob further claims that he can further make his "free-riding" more efficient by using a collection of multiple computers (with distinct IP addresses) in the computer lab in his department. How can he do that?

(a) Yes, it is possible. Although tit-for-tat favours peers that upload, every peer also optimistically unchokes one randomly chosen neighbour every 30 seconds and sends to it regardless of what that neighbour uploads. Bob will be picked as an optimistically unchoked peer from time to time by different neighbours, so he slowly receives chunks and can eventually collect the whole file (more slowly than a peer that uploads). Seeders, which have nothing to download, also upload without receiving anything in return.

(b) Bob runs a BitTorrent client on each lab computer, each joining the torrent as a separate peer (distinct IP address), and each still uploading nothing. Each computer is independently optimistically unchoked by other peers, so together they receive chunks at several times the rate of one free-rider. Bob has them request different chunks (a coordinating program assigns disjoint chunks), then he combines the chunks they collect, for example over the lab LAN, to assemble the complete file faster.

Unit 3

78 questions

Section 1: Transport Layer Principles and Services

16 leading questions
U3 · S1 · Q1LQ
What services does the transport layer provide?

The transport layer provides logical communication between application processes running on different hosts, so each process behaves as if it were directly connected to the other.

  • Process-to-process delivery through multiplexing and demultiplexing with port numbers (extends the network layer's host-to-host delivery).
  • Error checking with a checksum (both UDP and TCP).
  • With the Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) only: reliable, in-order byte-stream delivery, connection management, flow control, and congestion control.

It does not provide delay or bandwidth guarantees on the Internet, because the network layer underneath cannot promise them.

U3 · S1 · Q2LQ
What principles are suggested for use in designing transport layer protocols?

The key principles come from the problem of communicating reliably over an unreliable network:

  • Error detection (checksums) to find corrupted bits.
  • Receiver feedback: positive and negative acknowledgments (ACKs/NAKs).
  • Retransmission of lost or corrupted data, driven by a timer.
  • Sequence numbers so the receiver can spot duplicates and reorder data.
  • Pipelining with windows (Go-Back-N, Selective Repeat) to keep the link busy.
  • Flow control so the sender does not overflow the receiver, and congestion control so senders do not overload the network.

The end-to-end argument also applies: put functions such as reliability in the end systems, keeping the network core simple.

U3 · S1 · Q3LQ
What is the relationship between the transport layer and the network layer?

The network layer gives logical communication between hosts; the transport layer gives logical communication between processes on those hosts.

The transport layer runs only in the end systems and sits on top of the network layer: it hands segments to the Internet Protocol (IP) to carry, and routers never look at transport headers. Its services are limited by the network layer's service model (for example, no delay guarantee if IP gives none), but it can add services IP lacks, such as reliable delivery and encryption. The textbook analogy: houses are hosts, IP is the postal service between houses, and the siblings who collect and hand out mail inside each house are the transport protocol.

U3 · S1 · Q4LQ
What are protocol data units (PDUs) called in TCP and UDP respectively?

A transport-layer protocol data unit (PDU) is called a segment. For the Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) it is a TCP segment. For the User Datagram Protocol (UDP) the textbook also calls it a segment (UDP segment), although the Internet standards (RFCs) call it a datagram; the textbook reserves "datagram" for the network-layer packet to avoid confusion.

U3 · S1 · Q5LQ
What is a best-effort delivery service?

A best-effort delivery service makes its "best effort" to deliver packets but gives no guarantees: packets may be lost, corrupted, duplicated, delivered out of order, or delayed by any amount, and nothing is promised about throughput. The Internet Protocol (IP) provides exactly this service; any guarantees (such as reliability in TCP) must be added above it by the end systems.

U3 · S1 · Q6LQ
What are the multiplexing and demultiplexing applications?

Multiplexing and demultiplexing let many application processes on one host share a single network-layer connection. Each process (a Web browser, an email client, a DNS lookup, a game) opens its own socket, and the transport layer uses port numbers to keep their data separate. Typical uses: a client running several network apps at once; a Web server on port 80 serving many clients simultaneously, each in its own connection socket; DNS and other UDP services answering many requesters on one port.

U3 · S1 · Q7LQ
Why is the IP service model called best-effort delivery service?

The Internet Protocol (IP) service model is called best-effort because IP tries to deliver each datagram from source host to destination host but makes no promises: it does not guarantee delivery, does not guarantee in-order delivery, and does not guarantee the integrity of the data (beyond its header checksum). Routers simply forward each datagram independently and drop it when buffers overflow, with no notification and no retransmission.

U3 · S1 · Q8LQ
Why is IP also called an unreliable service?

IP is called unreliable because it has no mechanism to recover from errors: there are no acknowledgments, no retransmissions, no sequence numbers for reordering, and no flow or congestion control. A datagram that is dropped at a full router queue, corrupted, or duplicated is simply lost or passed on; the sender is never told. "Unreliable" does not mean IP usually fails, only that it does not guarantee success.

U3 · S1 · Q9LQ
How does TCP provide reliable data transfer?

The Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) builds reliability on top of unreliable IP with:

  • Checksums to detect corrupted segments (which are discarded).
  • Sequence numbers (byte numbers) to detect loss and duplicates and to put data back in order.
  • Cumulative acknowledgments telling the sender the next byte expected.
  • A retransmission timer (timeout based on estimated round-trip time) and fast retransmit after three duplicate ACKs.
  • A sliding window so many segments can be in flight (pipelining).

The result is that the receiving process gets an uncorrupted, gap-free, duplicate-free, in-order byte stream.

U3 · S1 · Q10LQ
What services do TCP provide?

The Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) provides:

  • Connection-oriented service: a three-way handshake sets up state before data flows, and the connection is torn down afterwards.
  • Reliable, in-order byte-stream delivery.
  • Full-duplex, point-to-point communication (one sender, one receiver, data both ways).
  • Flow control: the sender never overflows the receiver's buffer.
  • Congestion control: senders slow down when the network is congested.
  • Multiplexing/demultiplexing and error detection (as UDP also does).

It does not provide timing, minimum throughput, or security guarantees (security comes from TLS on top of TCP).

U3 · S1 · Q11LQ
What is multiplexing and demultiplexing respectively?

Multiplexing (at the sender) is gathering data chunks from different sockets, adding a transport header to each (including source and destination port numbers) to make segments, and passing the segments to the network layer.

Demultiplexing (at the receiver) is examining the header fields of each arriving segment and delivering its data to the correct socket.

U3 · S1 · Q12LQ
How are multiplexing and demultiplexing done in TCP?

A TCP socket is identified by a four-tuple: (source IP address, source port, destination IP address, destination port). When a segment arrives, the host uses all four values to choose the connection socket, so two segments with the same destination port but different source IP or source port go to different sockets. A server has a welcoming socket on a well-known port (such as 80); each accepted connection gets its own new connection socket with the same local port but a different four-tuple. On the sending side, multiplexing simply writes the connection's port numbers into each segment header.

U3 · S1 · Q13LQ
What are sockets? How are sockets related to ports?

A socket is the door between an application process and the transport layer: the process writes data into it and reads data out of it, and the operating system handles everything below.

A port number is the 16-bit identifier (0-65535) that names a socket on a host. A UDP socket is identified by (destination IP, destination port); a TCP connection socket by the four-tuple (source IP, source port, destination IP, destination port). So ports are the addresses the transport layer uses to find the right socket.

U3 · S1 · Q14LQ
What are some well-known port numbers?

Ports 0-1023 are well-known port numbers, reserved for standard services:

PortServiceTransport
20/21File Transfer Protocol (FTP) data/controlTCP
22Secure Shell (SSH)TCP
23TelnetTCP
25Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP)TCP
53Domain Name System (DNS)UDP (TCP for large replies)
80Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP)TCP
110 / 143POP3 / IMAPTCP
161Simple Network Management Protocol (SNMP)UDP
443HTTPS (HTTP over TLS)TCP
U3 · S1 · Q15LQ
What are connectionless multiplexing/demultiplexing and connection-oriented multiplexing/demultiplexing, respectively? What are the differences?

Connectionless (UDP) demultiplexing uses only the two-tuple (destination IP, destination port). All segments to that port go to the same socket, even from different senders; the source IP and port are only carried so the receiver can reply.

Connection-oriented (TCP) demultiplexing uses the four-tuple (source IP, source port, destination IP, destination port). Segments from different clients to the same server port go to different connection sockets.

Difference: in UDP one socket serves everyone, while in TCP each connection has its own socket, created when the server accepts a connection request on its welcoming socket.

U3 · S1 · Q16LQ
How are Web servers and TCP related?

A Web server listens on a welcoming TCP socket on port 80 (443 for HTTPS), so all client segments carry destination port 80. When a client connects, the server creates a new connection socket for that client, identified by the four-tuple; it then demultiplexes using the source IP and source port, so many clients (even two connections from one host) are kept separate. The server may hand each connection socket to a new process or, more commonly today, a new thread. With non-persistent HTTP a new TCP connection and socket are made for every request/response, which costs server resources; persistent HTTP reuses one connection.

Section 2: Learning Objectives

23 leading questions
U3 · S2 · Q1LQ
How is a transport layer segment formatted? How is each of the fields created?

Every transport segment is a header followed by the application data. Both UDP and TCP headers start with a 16-bit source port and a 16-bit destination port, and both carry a 16-bit checksum.

  • Ports: the destination port comes from the socket the sending process addressed; the source port is the sender's own socket (a well-known port for servers, an ephemeral port chosen by the operating system for clients).
  • Length (UDP): header plus data in bytes, computed by the sender.
  • Checksum: the 1s complement of the 1s complement sum of all 16-bit words (plus a pseudo-header of IP addresses), computed by the sender.
  • TCP adds the sequence number (byte number of the first data byte), acknowledgment number (next byte expected), header length, flags, receive window, and options, all filled in from the connection's state.
U3 · S2 · Q2LQ
Why is UDP called a connectionless transport protocol?

The User Datagram Protocol (UDP) is connectionless because there is no handshake before data is sent and no connection state kept in either end system. Each segment is sent independently, addressed only by destination IP and port, and the receiver demultiplexes it with no knowledge of any earlier segment.

U3 · S2 · Q3LQ
Why is TCP called a connection-oriented transport protocol?

The Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) is connection-oriented because the two processes must first "handshake" (the three-way handshake) to set up a connection, and both ends then keep connection state: sequence numbers, buffers, window sizes, and timers. The connection exists only in the two end systems; routers in between keep no state about it. The connection is explicitly closed with FIN segments.

U3 · S2 · Q4LQ
What advantages does UDP have over TCP?
  • Finer application control over what and when data is sent: no congestion control throttling, no retransmission delay; good for real-time apps.
  • No connection setup: no handshake delay (why DNS uses UDP).
  • No connection state: a server can support many more active clients.
  • Small header overhead: 8 bytes versus TCP's 20 bytes.

Applications that need reliability over UDP can add it themselves (as QUIC does).

U3 · S2 · Q5LQ
What transport protocols are used by the following application protocols: SMTP, Telnet, HTTP, FTP, NFS, SNMP, RIP, and DNS?
Application protocolTransport
Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP)TCP
Telnet (remote login)TCP
Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP)TCP (HTTP/3 uses QUIC over UDP)
File Transfer Protocol (FTP)TCP
Network File System (NFS)Typically UDP (classic); TCP in modern versions
Simple Network Management Protocol (SNMP)UDP
Routing Information Protocol (RIP)UDP
Domain Name System (DNS)Typically UDP

The 8th edition's Figure 3.6 covers SMTP, Telnet, HTTP, FTP, SNMP and DNS. The NFS and RIP rows come from earlier editions. Rule of thumb: applications that need every byte correct use TCP; short request/reply and management traffic uses UDP.

U3 · S2 · Q6LQ
How is a UDP segment formatted?

The UDP header is only 8 bytes: four 16-bit fields, followed by the application data.

  0             15 16            31
 +----------------+----------------+
 |  Source port   |   Dest port    |
 +----------------+----------------+
 |    Length      |    Checksum    |
 +----------------+----------------+
 |     Application data (message)  |
 +---------------------------------+

Length is the number of bytes in the whole segment (header plus data); the checksum is used for error detection.

U3 · S2 · Q7LQ
What is checksum? How is checksum calculated in UDP?

A checksum is a value computed from the bits of a segment and sent with it, so the receiver can detect whether bits were flipped in transit.

UDP computation at the sender:

  1. Treat the segment (plus a pseudo-header of IP addresses, protocol, and length) as a sequence of 16-bit words, with the checksum field set to 0.
  2. Add the words using 1s complement arithmetic: any carry out of the top bit is wrapped around and added to the low bit.
  3. Take the 1s complement (invert every bit) of the sum; that is the checksum.

The receiver adds all words including the checksum; if the result is all 1s (1111111111111111) no error is detected, and any 0 bit means an error. It cannot catch every error (for example, two compensating bit flips).

U3 · S2 · Q8LQ
What is reliable data transfer? What is unidirectional data transfer? What is bidirectional data transfer?

Reliable data transfer means that no transferred data bits are corrupted or lost and all are delivered in the order sent, even though the channel below may be unreliable.

Unidirectional data transfer: application data flows from the sending side to the receiving side only (control packets such as ACKs still flow back); this is the case the textbook's rdt protocols study.

Bidirectional (full-duplex) data transfer: both sides send data to each other at the same time, as in TCP.

U3 · S2 · Q9LQ
What do the sender and the receiver need to do in order to achieve reliable data transfer over a perfectly reliable channel?

Over a perfectly reliable channel (rdt1.0) almost nothing is needed. The sender just accepts data from above (rdt_send), makes a packet, and sends it (udt_send). The receiver just receives the packet (rdt_rcv), extracts the data, and delivers it up (deliver_data). No feedback, checksums, or sequence numbers are needed; each side has a single-state finite-state machine (FSM), assuming the receiver is as fast as the sender.

U3 · S2 · Q10LQ
How could reliable data transfer be achieved over a channel with bit errors?

Over a channel that can flip bits but not lose packets (rdt2.x), the protocol uses an automatic repeat request (ARQ) scheme:

  • Error detection: a checksum in each packet.
  • Receiver feedback: an ACK for a good packet, a NAK (or, in rdt2.2, a duplicate ACK for the previous packet) for a corrupted one.
  • Retransmission: the sender resends on a NAK or a corrupted ACK.
  • Sequence numbers (1 bit, 0/1, is enough for stop-and-wait) so the receiver can tell a retransmission from new data when an ACK/NAK was corrupted.

The sender is stop-and-wait: it sends one packet and waits for feedback before sending the next.

U3 · S2 · Q11LQ
How could reliable data transfer be achieved over a lossy channel with bit errors?

When packets (data or ACKs) can also be lost (rdt3.0), the sender adds a countdown timer. It starts the timer whenever it sends a packet, and if no valid ACK arrives before the timeout it retransmits. This may create duplicate packets (when the packet was only delayed or the ACK was lost), but the checksum, ACKs, and 1-bit sequence numbers already let the receiver discard duplicates and re-ACK them. rdt3.0 is called the alternating-bit protocol.

U3 · S2 · Q12LQ
Protocol rdt3.0 is a stop-and-wait data transfer protocol with poor performance. What techniques can be used to improve its performance?

rdt3.0's utilization is tiny because the sender is idle for a whole round-trip time (RTT) after each packet: \(U = \frac{L/R}{RTT + L/R}\) (0.00027 in the textbook's 1 Gbps cross-country example). The fix is pipelining: let the sender have many unacknowledged packets in flight. This requires a larger range of sequence numbers, buffering of unacknowledged packets at the sender (and possibly out-of-order packets at the receiver), and a pipelined error-recovery method: Go-Back-N or Selective Repeat. With a window of N packets, utilization rises roughly N times.

U3 · S2 · Q13LQ
What are the two basic approaches to pipelined error recovery?
  • Go-Back-N (GBN): up to N unacknowledged packets; the receiver accepts only in-order packets and sends cumulative ACKs; on a timeout the sender retransmits the lost packet and all packets after it.
  • Selective Repeat (SR): the receiver individually ACKs and buffers out-of-order packets; the sender keeps a timer per packet and retransmits only the packets it suspects were lost or corrupted.
U3 · S2 · Q14LQ
What is the key feature of a Go-Back-N (GBN) protocol?

In Go-Back-N (GBN) the sender may have up to N unacknowledged packets in its window, and recovery is all-or-nothing:

  • The receiver accepts only the next in-order packet; out-of-order packets are discarded and the last in-order packet is re-ACKed.
  • ACKs are cumulative: ACK n means all packets up to and including n arrived.
  • There is a single timer for the oldest unacknowledged packet; on timeout the sender "goes back" and resends every unacknowledged packet in the window.

Simple receiver (no buffering), but one loss can trigger many needless retransmissions.

U3 · S2 · Q15LQ
Why is the GBN protocol also called a sliding-window protocol?

The sender's allowed sequence numbers form a window of size N over the sequence-number space: from base (oldest unacknowledged packet) up to base + N - 1. As cumulative ACKs arrive, base moves forward, so the window slides forward along the sequence numbers, allowing new packets to be sent. That is why GBN is called a sliding-window protocol. The window size N also serves to limit the sender for flow and congestion control.

U3 · S2 · Q16LQ
What is the key feature of the selective repeat protocol? What problem is it intended to solve?

Selective Repeat (SR) has the receiver individually acknowledge every correctly received packet, buffering out-of-order ones, and the sender keeps a timer per packet and retransmits only the packets that were not acknowledged. It solves GBN's problem of unnecessary retransmissions: in GBN one lost packet in a large window, especially with a large bandwidth-delay product, forces the whole window to be resent. Its cost is receiver buffering, and its window size must be at most half the sequence-number space so the receiver cannot confuse new packets with retransmissions.

U3 · S2 · Q17LQ
Why is TCP said to be point-to-point?

A TCP connection is always between exactly one sender and one receiver, identified by its two sockets (the four-tuple). TCP cannot multicast: transferring data from one sender to many receivers in a single send operation is not possible. (It is still full-duplex: both ends send data to each other over the same connection.)

U3 · S2 · Q18LQ
What is a three-way handshake?

The three-way handshake sets up a TCP connection with three segments:

  1. The client sends a SYN segment (SYN bit = 1, no data) with a randomly chosen initial sequence number client_isn.
  2. The server allocates buffers and variables and replies with a SYNACK (SYN = 1, ACK = 1): its own initial sequence number server_isn and acknowledgment number client_isn + 1.
  3. The client allocates its own buffers and sends an ACK (SYN = 0) with acknowledgment number server_isn + 1; this third segment may already carry data.

It lets both sides agree on initial sequence numbers and confirms both directions work.

U3 · S2 · Q19LQ
What fields does a TCP segment have, and what role is assigned to each?
FieldRole
Source / destination port (16 bits each)Multiplexing and demultiplexing
Sequence number (32)Byte-stream number of the first data byte in the segment
Acknowledgment number (32)Next byte expected from the other side (cumulative ACK)
Header length (4)Header size in 32-bit words (options make it variable; usually 20 bytes)
Flags: CWR, ECE, URG, ACK, PSH, RST, SYN, FINCongestion notification (ECN), urgent data, ACK valid, push, reset, connection setup and teardown
Receive window (16)Flow control: free bytes in the receiver's buffer
Internet checksum (16)Error detection
Urgent data pointer (16)Last byte of urgent data (rarely used)
Options (variable)Maximum segment size (MSS) negotiation, window scaling, timestamps, SACK
U3 · S2 · Q20LQ
The Internet Protocol (IP) is an unreliable best-effort protocol, which runs under TCP. How does TCP provide reliable data transfer service over the unreliable IP?

TCP adds reliability at the end systems:

  • Checksum: corrupted segments are discarded.
  • Byte sequence numbers and cumulative ACKs: the receiver says which byte it expects next; gaps and duplicates are detected; out-of-order data is usually buffered.
  • Single retransmission timer with TimeoutInterval = EstimatedRTT + 4 DevRTT; on timeout the oldest unacknowledged segment is retransmitted and the timeout is doubled.
  • Fast retransmit: three duplicate ACKs cause retransmission before the timer expires.
  • Pipelining with a sliding window (a GBN/SR hybrid).
U3 · S2 · Q21LQ
Why does TCP need flow control?

The receiving TCP puts arriving bytes into a receive buffer, and the application may read from it slowly or rarely. Without flow control a fast sender could overflow that buffer, and the data would be lost. TCP's flow control is a speed-matching service: the receiver advertises its free space, rwnd = RcvBuffer - (LastByteRcvd - LastByteRead), in every segment, and the sender keeps LastByteSent - LastByteAcked ≤ rwnd. When rwnd = 0 the sender keeps sending 1-byte probe segments so it learns when space opens up. Flow control protects the receiver; congestion control protects the network.

U3 · S2 · Q22LQ
How is a TCP connection managed?
  • Setup: the three-way handshake (SYN, SYNACK, ACK) exchanges initial sequence numbers and allocates buffers and variables.
  • Data transfer: full-duplex, reliable, with flow and congestion control.
  • Teardown: either side sends a segment with the FIN bit set; the other side ACKs it and later sends its own FIN, which is ACKed; the side that closed first waits in TIME_WAIT (typically 30 s to 2 minutes) before releasing resources, so a lost final ACK can be resent.
  • Reset: a segment with the RST bit is sent when a SYN arrives for a port with no socket.
U3 · S2 · Q23LQ
What are the various TCP states?

Client (typical sequence): CLOSED → (send SYN) SYN_SENT → (receive SYNACK, send ACK) ESTABLISHED → (send FIN) FIN_WAIT_1 → (receive ACK) FIN_WAIT_2 → (receive FIN, send ACK) TIME_WAIT → (wait 30 s) CLOSED.

Server: CLOSED → (create listen socket) LISTEN → (receive SYN, send SYNACK) SYN_RCVD → (receive ACK) ESTABLISHED → (receive FIN, send ACK) CLOSE_WAIT → (send FIN) LAST_ACK → (receive ACK) CLOSED.

Section 3: Learning Objectives

20 leading questions
U3 · S3 · Q1LQ
What is network congestion, and what are its causes?

Network congestion is when too many sources send too much data too fast for the network to handle, so router queues build up and overflow. Symptoms are long queuing delays and lost packets (unlike flow control, which is about one receiver). Causes and costs from the textbook scenarios:

  • Arrival rate approaching link capacity gives very large queuing delays.
  • Finite buffers cause drops, and the sender wastes capacity retransmitting (including unneeded retransmissions of delayed packets).
  • With multi-hop paths, a packet dropped downstream wastes the capacity of every upstream link it used, so throughput can collapse toward zero as offered load grows.
U3 · S3 · Q2LQ
What are the two approaches to congestion control?
  • End-to-end congestion control: the network gives no explicit help; end systems infer congestion from observed loss and delay. Classic TCP works this way.
  • Network-assisted congestion control: routers give explicit feedback, either a direct choke packet to the sender or a mark/field in packets flowing to the receiver, which tells the sender (for example, ATM ABR, DECnet, and Explicit Congestion Notification (ECN) in TCP/IP).
U3 · S3 · Q3LQ
How is network congestion controlled in an ATM ABR service?

Asynchronous Transfer Mode (ATM) Available Bit Rate (ABR) uses network-assisted congestion control: each router (switch) tells the sender the maximum sending rate it can support on its outgoing link, and the sender keeps its rate at or below that. This is all the 8th edition says (Section 3.6.2); the details below come from earlier editions.

The sender mixes resource-management (RM) cells in with its data cells. Switches can set congestion bits (EFCI in data cells, CI and NI in RM cells) or lower the explicit rate (ER) field in RM cells. The destination returns the RM cells to the sender, which adjusts its rate to the lowest ER on the path.

U3 · S3 · Q4LQ
How is network congestion controlled in TCP? Why does TCP use end-to-end congestion control rather than network-assistant congestion control?

TCP has each sender limit its rate with a congestion window (cwnd): the amount of unacknowledged data must be at most min(cwnd, rwnd), so rate is about cwnd/RTT. The sender treats a loss event (timeout or three duplicate ACKs) as a sign of congestion and cuts cwnd; ACKs arriving are taken as a sign that all is well and cwnd grows (slow start, congestion avoidance, fast recovery).

It uses end-to-end control because the IP layer provides no explicit congestion feedback to end systems: routers in the Internet were designed to stay simple and stateless, and TCP must work over any IP network. (ECN is now an optional network-assisted addition.)

U3 · S3 · Q5LQ
What are the principles for TCP?

TCP congestion control follows a few guiding principles:

  • A lost segment implies congestion, so the sending rate should be decreased.
  • An acknowledged segment means the network is delivering, so the rate can be increased when ACKs arrive (TCP is self-clocking).
  • Bandwidth probing: keep increasing the rate until loss occurs, then back off and start probing again.

Each sender acts on local information only, yet together they share the bottleneck.

U3 · S3 · Q6LQ
What is per-connection throughput?

Per-connection throughput is the rate (in bits per second or bytes per second) at which data is delivered to the receiving application by one connection. In the congestion scenarios, it is the "output rate" \(\lambda_{out}\) of a connection, which can be at most R/2 when two connections share a link of rate R. For TCP it is roughly cwnd/RTT, averaging about \(0.75\,W/RTT\) in steady state.

U3 · S3 · Q7LQ
What is offered load to network?

The offered load is the total rate at which a source hands data to the network, including retransmissions, written \(\lambda'_{in}\). It differs from \(\lambda_{in}\), the rate of original application data. When the network drops packets, \(\lambda'_{in} > \lambda_{in}\), and part of the link capacity is spent on retransmitted copies instead of new data.

U3 · S3 · Q8LQ
What approaches to congestion control are used in TCP?

Classic TCP uses end-to-end congestion control: it infers congestion from loss events (timeouts and triple duplicate ACKs), and newer variants also use rising delay (TCP Vegas, BBR). The algorithm combines slow start, congestion avoidance (additive increase), and fast recovery (multiplicative decrease), as in TCP Reno and TCP CUBIC. TCP can also use network-assisted feedback through Explicit Congestion Notification (ECN): routers mark the IP header, the receiver echoes it with the ECE bit, and the sender halves cwnd as for a loss.

U3 · S3 · Q9LQ
How is ABR (available bit rate) approach used for congestion control in ATM networks?

Available Bit Rate (ABR) is an elastic Asynchronous Transfer Mode (ATM) service. The sender may use spare capacity, and the network tells it, in an explicit rate, how fast it may send. The 8th edition gives only this: a router informs the sender of the maximum rate it can support on an outgoing link (network-assisted, rate-based control). Earlier editions list three mechanisms:

  • EFCI bit in data cells, set by a congested switch. The destination then sets CI in the returning RM cell.
  • CI and NI bits in resource-management (RM) cells: CI = decrease the rate, NI = do not increase it.
  • ER field in RM cells, lowered by each switch to the rate it can support. The sender ends up with the minimum along the path.
U3 · S3 · Q10LQ
What is a choked packet?

A choke packet is a control packet sent directly from a congested router back to a sender, telling it to reduce its sending rate. It is one of the two forms of network-assisted feedback; the other is marking a packet flowing to the receiver, which then tells the sender (taking at least a full round-trip time to have effect).

U3 · S3 · Q11LQ
How does a TCP sender coordinate its sending bit rate for congestion control?

The sender keeps a congestion window cwnd and limits unacknowledged data to LastByteSent - LastByteAcked ≤ min(cwnd, rwnd), so its rate is about cwnd/RTT bytes per second. It adjusts cwnd from what it observes:

  • ACKs arriving: increase cwnd (doubling per RTT in slow start; +1 MSS per RTT in congestion avoidance). Because increases are driven by ACKs, TCP is self-clocking.
  • Three duplicate ACKs: halve cwnd (Reno fast recovery).
  • Timeout: set cwnd to 1 MSS and slow start again.
U3 · S3 · Q12LQ
What is the TCP slow start state?

Slow start is the state in which a new (or timed-out) connection begins with cwnd = 1 MSS and increases cwnd by 1 MSS for every ACK received, so cwnd doubles every RTT (exponential growth). It ends when: a timeout occurs (ssthresh = cwnd/2, cwnd = 1, restart slow start); cwnd reaches ssthresh (move to congestion avoidance); or three duplicate ACKs arrive (fast retransmit, then fast recovery). "Slow" refers only to the small starting value.

U3 · S3 · Q13LQ
What is congestion avoidance? How does it work?

Congestion avoidance is the state TCP enters when cwnd reaches ssthresh, about half the window at which congestion last occurred. Instead of doubling, cwnd grows by about 1 MSS per RTT (linear, additive increase): each ACK adds MSS·(MSS/cwnd). On a timeout, ssthresh = cwnd/2 and cwnd = 1 MSS (back to slow start). On three duplicate ACKs, ssthresh = cwnd/2 and cwnd = ssthresh + 3 MSS (enter fast recovery).

U3 · S3 · Q14LQ
How does TCP achieve fast recovery from missing segments?

After three duplicate ACKs, TCP does a fast retransmit of the missing segment without waiting for the timer, and in TCP Reno it enters fast recovery: ssthresh = cwnd/2 and cwnd = ssthresh + 3 MSS (the three segments that left the network). cwnd grows by 1 MSS for each further duplicate ACK; when the ACK for the missing segment arrives, cwnd is set to ssthresh and TCP returns to congestion avoidance. This avoids a drop to cwnd = 1, since duplicate ACKs show the network is still delivering segments.

U3 · S3 · Q15LQ
What are TCP Tahoe and TCP Reno, respectively?
TCP TahoeTCP Reno
Timeoutssthresh = cwnd/2, cwnd = 1 MSS, slow startSame
Three duplicate ACKsssthresh = cwnd/2, cwnd = 1 MSS, slow startssthresh = cwnd/2, cwnd = ssthresh + 3 MSS, fast recovery then congestion avoidance

Tahoe is the earlier version; Reno added fast recovery, so a mild loss (triple duplicate ACK) costs only half the window instead of all of it.

U3 · S3 · Q16LQ
Why is TCP congestion control referred to as an additive-increase, multiplicative-decrease (AIMD)?

Ignoring slow start and timeouts, TCP increases cwnd additively (by 1 MSS every RTT) while there is no loss, and decreases it multiplicatively (halves it) on a triple-duplicate-ACK loss. That gives the characteristic sawtooth pattern, and the name additive-increase, multiplicative-decrease (AIMD). AIMD is what makes competing TCP flows converge toward an equal share of a bottleneck.

U3 · S3 · Q17LQ
How is TCP throughput measured?

TCP throughput is measured as data delivered divided by time, in bits per second, averaged over a long period (many round-trip times (RTTs)), since the instantaneous rate is about cwnd/RTT and keeps changing.

If W is the window (in bytes) when loss occurs, the rate in congestion avoidance cycles between W/(2·RTT) and W/RTT, so the average throughput is about \(0.75\,W/RTT\). In terms of loss rate L, the macroscopic formula is

\[ \text{throughput} \approx \frac{1.22 \cdot MSS}{RTT\,\sqrt{L}} \]

where MSS is the maximum segment size.

U3 · S3 · Q18LQ
What is so-called fairness for TCP connections, especially when they share a single link? How is fairness achieved?

A congestion control mechanism is fair if K connections sharing a bottleneck link of rate R each get an average rate of about R/K. TCP AIMD achieves this (ideally) because every flow gains throughput at the same additive rate but loses a share proportional to its size on loss, so on a plot of the two throughputs the operating point moves toward the equal-share line. The result assumes equal RTTs, the same MSS, and long flows in congestion avoidance; in practice connections with smaller RTTs get more bandwidth.

U3 · S3 · Q19LQ
How is fairness achieved in UDP?

UDP has no congestion control, so fairness is not achieved by UDP itself. A UDP application (for example, streaming or Internet phone) sends at its own constant rate and does not back off, so it can crowd out TCP flows that politely reduce their rates. Fairness has to come from outside: the application adding its own congestion control (TCP-friendly rate control, QUIC), or the network policing such traffic.

U3 · S3 · Q20LQ
How is fairness achieved in parallel TCP connections?

It is not achieved: TCP fairness is per connection, not per application. An application that opens several parallel TCP connections gets a proportionally larger share. Example: on a link of rate R with 9 existing connections, a new application with 1 connection gets about R/10, but one with 11 parallel connections gets 11/20 of R, more than half. Web browsers using parallel connections exploit this.

Chapter 3 problems

19 of 19 assigned problems fit a 20-minute exam slot
U3 · Ch3 · P2~4 min

Consider Figure 3.5. What are the source and destination port values in the segments flowing from the server back to the clients' processes? What are the IP addresses in the network-layer datagrams carrying the transport-layer segments?

Figure 3.5 (redrawn): host C opens two HTTP connections to Web server B, using source ports 7532 and 26145; host A opens one, using source port 26145. All three are sent to destination port 80. The hosts' IP addresses are A, B and C.

On the way back the server swaps source and destination: its own port 80 becomes the source port, and the client's port becomes the destination port. The IP addresses are swapped the same way.

Segment from B toSource portDest portSource IPDest IP
A (its one connection)8026145BA
C (first connection)807532BC
C (second connection)8026145BC

Source port 80; destination ports 26145 (to A), 7532 and 26145 (to C). Source IP B; destination IP A or C.

The two segments with destination port 26145 do not clash: they go to different hosts (A and C), and even at the server they were told apart by source IP.

U3 · Ch3 · P3~8 min

UDP and TCP use 1s complement for their checksums. Suppose you have the following three 8-bit bytes: 01010011, 01100110, 01110100. What is the 1s complement of the sum of these 8-bit bytes? (Note that although UDP and TCP use 16-bit words in computing the checksum, for this problem you are being asked to consider 8-bit sums.) Show all work. Why is it that UDP takes the 1s complement of the sum; that is, why not just use the sum? With the 1s complement scheme, how does the receiver detect errors? Is it possible that a 1-bit error will go undetected? How about a 2-bit error?

Add with wraparound (a carry out of bit 7 is added back to bit 0):

    01010011
  + 01100110
  ----------
    10111001
  + 01110100
  ----------
  1 00101101    carry out of the top bit
  +        1    wrap it around
  ----------
    00101110    sum
    11010001    1s complement (invert every bit)

Checksum = 11010001

Why the complement? The receiver adds all the bytes including the checksum. Since a number plus its 1s complement is all 1s, an error-free segment gives 11111111; checking "all ones" is simpler than comparing a recomputed sum against a stored one.

Detection: if any bit of the receiver's total is 0, an error is detected.

1-bit error: always detected - flipping one bit changes the sum.

2-bit error: can go undetected - e.g. if bit 0 of the first byte flips 1→0 and bit 0 of the second byte flips 0→1, the sum is unchanged.

U3 · Ch3 · P5~3 min

Suppose that the UDP receiver computes the Internet checksum for the received UDP segment and finds that it matches the value carried in the checksum field. Can the receiver be absolutely certain that no bit errors have occurred? Explain.

No. The checksum is a 16-bit summary of a much longer segment, so many different bit patterns give the same checksum. Errors that cancel in the 1s complement sum go undetected: for example, two 16-bit words where the same bit position flips 0→1 in one and 1→0 in the other leave the sum unchanged. A matching checksum only means no error was detected, not that none occurred.

U3 · Ch3 · P15~6 min

Consider the cross-country example shown in Figure 3.17. How big would the window size have to be for the channel utilization to be greater than 98 percent? Suppose that the size of a packet is 1,500 bytes, including both header fields and data.

(Figure 3.17's cross-country example: transmission rate R = 1 Gbps, round-trip propagation delay RTT = 30 ms.)

Transmission time of one packet:

\[ \frac{L}{R} = \frac{1500 \times 8 \text{ bits}}{10^9 \text{ bits/s}} = 12\ \mu\text{s} = 0.012 \text{ ms} \]

With a window of n packets sent back-to-back, the sender is busy for \(nL/R\) out of every \(RTT + L/R\):

\[ U = \frac{n\,L/R}{RTT + L/R} = \frac{0.012\,n}{30.012} > 0.98 \;\Rightarrow\; n > \frac{0.98 \times 30.012}{0.012} = 2450.98 \]

The window must be at least 2451 packets. (Check: \(2451 \times 0.012 / 30.012 = 0.98001\).)

U3 · Ch3 · P16~5 min

Suppose an application uses rdt 3.0 as its transport layer protocol. As the stop-and-wait protocol has very low channel utilization (shown in the cross-country example), the designers of this application let the receiver keep sending back a number (more than two) of alternating ACK 0 and ACK 1 even if the corresponding data have not arrived at the receiver. Would this application design increase the channel utilization? Why? Are there any potential problems with this approach? Explain.

Yes, utilization increases. The sender transmits its next packet as soon as it receives the matching ACK, so the stream of early ACKs lets it send packet after packet without waiting a round trip for each: in effect it becomes pipelined.

But reliability is lost. An ACK no longer means "your packet arrived". If a data packet is lost or corrupted, the sender has already received an (early) ACK for it and will never retransmit it, so data is silently lost. With only 1-bit sequence numbers, the receiver also cannot tell which of many in-flight packets is which, so it can accept duplicates or misorder data. The right way to raise utilization is a real pipelined protocol (Go-Back-N or Selective Repeat) with larger sequence numbers.

U3 · Ch3 · P17~10 min

Consider two network entities, A and B, which are connected by a perfect bi-directional channel (i.e., any message sent will be received correctly; the channel will not corrupt, lose, or re-order packets). A and B are to deliver data messages to each other in an alternating manner: First, A must deliver a message to B, then B must deliver a message to A, then A must deliver a message to B and so on. If an entity is in a state where it should not attempt to deliver a message to the other side, and there is an event like rdt_send(data) call from above that attempts to pass data down for transmission to the other side, this call from above can simply be ignored with a call to rdt_unable_to_send(data), which informs the higher layer that it is currently not able to send data. [Note: This simplifying assumption is made so you don't have to worry about buffering data.]

Draw a FSM specification for this protocol (one FSM for A, and one FSM for B!). Note that you do not have to worry about a reliability mechanism here; the main point of this question is to create a FSM specification that reflects the synchronized behavior of the two entities. You should use the following events and actions that have the same meaning as protocol rdt1.0 in Figure 3.9: rdt_send(data), packet = make_pkt(data), udt_send(packet), rdt_rcv(packet), extract(packet,data), deliver_data(data). Make sure your protocol reflects the strict alternation of sending between A and B. Also, make sure to indicate the initial states for A and B in your FSM descriptions.

Each entity needs two states: "my turn to send" and "waiting to receive". A starts in the sending state, B in the receiving state. Transitions are written event / actions.

FSM for A (initial state marked ⇒):

⇒ [Wait for call from above]
      rdt_send(data) / packet = make_pkt(data); udt_send(packet)
          --> [Wait for packet from B]
      rdt_rcv(packet)  (cannot occur; B is not allowed to send) / none

  [Wait for packet from B]
      rdt_rcv(packet) / extract(packet,data); deliver_data(data)
          --> [Wait for call from above]
      rdt_send(data) / rdt_unable_to_send(data)     (self-loop)

FSM for B (same machine, opposite starting state):

⇒ [Wait for packet from A]
      rdt_rcv(packet) / extract(packet,data); deliver_data(data)
          --> [Wait for call from above]
      rdt_send(data) / rdt_unable_to_send(data)     (self-loop)

  [Wait for call from above]
      rdt_send(data) / packet = make_pkt(data); udt_send(packet)
          --> [Wait for packet from A]

A: initial state "Wait for call from above"; B: initial state "Wait for packet from A". Each entity can send only right after it has received, so sends strictly alternate A, B, A, B, ...

U3 · Ch3 · P19~15 min

Consider a scenario in which Host A wants to simultaneously send packets to Hosts B and C. A is connected to B and C via a broadcast channel - a packet sent by A is carried by the channel to both B and C. Suppose that the broadcast channel connecting A, B, and C can independently lose and corrupt packets (and so, for example, a packet sent from A might be correctly received by B, but not by C). Design a stop-and-wait-like error-control protocol for reliably transferring packets from A to B and C, such that A will not get new data from the upper layer until it knows that both B and C have correctly received the current packet. Give FSM descriptions of A and C. (Hint: The FSM for B should be essentially the same as for C.) Also, give a description of the packet format(s) used.

Packet formats. Data packet (A→B,C): [seq (1 bit) | checksum | data]. ACK packet (B or C → A): [ACK | seq (1 bit) | receiver id (B or C) | checksum]. The receiver id lets A tell which host has acknowledged.

Sender A (rdt3.0 extended; shown for seq 0, the seq 1 half is symmetric):

⇒ [Wait for call 0 from above]
    rdt_send(data) / sndpkt = make_pkt(0, data, checksum); udt_send(sndpkt);
                     start_timer; ackB = false; ackC = false
        --> [Wait for ACK 0 from B and C]

  [Wait for ACK 0 from B and C]
    rdt_rcv(rcvpkt) && notcorrupt && isACK(rcvpkt,0) && from B / ackB = true
    rdt_rcv(rcvpkt) && notcorrupt && isACK(rcvpkt,0) && from C / ackC = true
      (after either: if ackB && ackC then stop_timer --> [Wait for call 1 from above])
    rdt_rcv(rcvpkt) && (corrupt || isACK(rcvpkt,1)) / do nothing
    timeout / udt_send(sndpkt); start_timer      (rebroadcast; B or C
                                                  discards a duplicate and re-ACKs)

Receiver C (identical to the rdt3.0 receiver, but ACKs carry C's id):

⇒ [Wait for 0 from below]
    rdt_rcv(rcvpkt) && notcorrupt && has_seq0 / extract; deliver_data(data);
         sndpkt = make_pkt(ACK, 0, C, checksum); udt_send(sndpkt)
        --> [Wait for 1 from below]
    rdt_rcv(rcvpkt) && (corrupt || has_seq1) /
         sndpkt = make_pkt(ACK, 1, C, checksum); udt_send(sndpkt)   (re-ACK last)

  [Wait for 1 from below]  (same with 0 and 1 swapped)

A keeps one timer and two flags; it moves on only when valid ACKs with the current sequence number have arrived from both B and C. B's FSM is C's with id B. A 1-bit sequence number is still enough because A never sends packet n+1 until both receivers hold packet n.

U3 · Ch3 · P22~8 min

Consider the GBN protocol with a sender window size of 4 and a sequence number range of 1,024. Suppose that at time t, the next in-order packet that the receiver is expecting has a sequence number of k. Assume that the medium does not reorder messages. Answer the following questions:

  1. What are the possible sets of sequence numbers inside the sender's window at time t? Justify your answer.
  2. What are all possible values of the ACK field in all possible messages currently propagating back to the sender at time t? Justify your answer.

N = 4. The receiver has received and ACKed everything up to k-1. (Arithmetic is modulo 1024.)

(a) Extreme 1: the sender has received all ACKs up to k-1, so its base is k: window [k, k+3]. Extreme 2: none of the last 4 ACKs (for k-4 ... k-1) has reached the sender yet; it cannot have sent past k-1 without base moving, so the window is [k-4, k-1]. Anything in between is possible:

[k-4, k-1], [k-3, k], [k-2, k+1], [k-1, k+2], or [k, k+3] - i.e. [k-i, k+3-i] for i = 0, 1, 2, 3, 4.

(b) In-flight ACKs can only be for packets the receiver has accepted (at most k-1) whose ACK the sender has not yet seen. If the window is [k-4, k-1], ACKs k-4 ... k-1 may still be travelling; also the sender must have received ACK k-5 for base to be k-4, but a copy of ACK k-5 (re-sent when an out-of-order packet arrived) may still be on its way. Nothing older than k-5 can be in flight, because ACK k-5 was already received and the channel does not reorder.

ACK values in flight: k-5, k-4, k-3, k-2, k-1.

U3 · Ch3 · P27~10 min

Host A and B are communicating over a TCP connection, and Host B has already received from A all bytes up through byte 126. Suppose Host A then sends two segments to Host B back-to-back. The first and second segments contain 80 and 40 bytes of data, respectively. In the first segment, the sequence number is 127, the source port number is 302, and the destination port number is 80. Host B sends an acknowledgment whenever it receives a segment from Host A.

  1. In the second segment sent from Host A to B, what are the sequence number, source port number, and destination port number?
  2. If the first segment arrives before the second segment, in the acknowledgment of the first arriving segment, what is the acknowledgment number, the source port number, and the destination port number?
  3. If the second segment arrives before the first segment, in the acknowledgment of the first arriving segment, what is the acknowledgment number?
  4. Suppose the two segments sent by A arrive in order at B. The first acknowledgment is lost and the second acknowledgment arrives after the first timeout interval. Draw a timing diagram, showing these segments and all other segments and acknowledgments sent. (Assume there is no additional packet loss.) For each segment in your figure, provide the sequence number and the number of bytes of data; for each acknowledgment that you add, provide the acknowledgment number.

First segment covers bytes 127-206; second covers bytes 207-246.

(a) Sequence number 127 + 80 = 207, source port 302, destination port 80.

(b) B now has everything through byte 206 and expects 207. The ACK travels B→A, so ports swap: ACK number 207, source port 80, destination port 302.

(c) Byte 127 is still missing; TCP ACKs are cumulative, so B repeats the next byte it expects: ACK number 127.

(d) The first ACK (207) is lost. A's timer for the oldest unacknowledged segment (Seq 127) expires before ACK 247 arrives, so A retransmits Seq 127 (80 bytes). Then ACK 247 arrives (it acknowledges all bytes through 246, so A sends nothing more). B receives the duplicate Seq 127 and replies with ACK 247 again.

Segments: Seq 127 (80 B), Seq 207 (40 B), retransmitted Seq 127 (80 B). ACKs: 207 (lost), 247, 247.

U3 · Ch3 · P31~12 min

Suppose that the five measured SampleRTT values (see Section 3.5.3) are 106 ms, 120 ms, 140 ms, 90 ms, and 115 ms. Compute the EstimatedRTT after each of these SampleRTT values is obtained, using a value of α = 0.125 and assuming that the value of EstimatedRTT was 100 ms just before the first of these five samples were obtained. Compute also the DevRTT after each sample is obtained, assuming a value of β = 0.25 and assuming the value of DevRTT was 5 ms just before the first of these five samples was obtained. Last, compute the TCP TimeoutInterval after each of these samples is obtained.

\[ \text{EstRTT} = 0.875\,\text{EstRTT} + 0.125\,\text{SampleRTT} \] \[ \text{DevRTT} = 0.75\,\text{DevRTT} + 0.25\,|\text{SampleRTT} - \text{EstRTT}| \] \[ \text{TimeoutInterval} = \text{EstRTT} + 4\,\text{DevRTT} \]

Sample 1 worked: EstRTT = 0.875(100) + 0.125(106) = 100.75 ms; DevRTT = 0.75(5) + 0.25|106 - 100.75| = 5.06 ms; Timeout = 100.75 + 4(5.06) = 121.00 ms. Continue with unrounded values (DevRTT uses the EstRTT just computed, the textbook's order):

U3 · Ch3 · P32~8 min

Consider the TCP procedure for estimating RTT. Suppose that α = 0.1. Let SampleRTT1 be the most recent sample RTT, let SampleRTT2 be the next most recent sample RTT, and so on.

  1. For a given TCP connection, suppose four acknowledgments have been returned with corresponding sample RTTs: SampleRTT4, SampleRTT3, SampleRTT2, and SampleRTT1. Express EstimatedRTT in terms of the four sample RTTs.
  2. Generalize your formula for n sample RTTs.
  3. For the formula in part (b) let n approach infinity. Comment on why this averaging procedure is called an exponential moving average.

Each update is EstimatedRTT = 0.1 SampleRTT + 0.9 EstimatedRTT. Take the first estimate as EstimatedRTT = SampleRTT4 (the oldest sample) and unroll.

(a)

\[ \text{Est} = 0.1\,S_1 + 0.1(0.9)\,S_2 + 0.1(0.9)^2\,S_3 + (0.9)^3\,S_4 \]

EstimatedRTT = 0.1 S1 + 0.09 S2 + 0.081 S3 + 0.729 S4 (weights sum to 1). If an earlier initial estimate is kept instead, S4's weight is 0.1(0.9)3 = 0.0729 and the remaining 0.6561 multiplies that initial estimate.

(b)

\[ \text{Est} = 0.1 \sum_{j=1}^{n-1} (0.9)^{j-1}\,S_j + (0.9)^{n-1}\,S_n \]

(c) As n → ∞, \((0.9)^{n-1} \to 0\), so

\[ \text{Est} = \frac{1}{9} \sum_{j=1}^{\infty} (0.9)^{j}\,S_j = 0.1 \sum_{j=1}^{\infty} (0.9)^{j-1}\,S_j \]

The weight of each sample shrinks by a factor 0.9 per newer sample - it decays exponentially with age. It is a moving average because it always reflects the latest samples, with old ones fading out; hence "exponential weighted moving average".

Compare GBN, SR, and TCP (no delayed ACK). Assume that the timeout values for all three protocols are sufficiently long such that five consecutive data segments and their corresponding ACKs can be received (if not lost in the channel) by the receiving host (Host B) and the sending host (Host A) respectively. Suppose Host A sends five data segments to Host B, and the second segment (sent from A) is lost. In the end, all five data segments have been correctly received by Host B.

  1. How many segments has Host A sent in total and how many ACKs has Host B sent in total? What are their sequence numbers? Answer this question for all three protocols.
  2. If the timeout values for all three protocol are much longer than 5 RTT, then which protocol successfully delivers all five data segments in shortest time interval?

Number the segments 1-5 (for TCP, think of each as one byte-range with "sequence number" 1-5); segment 2 is lost.

(a)

ProtocolSegments sent by AACKs sent by B
GBN9: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, then on timeout 2, 3, 4, 58: ACK 1, 1, 1, 1 (3, 4, 5 discarded, ACK 1 repeated), then 2, 3, 4, 5
SR6: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, then on timeout 25: ACK 1, 3, 4, 5, then 2

U3 · Ch3 · P40~15 min

Consider Figure 3.61. Assuming TCP Reno is the protocol experiencing the behavior shown above, answer the following questions. In all cases, you should provide a short discussion justifying your answer.

Figure 3.61 (redrawn): TCP window size as a function of time. Values by round 1-26: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 33, 34, ..., 42 (round 16), 24 (round 17), 25, 26, 27, 28, 29 (round 22), 1 (round 23), 2, 4, 8 (round 26).

  1. Identify the intervals of time when TCP slow start is operating.
  2. Identify the intervals of time when TCP congestion avoidance is operating.
  3. After the 16th transmission round, is segment loss detected by a triple duplicate ACK or by a timeout?
  4. After the 22nd transmission round, is segment loss detected by a triple duplicate ACK or by a timeout?
  5. What is the initial value of ssthresh at the first transmission round?
  6. What is the value of ssthresh at the 18th transmission round?
  7. What is the value of ssthresh at the 24th transmission round?
  8. During what transmission round is the 70th segment sent?
  9. Assuming a packet loss is detected after the 26th round by the receipt of a triple duplicate ACK, what will be the values of the congestion window size and of ssthresh?
  10. Suppose TCP Tahoe is used (instead of TCP Reno), and assume that triple duplicate ACKs are received at the 16th round. What are the ssthresh and the congestion window size at the 19th round?
  11. Again suppose TCP Tahoe is used, and there is a timeout event at 22nd round. How many packets have been sent out from 17th round till 22nd round, inclusive?
  1. cwnd doubles each round: rounds 1-6 and 23-26.
  2. cwnd grows by 1 per round: rounds 6-16 and 17-22.
  3. cwnd fell from 42 to 24 = 42/2 + 3, not to 1, which is Reno's fast recovery: triple duplicate ACK.
  4. cwnd fell from 29 to 1: timeout.
  5. Doubling stopped at 32 (next round 33, not 64): ssthresh = 32.
  6. Set at the loss after round 16: ssthresh = 42/2 = 21.
  7. Set at the timeout after round 22: ssthresh = ⌊29/2⌋ = 14 (rounds 23-26 stay below it, so still slow start).
  8. Running total of segments: rounds 1-6 send 1+2+4+8+16+32 = 63; round 7 sends segments 64-96. Round 7.
  9. cwnd in round 26 is 8: ssthresh = 4, cwnd = 4 + 3 = 7.
  10. Tahoe: ssthresh = 42/2 = 21 and cwnd restarts at 1: round 17 = 1, 18 = 2, 19 = 4. ssthresh = 21, cwnd = 4.
  11. Rounds 17-22: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, then 21 (capped at ssthresh, then linear). Total = 1+2+4+8+16+21 = 52 segments.
U3 · Ch3 · P43~5 min

Host A is sending an enormous file to Host B over a TCP connection. Over this connection there is never any packet loss and the timers never expire. Denote the transmission rate of the link connecting Host A to the Internet by R bps. Suppose that the process in Host A is capable of sending data into its TCP socket at a rate S bps, where S = 10 · R. Further suppose that the TCP receive buffer is large enough to hold the entire file, and the send buffer can hold only one percent of the file. What would prevent the process in Host A from continuously passing data to its TCP socket at rate S bps? TCP flow control? TCP congestion control? Or something else? Elaborate.

Something else: the full send buffer (limited by the link rate R).

Not flow control: the receive buffer can hold the whole file, so rwnd never limits A. Not congestion control: there is never any loss or timeout, so cwnd keeps growing and never cuts the rate.

The bottleneck is A's own access link. TCP can drain the send buffer at most at R bps, but the process fills it at S = 10R bps. The send buffer (1% of the file) fills quickly; once full, the socket write call blocks until space frees up, so the process is held to about R bps on average.

U3 · Ch3 · P45~15 min

Consider Figure 3.54. Suppose that at t3, the sending rate at which congestion loss next occurs drops to 0.75*Wmax (unbeknownst to the TCP senders, of course). Show the evolution of both TCP Reno and TCP CUBIC for two more rounds each (Hint: note that the times at which TCP Reno and TCP CUBIC react to congestion loss may not be the same anymore).

Figure 3.54 (redrawn, idealized): after slow start, both senders' rates oscillate between Wmax/2 and Wmax, losing at t1, t2, t3, ...; Reno rises in a straight line, CUBIC rises quickly and then flattens near Wmax.

Model of Figure 3.54 (idealized, as drawn): time in units of T = t1 - t0, rate in units of Wmax. Both senders halve on loss. Reno climbs linearly from 0.5 to 1 in T, slope 0.5 per T. CUBIC follows \(W(t) = C(t-K)^3 + W_{max}\) with \(K = \sqrt[3]{(W_{max} - W_{max}/2)/C}\); K = T in the figure, so C = 0.5. Just after t3 both are at 0.5.

TCP Reno. Round 1: from 0.5 it reaches 0.75 after (0.75 - 0.5)/0.5 = 0.5T, so loss at t3 + 0.50T; it halves to 0.375. Round 2: climb 0.375 → 0.75 takes 0.75T, so loss at t3 + 1.25T, back to 0.375. New sawtooth between 0.375 and 0.75 Wmax, period 0.75T.

TCP CUBIC. Round 1: still aiming at the old Wmax = 1 with K = T: \(0.5(t-1)^3 + 1 = 0.75 \Rightarrow t = 1 - \sqrt[3]{0.5} = 0.21T\), so loss at t3 + 0.21T (earlier than Reno, because its curve is steeper at first). It sets Wmax = 0.75 and drops to 0.375. Round 2: \(K = \sqrt[3]{0.375/0.5} = \sqrt[3]{0.75} = 0.91T\); it rises fast, flattens just below 0.75 and is hit at K: loss at t3 + 1.11T, then again every 0.91T.
