1110000224.0.0.0/72
11100001 1225.128.0.0/93
otherwise0.0.0.0/03

224.0.0.0/10 -> 0; 224.64.0.0/16 -> 1; 224.0.0.0/7 -> 2; 225.128.0.0/9 -> 3; otherwise -> 3.

Check: the /7 holds 225 = 33,554,432 addresses; removing the /10 (4,194,304), /16 (65,536) and /9 (8,388,608) leaves 20,905,984, exactly the size of link 2's range (225 - 222 - 216 - 223).

U4 · Ch4 · P19~8 min

Suppose you are interested in detecting the number of hosts behind a NAT. You observe that the IP layer stamps an identification number sequentially on each IP packet. The identification number of the first IP packet generated by a host is a random number, and the identification numbers of the subsequent IP packets are sequentially assigned. Assume all IP packets generated by hosts behind the NAT are sent to the outside world.

  1. Based on this observation, and assuming you can sniff all packets sent by the NAT to the outside, can you outline a simple technique that detects the number of unique hosts behind a NAT? Justify your answer.
  2. If the identification numbers are not sequentially assigned but randomly assigned, would your technique work? Justify your answer.
  1. Yes. Capture every packet leaving the NAT and record the 16-bit identification field of its IP header. Group the IDs into chains in which each ID is exactly one more than the previous one in the chain (wrapping from 65,535 to 0). Each host numbers its packets consecutively from its own random start, so each host produces exactly one chain, and different hosts start at independent random points among 65,536 values, so their chains are very unlikely to overlap. Therefore the number of distinct sequential chains = the number of hosts behind the NAT. Example: IDs 4113, 870, 4114, 871, 52001, 4115, 872, 52002 form the chains {4113, 4114, 4115}, {870, 871, 872}, {52001, 52002}: 3 hosts. (Counting source IP addresses would not work, since the NAT gives every packet the same public address.)
  2. No. With random IDs a host's next packet is not "previous + 1", so nothing links packets of the same host; a chance +1 link occurs with probability only 1/65,536. The technique fails: it would report roughly one "host" per packet.
U4 · Ch4 · P20~12 min

In this problem, we'll explore the impact of NATs on P2P applications. Suppose a peer with username Arnold discovers through querying that a peer with username Bernard has a file it wants to download. Also suppose that Bernard and Arnold are both behind a NAT. Try to devise a technique that will allow Arnold to establish a TCP connection with Bernard without application-specific NAT configuration. If you have difficulty devising such a technique, discuss why.

Why it is hard. Bernard has a private address, so Arnold can only aim his TCP SYN at Bernard's NAT's public address, and the NAT forwards an incoming segment only if its translation table has an entry for it, which is created only when Bernard sends something out. Arnold's SYN therefore matches nothing and is dropped. Connection reversal (asking Bernard, via a server he is connected to, to connect out to Arnold) fails for the same reason, because Arnold is also behind a NAT. Two peers behind NATs cannot connect directly without help from a third party with a public address.

Technique 1: relay. Both peers open ordinary outgoing TCP connections to a public relay server R (outgoing connections are always allowed by a NAT). R copies bytes between the two connections. This always works, but every byte crosses R twice and the path is longer; strictly it is two TCP connections, not one direct one.

Technique 2: TCP hole punching. Both peers connect out to a public rendezvous server S, which learns each one's public IP address and port (for example Arnold appears as 1.1.1.1:6000 and Bernard as 2.2.2.2:7000) and tells each peer the other's. Both then send a SYN to the other's public address at about the same time, from the same local port they used with S. Each outgoing SYN creates an entry in its own NAT, so the other peer's SYN is then accepted, and TCP simultaneous open gives a direct connection.

A direct connection is impossible without a third party; use a relay (always works) or hole punching via a rendezvous server (direct, but not guaranteed). Hole punching fails with symmetric NATs, which choose a new public port for every destination so the port S saw is not the one used toward the peer, and with NATs that reset or drop unexpected SYNs; practical systems therefore try hole punching and fall back to a relay (the STUN, TURN and ICE standards).

U4 · Ch4 · P22~10 min

Consider again the SDN OpenFlow network shown in Figure 4.30 (redrawn below; small numbers are switch port numbers). Suppose that the desired forwarding behavior for datagrams arriving from hosts h3 or h4 at s2 is as follows:

  • any datagrams arriving from host h3 and destined for h1, h2, h5 or h6 should be forwarded in a clockwise direction in the network;
  • any datagrams arriving from host h4 and destined for h1, h2, h5 or h6 should be forwarded in a counter-clockwise direction in the network.

Specify the flow table entries in s2 that implement this forwarding behavior.

Wiring at s2: port 1 goes to s3, port 2 goes to s1, port 3 to h3, port 4 to h4. With s3 top-left, s2 top-right and s1 at the bottom, clockwise is s2 -> s1 -> s3, so from s2 clockwise = port 2 (toward s1) and counter-clockwise = port 1 (toward s3). Hosts h1, h2 are 10.1.*.* and h5, h6 are 10.3.*.*. Match on the ingress port and the source address to tell h3 from h4:

MatchAction
Ingress port = 3; IP Src = 10.2.0.3; IP Dst = 10.1.*.*Forward(2)
Ingress port = 3; IP Src = 10.2.0.3; IP Dst = 10.3.*.*Forward(2)
Ingress port = 4; IP Src = 10.2.0.4; IP Dst = 10.1.*.*Forward(1)
Ingress port = 4; IP Src = 10.2.0.4; IP Dst = 10.3.*.*Forward(1)

h3's traffic to h1, h2, h5, h6 -> Forward(2) (clockwise); h4's traffic -> Forward(1) (counter-clockwise). Listing the four exact destination addresses per source (8 rows) is equally correct. Note h3 -> h5 takes the long way s2 -> s1 -> s3, which is what the problem asks for.

Unit 5

111 questions

Section 1: Introduction to the Network Control Plane

6 leading questions · Textbook Section 5.1. Introduction,
U5 · S1 · Q1LQ
What role does a forwarding table play in a router?

A forwarding table is the router's lookup table for the data plane: the router looks up a value in an arriving packet's header (in traditional routing, the destination IP address, matched by longest prefix) and the table entry names the outgoing link interface to which the packet must be switched.

Forwarding is a local, per-router action that takes nanoseconds, while the contents of the table are decided by the control plane.

U5 · S1 · Q2LQ
What does a flow table play?

A flow table is the generalized form of the forwarding table used in software-defined networking (SDN), for example by OpenFlow switches.

Each entry matches on many header fields from the link, network and transport layers (MAC addresses, IP addresses, port numbers, VLAN and so on), and it holds an action to apply to matching packets: forward to a port, drop, modify header fields or send to the controller, plus counters.

So a flow table implements "match plus action", which covers forwarding, firewalling, load balancing and network address translation (NAT) in one device.

U5 · S1 · Q3LQ
How is a forwarding table in a router computed, maintained, and installed?

In the traditional per-router approach, every router runs a routing algorithm component, and these components communicate with each other through a routing protocol such as Open Shortest Path First (OSPF) or the Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) to compute the routes.

Each router builds its own forwarding table from the result and installs it locally, and the tables are maintained by recomputing whenever the protocol reports a topology or cost change.

U5 · S1 · Q4LQ
How is flow table in a router computed, maintained, and installed?

In software-defined networking (SDN), a logically centralized remote controller computes the flow tables for all switches, using its global view of the network and the network-control applications that run on it (routing, access control, load balancing).

The controller then installs and updates entries in each switch through a southbound protocol such as OpenFlow, and the switches report events (link changes, new flows) back to the controller, which recomputes and pushes new entries.

The switches themselves run no routing algorithm.

U5 · S1 · Q5LQ
What is per-router control?

Per-router control is the traditional control-plane approach in which an individual routing algorithm runs in each and every router.

Each router has a routing component that exchanges routing messages with the routing components of other routers, and each router computes its own forwarding table, so the control plane is distributed across all routers.

Open Shortest Path First (OSPF) and the Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) work this way.

U5 · S1 · Q6LQ
What is logically centralized control? How can it be implemented?

Logically centralized control is the approach in which one logical controller computes and distributes the forwarding (flow) tables used by every router.

It is implemented with a remote SDN controller that talks to a local control agent (CA) in each router or switch through a protocol such as OpenFlow; the CAs have no routing logic and only carry out the controller's instructions.

"Logically" centralized means it behaves like one controller, but for performance, scalability and fault tolerance it is usually a set of distributed servers that share a consistent view of the network state.

Section 2: Routing Algorithms

36 leading questions · Textbook Section 5.2, Routing Algorithms,
U5 · S2 · Q1LQ
What are the goals of a routing algorithm?

The goal of a routing algorithm is to determine good paths (routes) from senders to receivers through the network of routers, where "good" usually means least cost.

A good algorithm should also be correct (loop-free), converge quickly after changes, be robust to failures, scale to large networks, keep message and computation overhead low, and respect policy where required (for example, between ISPs).

U5 · S2 · Q2LQ
What is the role of the network layer routing protocol?

The network-layer routing protocol is the means by which routers exchange the information (link costs, distance vectors, reachability) that the routing algorithm needs.

Its role is to let each router (or a central controller) work out routes and build the forwarding tables that the data plane uses to forward datagrams end to end.

U5 · S2 · Q3LQ
What is the least cost path between two hosts in a network? What condition does such a path have to meet?

Given a graph with a cost on each edge, the cost of a path \((x_1,x_2,\dots,x_p)\) is the sum of its edge costs \(c(x_1,x_2)+\dots+c(x_{p-1},x_p)\), and the least-cost path is the path between source and destination with the smallest such sum.

Condition: every consecutive pair of nodes on the path must be joined by an edge in the graph, and no other path between the two nodes may have a smaller total cost (there can be ties).

U5 · S2 · Q4LQ
What is the shortest path between two hosts in a network?

The shortest path is the path between source and destination that uses the smallest number of links (hops).

It is the least-cost path in the special case where every link has the same cost (for example cost 1 per hop).

U5 · S2 · Q5LQ
When can the shortest path and the least cost path be identical?

The shortest path and the least-cost path are identical when all links in the network have the same cost, because then the total cost is just a constant times the number of hops.

They can also coincide by chance in a particular graph, but equal link costs is the general condition.

U5 · S2 · Q6LQ
What are routing algorithms used for?

Routing algorithms are used to compute the paths that datagrams take from source to destination, and from those paths to build the forwarding tables in routers (or flow tables in SDN switches).

They let the network pick least-cost routes, adapt when links fail or costs change, and enforce policies about which routes may be used.

U5 · S2 · Q7LQ
How is a graph used to formulate routing problems?

The network is modelled as a graph \(G=(N,E)\): the nodes \(N\) are routers and the edges \(E\) are the physical links between them.

Each edge \((x,y)\) has a cost \(c(x,y)\) that may reflect physical length, link speed, delay or monetary cost; \(c(x,y)=\infty\) if there is no edge.

Routing then becomes the problem of finding a least-cost path between pairs of nodes in this graph.

U5 · S2 · Q8LQ
What is a centralized routing algorithm?

A centralized (global) routing algorithm computes the least-cost path using complete, global knowledge of the network: the whole topology and all link costs are known as input before the calculation starts.

The calculation can run at one central site (an SDN controller) or be replicated in every router; link-state algorithms such as Dijkstra's are the standard example.

U5 · S2 · Q9LQ
What is a decentralized routing algorithm?

In a decentralized routing algorithm, the least-cost paths are computed in an iterative, distributed way: no node has complete information about all link costs.

Each node starts knowing only the costs of its own links, and through repeated exchange of information with its neighbours it gradually computes least-cost paths; the distance-vector algorithm is the standard example.

U5 · S2 · Q10LQ
What is a static routing algorithm?

In a static routing algorithm, routes change very slowly over time, often only as a result of human intervention, for example an administrator editing a forwarding table by hand.

It is simple and predictable but does not react to failures or congestion.

U5 · S2 · Q11LQ
What is a dynamic routing algorithm?

A dynamic routing algorithm changes the routing paths as the network traffic loads or topology change.

It can run periodically or in direct response to link-cost or topology changes, which makes it responsive, but it is more prone to routing loops and route oscillation.

U5 · S2 · Q12LQ
What is a load-sensitive routing algorithm?

In a load-sensitive routing algorithm, link costs vary dynamically to reflect the current level of congestion in the underlying link, so a highly congested link gets a high cost and routing steers traffic around it.

Today's Internet routing algorithms, such as the Routing Information Protocol (RIP), Open Shortest Path First (OSPF) and the Border Gateway Protocol (BGP), are load-insensitive because load-sensitive costs can cause routes to oscillate.

U5 · S2 · Q13LQ
What is a global routing algorithm for a network?

A global routing algorithm computes the least-cost path between a source and a destination using complete, global knowledge of the network: every node knows the full topology and every link cost before the computation starts.

That knowledge is gathered first (by a link-state broadcast), and the computation, such as Dijkstra's algorithm, can then run at one site or be replicated at every router.

U5 · S2 · Q14LQ
Why is a global routing algorithm referred to as a link-state algorithm?

It is called a link-state (LS) algorithm because each node must know the state, that is the cost, of every link in the network.

To get this, every node floods link-state packets carrying the identities and costs of its attached links to all other nodes (a link-state broadcast), so every node ends up with an identical, complete map on which it runs Dijkstra's algorithm.

U5 · S2 · Q15LQ
What are the differences between distance-vector algorithms and link-state algorithms?
Link state (LS), e.g. Dijkstra / Open Shortest Path First (OSPF)Distance vector (DV), e.g. Bellman-Ford / Routing Information Protocol (RIP)
KnowledgeGlobal: every node learns the whole topology and all link costs.Decentralized: a node knows only its own link costs and its neighbours' distance vectors.
Message complexityEach node floods its link costs to all nodes: O(|N||E|) messages, and every change must be flooded.Messages go only between neighbours, and only when a vector changes.
Speed of convergenceO(|N|2) computation, converges quickly (oscillation possible with load-sensitive costs).Can converge slowly, may have routing loops while converging and the count-to-infinity problem.
RobustnessA router that advertises a wrong link cost corrupts only that link's cost; each node computes its own table.A wrong distance advertised by one router spreads to every router (errors propagate).
OperationEach node computes all routes by itself.Iterative, asynchronous and distributed: each node recomputes from neighbours' results.
U5 · S2 · Q16LQ
How are link cost changes and link failures handled in the distance-vector algorithm?

When a node detects that the cost of an attached link has changed, it recomputes its distance vector with the Bellman-Ford equation \(D_x(y)=\min_v\{c(x,v)+D_v(y)\}\), and if its vector changed it sends the new vector to its neighbours, who repeat the process.

Good news travels fast: a cost decrease spreads in a few iterations.

Bad news travels slowly: after a cost increase or a link failure, a node may believe a neighbour's old distance that actually routes back through itself, so a routing loop forms and the two nodes raise their costs a little at a time (the count-to-infinity problem).

Poisoned reverse helps: if z routes to x through y, z tells y that its distance to x is infinity, which removes loops between two neighbours, but not loops involving three or more nodes.

A failed link is treated as a cost of infinity; protocols such as the Routing Information Protocol (RIP) cap infinity at 16 hops so counting stops.

U5 · S2 · Q17LQ
What is hot potato routing?

Hot potato routing is the inter-AS rule in which a router chooses, among the gateways that can reach a destination outside its autonomous system (AS), the gateway with the least intra-AS cost from itself.

The idea is to get the packet out of its own AS as quickly (as cheaply) as possible, "like a hot potato", without caring about the cost of the rest of the path outside the AS.

In the Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) route selection it is the third rule, applied after local preference and shortest AS-PATH.

U5 · S2 · Q18LQ
What is shortest path first routing?

Shortest path first (SPF) routing sends each packet along the least-cost path computed by Dijkstra's algorithm, which permanently adds to the solved set the not-yet-solved node with the smallest current distance first, then updates its neighbours.

Link-state protocols use it, which is why Open Shortest Path First (OSPF) is called "Open Shortest Path First".

U5 · S2 · Q19LQ
What is least loaded path routing?

Least loaded path routing (from circuit-switched telephone networks) chooses, among the candidate paths between two switches, the path whose most heavily loaded link has the most spare capacity.

The load of a path is defined by its bottleneck link, so the algorithm picks the path with the lightest bottleneck, which spreads calls and lowers blocking.

U5 · S2 · Q20LQ
What is maximum free circuit routing?

Maximum free circuit routing, used for circuit-switched networks, picks the path that has the greatest number of free circuits.

The number of free circuits on a path is the sum of the free circuits on all its links, so this rule favours paths with lots of spare capacity overall (unlike least-loaded routing, which looks only at the bottleneck link).

U5 · S2 · Q21LQ
What are gateway routers?

Gateway routers are the routers at the edge of an autonomous system (AS) that connect directly to one or more routers in other ASs.

They run the external part of the Border Gateway Protocol (eBGP) with routers in neighbouring ASs to exchange reachability information, and they pass it to the routers inside their own AS with internal BGP (iBGP).

U5 · S2 · Q22LQ
What is the intra-autonomous routing protocol?

An intra-AS routing protocol (also called an interior gateway protocol) determines how packets are routed between source and destination routers inside one autonomous system (AS).

All routers in the AS run the same one, chosen by the AS's administrator, and it optimizes for performance (least cost) because there are no policy concerns inside a single administration.

Examples are Open Shortest Path First (OSPF) and IS-IS (link-state), and the Routing Information Protocol (RIP) and Cisco's Enhanced Interior Gateway Routing Protocol (EIGRP) (distance-vector family).

U5 · S2 · Q23LQ
Based on the IPv4 addressing scheme, what is the maximum number of hosts that can be addressed?

An IPv4 address is 32 bits long, so there are at most \(2^{32}=4{,}294{,}967{,}296\) (about 4.3 billion) addresses.

The usable number of hosts is smaller, because some blocks are reserved (private, multicast, loopback, experimental) and every subnet loses its network and broadcast addresses; network address translation (NAT) lets many more hosts share the space.

U5 · S2 · Q24LQ
What is a network address? What is a network mask?

A network (subnet) address is the common prefix that all interfaces on one subnet share, written with the host bits set to 0, for example 223.1.1.0/24.

A network mask (subnet mask) is a 32-bit value with 1s in the prefix positions and 0s in the host positions, such as 255.255.255.0 for /24.

ANDing an IP address with the mask gives the network address, which is how a router or host decides whether a destination is on its own subnet.

U5 · S2 · Q25LQ
What is classful addressing? What are the differences between class A, B, C, and D addresses?

Classful addressing was the original IPv4 scheme in which the network part of an address had a fixed length of 8, 16 or 24 bits, determined by the first bits of the address.

ClassLeading bitsFirst octetNetwork / host splitUse
A00-1278 / 24 bits (about 16.7 million hosts)Very large networks
B10128-19116 / 16 bits (65,534 hosts)Medium networks
C110192-22324 / 8 bits (254 hosts)Small networks
D1110224-239No split; 28-bit group IDMulticast

(Class E, starting 1111, was reserved for experiments.)

U5 · S2 · Q26LQ
Why are the four address classes no longer formally part of the IP addressing architecture?

The classes were dropped because they wasted address space and grew the routing tables.

A class C block (254 hosts) was too small for most organizations and a class B block (65,534 hosts) far too large, so an organization with 2,000 hosts got a class B and left most of it unused, which rapidly exhausted the class B space.

Classless Interdomain Routing (CIDR) replaced them in 1993 with prefixes of any length, so blocks can be sized to need and aggregated.

U5 · S2 · Q27LQ
What is Classless Interdomain Routing (CIDR)?

Classless Interdomain Routing (CIDR) is the Internet's address assignment strategy in which the network portion of an IP address can be any length.

An address is written a.b.c.d/x, where the x most significant bits are the prefix (network part) and the rest identify hosts or further subnets.

CIDR lets organizations get blocks sized to their needs and lets ISPs aggregate many customers' prefixes into one advertisement, and routers forward with longest prefix matching.

U5 · S2 · Q28LQ
What is subnetting?

Subnetting is dividing one block of IP addresses into smaller subnetworks by taking some of the host bits and using them as extra prefix bits.

A subnet is the set of interfaces that can reach each other without going through a router, and all of them share the same subnet prefix, e.g. 223.1.1.0/24.

For example, 200.23.16.0/20 can be split into eight /23 subnets for different departments.

U5 · S2 · Q29LQ
What is the Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol (DHCP)?

The Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol (DHCP) lets a host obtain an IP address automatically, together with its subnet mask, its first-hop (default gateway) router and its local DNS server address.

It is a plug-and-play, client-server protocol running over UDP (ports 67 and 68) in four steps: DHCP discover (broadcast), DHCP offer, DHCP request and DHCP ACK.

Addresses are leased for a limited time, so they can be reused when hosts leave.

U5 · S2 · Q30LQ
What is route aggregation or route summarization and how does it work?

Route aggregation (route summarization) is the use of one prefix to advertise many smaller networks.

An ISP that gives its customers contiguous blocks, for example eight /23 blocks inside 200.23.16.0/20, advertises only "send me anything matching 200.23.16.0/20" to the rest of the Internet.

This keeps routing tables small and hides internal changes; if a customer's block lies elsewhere, the more specific prefix is also advertised and longest prefix matching picks it.

U5 · S2 · Q31LQ
How does an ISP get a block of addresses?

An ISP obtains a block of addresses from a regional Internet registry (RIR) such as ARIN in North America, which in turn gets its space from the Internet Assigned Numbers Authority (IANA) under ICANN.

A smaller ISP may instead get a block carved out of its upstream provider's larger allocated block, and it then divides its block among its customers.

U5 · S2 · Q32LQ
Which organization is responsible for the management of IP addresses?

Global IP address space is managed by the Internet Corporation for Assigned Names and Numbers (ICANN), which runs the Internet Assigned Numbers Authority (IANA) function.

ICANN/IANA allocates blocks to the regional Internet registries (ARIN, RIPE NCC, APNIC, LACNIC, AFRINIC), which allocate them to ISPs and large organizations; ICANN also manages the DNS root servers and domain names.

U5 · S2 · Q33LQ
How is an IP datagram formatted?

An IPv4 datagram has a header (20 bytes without options) followed by the payload.

  • Version (4 bits), header length (4 bits), type of service (8 bits), total datagram length (16 bits).
  • Identifier (16 bits), flags (3 bits) and fragment offset (13 bits), used for fragmentation and reassembly.
  • Time-to-live (TTL, 8 bits, decremented at each router), upper-layer protocol (8 bits, e.g. 6 = TCP, 17 = UDP), header checksum (16 bits).
  • 32-bit source IP address and 32-bit destination IP address.
  • Options (rarely used), then data, usually a TCP or UDP segment.
U5 · S2 · Q34LQ
How is an IP datagram transported from source to destination?

The source host builds an IP datagram with the destination address and sends it to its first-hop (default gateway) router.

Each router on the way looks up the destination address in its forwarding table using longest prefix matching, decrements the time-to-live, recomputes the header checksum, and forwards the datagram out of the matching interface, fragmenting it if the next link's maximum transmission unit (MTU) is too small.

This repeats hop by hop until the router attached to the destination subnet delivers the datagram over the link layer to the destination host, whose IP layer reassembles it if needed and passes the payload to the transport protocol named in the protocol field.

Delivery is best effort: datagrams can be lost, reordered or duplicated.

U5 · S2 · Q35LQ
What is the maximum transfer unit (MTU)?

The maximum transfer (transmission) unit (MTU) is the largest amount of data that a link-layer frame can carry, and so the largest IP datagram that can cross that link; for Ethernet it is 1,500 bytes.

If a datagram is larger than the outgoing link's MTU, IPv4 fragments it into smaller datagrams, which are reassembled only at the destination; IPv6 does not fragment in routers and instead returns an ICMP "packet too big" message.

U5 · S2 · Q36LQ
What is the Internet Control Message Protocol (ICMP) used for?

The Internet Control Message Protocol (ICMP) is used by hosts and routers to report network-layer errors and to exchange network-layer information, for example "destination network unreachable", "TTL expired" or an echo request and echo reply.

ICMP messages are carried as the payload of IP datagrams (protocol number 1), so architecturally ICMP sits just above IP, but it is considered part of the network layer.

Each message has a type and a code field and carries the IP header plus the first 8 bytes of the datagram that caused the error, so the sender can tell which packet failed.

The ping program (echo request type 8, echo reply type 0) and traceroute (which relies on "TTL expired", type 11, and "port unreachable", type 3 code 3) are built on ICMP.

Section 3: Intra-AS Routing in the Internet: OSPF

14 leading questions · Textbook Section 5.3, Intra-AS Routing in the Internet: OSPF,
U5 · S3 · Q1LQ
What is an autonomous system?

An autonomous system (AS) is a group of routers under the same administrative control, typically one ISP or one company network, that run the same intra-AS routing protocol and share routing information among themselves.

Each AS is identified by a globally unique AS number (ASN) assigned by a regional Internet registry, and to the rest of the Internet it appears as one routing entity that is reached through its gateway routers.

U5 · S3 · Q2LQ
By organizing routers on a large-scale network, such as the Internet, into autonomous systems, what problems are solved?

Organizing routers into autonomous systems (ASs) solves two problems of flat routing.

  • Scale: with hundreds of millions of routers, storing and exchanging routes to every destination would overwhelm memory and bandwidth, and link-state broadcasts or distance-vector iterations would never converge. With ASs, routers only track routes inside their AS plus routes to other ASs.
  • Administrative autonomy: each ISP or company can run its own network as it wishes, choose its own routing protocol and hide its internal organization from outsiders.
U5 · S3 · Q3LQ
What is an intra-autonomous system routing protocol?

An intra-AS routing protocol (also called an interior gateway protocol) determines how packets are routed between source and destination routers inside one autonomous system (AS).

All routers in the AS run the same one, chosen by the AS's administrator, and it optimizes for performance (least cost) because there are no policy concerns inside a single administration.

Examples are Open Shortest Path First (OSPF) and IS-IS (link-state), and the Routing Information Protocol (RIP) and Cisco's Enhanced Interior Gateway Routing Protocol (EIGRP) (distance-vector family).

U5 · S3 · Q4LQ
Why is intra-autonomous system routing needed?

Intra-AS routing is needed so that the routers inside one autonomous system (AS) can find least-cost paths to each other and to the AS's gateway routers.

Inter-AS routing (BGP) only says which gateway to use to leave the AS; the intra-AS protocol is what carries packets from any internal router to that gateway and from an arriving gateway to the internal destination.

It also lets each administrator pick a protocol tuned for performance within its own network.

U5 · S3 · Q5LQ
What is the Internet control message protocol (ICMP) used for?

The Internet Control Message Protocol (ICMP) is used by hosts and routers to report network-layer errors and to exchange network-layer information, for example "destination network unreachable", "TTL expired" or an echo request and echo reply.

ICMP messages are carried as the payload of IP datagrams (protocol number 1), so architecturally ICMP sits just above IP, but it is considered part of the network layer.

Each message has a type and a code field and carries the IP header plus the first 8 bytes of the datagram that caused the error, so the sender can tell which packet failed.

The ping program (echo request type 8, echo reply type 0) and traceroute (which relies on "TTL expired", type 11, and "port unreachable", type 3 code 3) are built on ICMP.

U5 · S3 · Q6LQ
What is an autonomous system (AS)?

An autonomous system (AS) is a group of routers under the same administrative control, typically one ISP or one company network, that run the same intra-AS routing protocol and share routing information among themselves.

Each AS is identified by a globally unique AS number (ASN) assigned by a regional Internet registry, and to the rest of the Internet it appears as one routing entity that is reached through its gateway routers.

U5 · S3 · Q7LQ
What is an intra-AS routing protocol used for?

An intra-AS routing protocol (also called an interior gateway protocol) determines how packets are routed between source and destination routers inside one autonomous system (AS).

All routers in the AS run the same one, chosen by the AS's administrator, and it optimizes for performance (least cost) because there are no policy concerns inside a single administration.

Examples are Open Shortest Path First (OSPF) and IS-IS (link-state), and the Routing Information Protocol (RIP) and Cisco's Enhanced Interior Gateway Routing Protocol (EIGRP) (distance-vector family).

U5 · S3 · Q8LQ
What is the routing information protocol (RIP) used for? How does it work?

The Routing Information Protocol (RIP) is an intra-AS (interior) routing protocol based on the distance-vector algorithm.

  • The cost metric is hop count: each link costs 1, and the maximum path cost is 15, with 16 meaning infinity, which limits RIP to small ASs.
  • Neighbouring routers exchange distance vectors (RIP response messages, also called advertisements, listing up to 25 destination subnets) about every 30 seconds.
  • Each router updates its routing table with the Bellman-Ford equation; if it hears nothing from a neighbour for 180 seconds, that neighbour is considered dead, and the change is propagated.
  • Poisoned reverse is used to limit loops.
U5 · S3 · Q9LQ
How is RIP implemented in UNIX?

In UNIX, RIP is implemented as an application-level process (daemon) called routed ("route-dee").

It sends and receives RIP advertisements in UDP segments on port 520, so a network-layer protocol is carried over a transport protocol, and it updates the kernel's forwarding table, which the IP layer in the kernel uses to forward packets.

U5 · S3 · Q10LQ
What is the open shortest path first protocol (OSPF) used for? How does it work?

Open Shortest Path First (OSPF) is a link-state intra-AS routing protocol, widely used by ISPs.

  • Each router floods link-state advertisements describing its attached links and their costs (set by the administrator) to all routers in the AS, whenever a link changes and periodically (at least every 30 minutes).
  • Every router thus has the complete topology and runs Dijkstra's algorithm to build a shortest-path tree to all subnets, with itself as root.
  • Messages are carried directly in IP (protocol 89), with OSPF's own reliable transfer and hello messages to check neighbours.
U5 · S3 · Q11LQ
What are the differences between RIP and OSPF?
Routing Information Protocol (RIP)Open Shortest Path First (OSPF)
AlgorithmDistance vector (Bellman-Ford)Link state (Dijkstra)
KnowledgeDistances heard from neighboursFull topology of the AS (or area)
MetricHop count, max 15Administrator-set link weights
UpdatesVectors to neighbours every 30 sFlooded to all routers on change and at least every 30 min
TransportUDP port 520Directly in IP (protocol 89)
ConvergenceSlow, count-to-infinity possibleFast, no count-to-infinity
Scale and extrasSmall networks; no areasLarge networks with hierarchy (areas), authentication, multiple equal-cost paths
U5 · S3 · Q12LQ
What advantages are embodied in OSPF Protocol?
  • Security: OSPF messages can be authenticated (simple password or MD5), so outsiders cannot inject false routes.
  • Multiple same-cost paths: traffic can be split over several equal-cost paths instead of one.
  • Integrated unicast and multicast support (MOSPF uses the same link-state database).
  • Hierarchy within one AS: the AS can be divided into areas with a backbone, which limits flooding and scales to large networks.
  • Fast, loop-free convergence because every router has the full map.
U5 · S3 · Q13LQ
What are the different uses of internal routers, area border routers, and backbone routers?

In a hierarchical OSPF autonomous system divided into areas:

  • Internal routers lie entirely inside one non-backbone area and perform only intra-area routing.
  • Area border routers belong to both an area and the backbone; they summarize their area's routes and advertise them to other areas, and route packets between areas.
  • Backbone routers run OSPF inside the backbone area, which carries all inter-area traffic (an area border router is also a backbone router).
  • Boundary routers exchange routing information with routers in other ASs (for example via BGP).
U5 · S3 · Q14LQ
What is enhanced internal gateway routing protocol (EIGR), and how does it work?

The Enhanced Interior Gateway Routing Protocol (EIGRP) is Cisco's intra-AS routing protocol, a successor to IGRP, built on a distance-vector approach.

It uses a composite metric (bandwidth and delay, optionally load and reliability) and the Diffusing Update Algorithm (DUAL), which keeps loop-free backup routes ("feasible successors") and coordinates route changes so that no routing loops form even temporarily.

Routers discover neighbours with hello messages and send only partial, bounded updates when something changes, rather than periodic full tables, so it converges quickly with little overhead.

Section 4: Routing Among the ISPs: BGP

18 leading questions · Textbook Section 5.4, Routing Among the ISPs: BGP,
U5 · S4 · Q1LQ
How are packets routed from one AS to another?

Packets are routed between autonomous systems (ASs) by the Border Gateway Protocol (BGP).

Gateway routers learn, through external BGP (eBGP) sessions with neighbouring ASs, which destination prefixes are reachable through them and with which AS-PATH, and they spread this to all routers in their own AS through internal BGP (iBGP).

Each router then selects a route (local preference, shortest AS-PATH, hot potato), uses the intra-AS protocol to find the interface toward the chosen gateway, and installs (prefix, interface) in its forwarding table, so the packet crosses the AS and leaves for the next AS until it reaches the destination AS.

U5 · S4 · Q2LQ
What is the border gateway protocol (BGP) used for?

The Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) is the inter-AS routing protocol of the Internet, the "glue" that holds the Internet's thousands of ISPs together.

It lets each subnet advertise its existence (prefix) to the rest of the Internet, lets each router obtain reachability information for prefixes from neighbouring ASs, and lets each router determine the "best" route to each prefix based on reachability and on policy.

It is a decentralized, asynchronous, path-vector protocol running over the Transmission Control Protocol (TCP), port 179.

U5 · S4 · Q3LQ
In inter-AS routing, what information does a border gateway routing protocol need to provide to a router?

The Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) gives each router two things.

  • Reachability: which destination prefixes in other ASs can be reached through each neighbouring AS, obtained by eBGP and spread inside the AS by iBGP.
  • Route attributes to choose the best route: the AS-PATH (list of ASs the advertisement passed through, also used to detect loops), the NEXT-HOP (IP address of the router interface that begins the AS-PATH), local preference and others.

From these the router decides which gateway to use for each prefix and installs the entry in its forwarding table.

U5 · S4 · Q4LQ
What is the best route between autonomous systems?

There is no single "shortest" best route between autonomous systems (ASs); the Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) chooses its best route by policy and attributes.

A router prefers the route with the highest local preference (set by the AS's policy, e.g. prefer customers over peers over providers); among those, the shortest AS-PATH; then the closest NEXT-HOP router (hot potato routing); then further tie-breakers such as BGP router IDs.

So the best route is the policy-preferred route, and only when policy does not decide is it the one through the fewest ASs.

U5 · S4 · Q5LQ
What are the four types of messages defined by BGP?

Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) peers exchange four message types over a Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) connection (port 179):

  • OPEN: opens the BGP session and authenticates the sender, carrying its AS number and parameters.
  • UPDATE: advertises a new path (prefix plus attributes) or withdraws a path that is no longer valid.
  • KEEPALIVE: keeps the session alive without updates, and acknowledges an OPEN.
  • NOTIFICATION: reports an error in a previous message, and is used to close a session.
U5 · S4 · Q6LQ
What are the differences between IBGP and EBGP?
External BGP (eBGP)Internal BGP (iBGP)
BetweenRouters in two different ASs (gateway to gateway)Routers in the same AS
PurposeLearn and advertise reachability across AS boundariesSpread routes learned by eBGP to all routers in the AS
AS-PATHThe sending AS prepends its ASNUnchanged; no ASN added
TopologyUsually directly connected neighboursLogical sessions, usually a full mesh, not necessarily physical links

Both are the same BGP protocol over TCP; they differ only in who the peers are.

U5 · S4 · Q7LQ
Why are there different inter-AS and intra-AS routing protocols?
  • Policy: between autonomous systems (ASs), policy (who may carry whose traffic, business relationships) dominates and must be expressible; inside an AS, one administrator controls everything, so policy matters little.
  • Scale: inter-AS routing must handle hundreds of thousands of prefixes and many ASs; hierarchical routing lets intra-AS routing stay small because external destinations are handled by the Border Gateway Protocol (BGP).
  • Performance: inside an AS the focus can be on least-cost performance, while between ASs policy often overrides performance.
U5 · S4 · Q8LQ
What is a global routing algorithm for a network?

A global routing algorithm computes the least-cost path between a source and a destination using complete, global knowledge of the network: every node knows the full topology and every link cost before the computation starts.

That knowledge is gathered first (by a link-state broadcast), and the computation, such as Dijkstra's algorithm, can then run at one site or be replicated at every router.

U5 · S4 · Q9LQ
Why is a global routing algorithm referred to as a link-state algorithm?

It is called a link-state (LS) algorithm because each node must know the state, that is the cost, of every link in the network.

To get this, every node floods link-state packets carrying the identities and costs of its attached links to all other nodes (a link-state broadcast), so every node ends up with an identical, complete map on which it runs Dijkstra's algorithm.

U5 · S4 · Q10LQ
What are the differences between distance-vector algorithms and link-state algorithms?
Link state (LS), e.g. Dijkstra / Open Shortest Path First (OSPF)Distance vector (DV), e.g. Bellman-Ford / Routing Information Protocol (RIP)
KnowledgeGlobal: every node learns the whole topology and all link costs.Decentralized: a node knows only its own link costs and its neighbours' distance vectors.
Message complexityEach node floods its link costs to all nodes: O(|N||E|) messages, and every change must be flooded.Messages go only between neighbours, and only when a vector changes.
Speed of convergenceO(|N|2) computation, converges quickly (oscillation possible with load-sensitive costs).Can converge slowly, may have routing loops while converging and the count-to-infinity problem.
RobustnessA router that advertises a wrong link cost corrupts only that link's cost; each node computes its own table.A wrong distance advertised by one router spreads to every router (errors propagate).
OperationEach node computes all routes by itself.Iterative, asynchronous and distributed: each node recomputes from neighbours' results.
U5 · S4 · Q11LQ
How are link cost changes and link failures handled in the distance-vector algorithm?

When a node detects that the cost of an attached link has changed, it recomputes its distance vector with the Bellman-Ford equation \(D_x(y)=\min_v\{c(x,v)+D_v(y)\}\), and if its vector changed it sends the new vector to its neighbours, who repeat the process.

Good news travels fast: a cost decrease spreads in a few iterations.

Bad news travels slowly: after a cost increase or a link failure, a node may believe a neighbour's old distance that actually routes back through itself, so a routing loop forms and the two nodes raise their costs a little at a time (the count-to-infinity problem).

Poisoned reverse helps: if z routes to x through y, z tells y that its distance to x is infinity, which removes loops between two neighbours, but not loops involving three or more nodes.

A failed link is treated as a cost of infinity; protocols such as the Routing Information Protocol (RIP) cap infinity at 16 hops so counting stops.

U5 · S4 · Q12LQ
What is hot potato routing?

Hot potato routing is the inter-AS rule in which a router chooses, among the gateways that can reach a destination outside its autonomous system (AS), the gateway with the least intra-AS cost from itself.

The idea is to get the packet out of its own AS as quickly (as cheaply) as possible, "like a hot potato", without caring about the cost of the rest of the path outside the AS.

In the Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) route selection it is the third rule, applied after local preference and shortest AS-PATH.

U5 · S4 · Q13LQ
How does BGP select a route?

When a router has two or more routes to the same prefix, the Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) applies these elimination rules in order until one route remains:

  1. Highest local preference attribute (set by the AS's policy).
  2. Shortest AS-PATH.
  3. Closest NEXT-HOP router, that is the least intra-AS cost to the gateway (hot potato routing).
  4. Further tie-breakers, for example the lowest BGP router identifier.
U5 · S4 · Q14LQ
What is head of line (HOL) blocking?

Head-of-the-line (HOL) blocking happens in a router's input-queued switch fabric: a queued packet at an input port must wait for transfer through the fabric, even though its own output port is free, because the packet at the head of the same input queue is blocked waiting for a different, busy output port.

Because of HOL blocking, input queues can grow without bound (the switch saturates at about 58% of input link capacity under uniform random traffic) once the arrival rate passes that limit.

U5 · S4 · Q15LQ
What was the main initiative for the IETF to develop Internet protocol version 6 (IPv6)?

The main motivation for the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF) was that the 32-bit IPv4 address space was running out, because of the rapid growth of the Internet and the way addresses were assigned.

The IETF used the opportunity to also streamline the header for faster router processing and to add flow labelling for better quality-of-service handling.

U5 · S4 · Q16LQ
What packet format is used by IPv6? What are the main changes compared to IPv4?

IPv6 uses a fixed 40-byte header: version, traffic class (8 bits), flow label (20 bits), payload length (16 bits), next header (like IPv4's protocol field), hop limit (like TTL), and 128-bit source and destination addresses.

Main changes from IPv4:

  • Addresses grow from 32 to 128 bits, and an anycast address type is added.
  • Fixed-length 40-byte header; options are moved to optional extension headers pointed to by "next header".
  • No fragmentation or reassembly in routers: a router that cannot forward a too-large datagram drops it and sends an ICMPv6 "packet too big" message.
  • No header checksum, because the transport and link layers already check errors and recomputing it at every hop was costly.
  • A flow label to identify packets of the same flow for special handling.
U5 · S4 · Q17LQ
What is IP-anycast?

IP anycast is a service in which the same IP address (prefix) is announced from several different locations, and the network delivers each datagram to just one of them, normally the "closest" one according to routing.

It is implemented with BGP: each server site advertises the same prefix, and each router picks the best (usually nearest) route by the normal BGP selection rules.

It is used by DNS root servers and by content distribution networks to send clients to a nearby replica.

U5 · S4 · Q18LQ
What is an anycast address?

An anycast address is a single IP address that is assigned to several interfaces or hosts (usually in different places), such that a datagram sent to it is delivered to only one member of the group, typically the nearest according to the routing protocol.

IPv6 defines anycast as an explicit address type; in IPv4 it is achieved by announcing the same prefix from many sites with BGP.

Section 5: The SDN Control Plane

2 leading questions · Textbook Section 5.5, The SDN Control Plane,
U5 · S5 · Q1LQ
What is a software-defined network (SDN)?

A software-defined network (SDN) is a network in which the control plane is separated from the data plane and moved into software.

Its four key characteristics are: flow-based forwarding (match plus action on many header fields, installed in flow tables); separation of the data plane (simple packet switches) from the control plane (servers and software); network control functions that live outside the switches, in a logically centralized controller (network operating system) with a global view; and a programmable network, in which control applications such as routing, access control or load balancing call the controller's northbound API.

The controller programs switches through a southbound protocol such as OpenFlow.

U5 · S5 · Q2LQ
What benefits does SDN offer?
  • Programmability and innovation: operators can write and change network control applications in software instead of waiting for router vendors.
  • Centralized global view: routing, traffic engineering and access control can be computed with knowledge of the whole network, which is easier and less error-prone than coordinating distributed protocols.
  • Generalized forwarding: one device type can act as router, switch, firewall, NAT or load balancer by changing its flow table.
  • Unbundling: commodity "white-box" switches, an open controller and third-party apps lower cost and vendor lock-in.
  • Easier management and faster reaction to failures or policy changes, as in data centres and Google's B4 network.

Section 6: ICMP: The Internet Control Message Protocol

4 leading questions · Textbook Section 5.6, ICMP: The Internet Control Message Protocol,
U5 · S6 · Q1LQ
What is the Internet communication message protocol (ICMP)?

The Internet Control Message Protocol (ICMP), defined in RFC 792, is the network-layer protocol that hosts and routers use to communicate network-layer information to each other, mainly error reports.

ICMP messages are carried inside IP datagrams (protocol number 1) and contain a type field, a code field, and the header plus the first 8 bytes of the IP datagram that caused the message.

U5 · S6 · Q2LQ
What is ICMP used for?

The Internet Control Message Protocol (ICMP) is used by hosts and routers to report network-layer errors and to exchange network-layer information, for example "destination network unreachable", "TTL expired" or an echo request and echo reply.

ICMP messages are carried as the payload of IP datagrams (protocol number 1), so architecturally ICMP sits just above IP, but it is considered part of the network layer.

Each message has a type and a code field and carries the IP header plus the first 8 bytes of the datagram that caused the error, so the sender can tell which packet failed.

The ping program (echo request type 8, echo reply type 0) and traceroute (which relies on "TTL expired", type 11, and "port unreachable", type 3 code 3) are built on ICMP.

U5 · S6 · Q3LQ
How is ICMP used by the well-known ping program?

The ping program sends an ICMP echo request (type 8, code 0) to the target host.

The destination's operating system answers with an ICMP echo reply (type 0, code 0) carrying the same identifier, sequence number and data, so ping matches each reply to its request and reports reachability, round-trip time and packet loss.

Ping talks to the ICMP layer directly, so no application server is needed on the target.

U5 · S6 · Q4LQ
How can an ICMP source quench message be used by a router in network congestion control?

An ICMP source quench message (type 4, code 0) was designed for congestion control: a congested router that is dropping datagrams sends a source quench to the source host, telling it to reduce its sending rate.

It is rarely used in practice and was formally deprecated (RFC 6633), because TCP has its own end-to-end congestion control that infers congestion from loss, and sending extra messages during congestion adds load and is unfair and easy to abuse.

Section 7: Network Management and SNMP, NETCONF/YANG

18 leading questions · Textbook Section 5.7, Network Management and SNMP, NETCONFIG/YANG,
U5 · S7 · Q1LQ
What is network management?

Network management includes the deployment, integration and coordination of the hardware, software and human elements to monitor, test, poll, configure, analyze, evaluate and control the network and element resources.

The goal is to meet the real-time, operational performance and quality-of-service requirements at a reasonable cost.

U5 · S7 · Q2LQ
What are the responsibilities of a network administrator?

A network administrator keeps the network running and meeting its requirements, for example:

  • Configuring devices and keeping configurations consistent (configuration management).
  • Detecting, diagnosing and fixing failures of interfaces, hosts and links (fault management).
  • Monitoring traffic, utilization and service-level agreements to plan capacity and keep performance up (performance management).
  • Tracking resource use per user or department (accounting management).
  • Controlling access and detecting intrusions (security management).
U5 · S7 · Q3LQ
What are the potential benefits of network management tools?
  • Detecting failure of an interface card at a host or router before users complain.
  • Host monitoring: checking that hosts are up and operational.
  • Monitoring traffic to aid resource deployment and capacity planning.
  • Detecting rapid changes in routing tables, which may indicate instability or misconfiguration.
  • Monitoring service-level agreements (SLAs).
  • Intrusion detection, by noticing unusual traffic.
  • Automating configuration of many devices, which reduces human error.
U5 · S7 · Q4LQ
What devices can be managed on a network?

A managed device is any network equipment that contains managed objects, for example hosts, routers, switches, middleboxes such as firewalls and NAT boxes, modems, printers, load balancers, wireless access points and the links between them.

Each managed device runs a network management agent that the managing server can query and configure.

U5 · S7 · Q5LQ
What is a service-level agreement (SLA)?

A service-level agreement (SLA) is a contract that defines specific performance metrics and acceptable levels of network-provider performance with respect to those metrics, for example availability (99.99%), maximum delay, throughput or packet loss.

Network management tools monitor whether the provider is meeting its SLA.

U5 · S7 · Q6LQ
What five areas of network management are defined by the ISO network management mode? What are the principal components of network management architecture?

The International Organization for Standardization (ISO) network management model defines five areas (FCAPS):

  • Fault management: detect, log and respond to fault conditions.
  • Configuration management: track which devices are on the network and their hardware and software configurations.
  • Accounting management: record and control users' and devices' access to network resources.
  • Performance management: quantify, measure, report and control utilization, throughput and delay.
  • Security management: control access to resources according to a policy.

The principal components of the network management architecture are: the managing server (an application, often with a human in the loop, that controls collection, processing and analysis of management information); managed devices containing managed objects; the management information base (MIB) holding those objects' data; a network management agent in each device; and a network management protocol (such as SNMP or NETCONF) between server and agents.

U5 · S7 · Q7LQ
What is the OSI CMISE/CMIP?

The Common Management Information Service Element (CMISE) and Common Management Information Protocol (CMIP) are the OSI (ISO) network management standards.

CMISE defines the management services (get, set, action, create, delete, event report) and CMIP is the protocol that carries them between manager and agent, using an object-oriented information model.

They are more powerful but much more complex than SNMP, and were developed slowly, so they saw little use outside telecommunications networks, while SNMP became the Internet standard.

U5 · S7 · Q8LQ
What is the Internet simple network management protocol (SNMP)?

The Simple Network Management Protocol (SNMP) is the Internet's application-layer protocol for conveying network management control and information messages between a managing server and an agent running on a managed device.

It is used mainly in request-response mode (the server reads or sets MIB object values in the agent) and also lets agents send unsolicited trap messages.

SNMP messages (PDUs) are usually carried over UDP (port 161 for requests, 162 for traps).

U5 · S7 · Q9LQ
What is the Internet management framework?

The Internet-standard management framework has four parts:

  • Definitions of network management objects, the managed objects, gathered into the management information base (MIB).
  • A data definition language, the structure of management information (SMI), that defines data types, the object model and rules for writing and revising management information.
  • A protocol, the Simple Network Management Protocol (SNMP), to carry information and commands between the managing entity and agents.
  • Security and administration capabilities, added mainly in SNMPv3.
U5 · S7 · Q10LQ
What is the structure of management information (SMI)?

The structure of management information (SMI) is the data definition language used to define the managed objects in a management information base (MIB).

It is based on ASN.1 and defines the basic data types, the object model, and the rules for writing and revising management information, so that every object has a well-defined syntax and meaning independent of the device or vendor.

U5 · S7 · Q11LQ
How is management information defined in SMI?

SMI defines management information as named objects.

Each managed object is declared with the OBJECT-TYPE construct, which states its data type (SYNTAX), its access rights (MAX-ACCESS, e.g. read-only or read-write), its status (current, deprecated, obsolete), a text DESCRIPTION, and its unique object identifier in the ASN.1 object-identifier tree (for example 1.3.6.1.2.1 for the standard MIB-2).

Related objects are grouped into a MIB module with the MODULE-IDENTITY construct.

U5 · S7 · Q12LQ
What language constructs does SMI provide?
  • Base data types (eleven in SMIv2): INTEGER, Integer32, Unsigned32, OCTET STRING, OBJECT IDENTIFIER, IPaddress, Counter32, Counter64, Gauge32, TimeTicks and Opaque.
  • OBJECT-TYPE: defines a managed object, its data type, status and semantics.
  • MODULE-IDENTITY: groups related objects into a MIB module, with contact and revision information.
  • NOTIFICATION-TYPE: defines the trap/notification messages an agent can send.
  • OBJECT-GROUP and conformance constructs, and higher-level structures such as tables (SEQUENCE OF rows).
U5 · S7 · Q13LQ
What is a management information base (MIB)?

A management information base (MIB) is the collection of managed objects whose values together reflect the current status of the network, for example the number of IP datagrams discarded at a router or the status of an interface.

Related objects are grouped into MIB modules, each object is named by an object identifier in a global tree, and the managing server reads and writes these objects through the agent using SNMP.

U5 · S7 · Q14LQ
What is the request response mode of SNMP?

In request-response mode, the managing server sends an SNMP request to an agent in a managed device, and the agent performs the operation and returns a Response PDU.

  • GetRequest, GetNextRequest and GetBulkRequest retrieve MIB object values (GetNext walks a table; GetBulk fetches large blocks).
  • SetRequest sets MIB object values, for configuration.
  • InformRequest passes MIB values between managers.

Each request carries a request ID; because SNMP usually runs over UDP, the server retransmits when no response arrives within a timeout.

U5 · S7 · Q15LQ
What is a trap message?

A trap message (SNMPv2-Trap PDU) is an unsolicited message that an agent sends to the managing server to report an exceptional event that changed MIB object values, for example a link going down or up, a cold or warm restart, or an authentication failure.

It lets the server learn about problems immediately without polling; it is not acknowledged (an InformRequest is the acknowledged alternative).

U5 · S7 · Q16LQ
What are SNMP applications?

In the SNMP architecture, an SNMP entity contains an engine (dispatcher, message processing subsystem, security subsystem, access control) plus applications.

  • Command generator: generates GetRequest and SetRequest PDUs and handles responses (in the managing server).
  • Command responder: receives and processes requests and returns responses (in the agent).
  • Notification originator: generates trap PDUs.
  • Notification receiver: accepts and processes traps.
  • Proxy forwarder: forwards requests, notifications and responses between entities.
U5 · S7 · Q17LQ
What does SNMPv3 provide?

SNMPv3 adds security and administration to SNMP.

  • User-based security: encryption (privacy) of PDUs, and authentication and integrity checks with a keyed hash so a message cannot be forged or altered.
  • Protection against playback (replay) attacks with nonces/timeliness values.
  • View-based access control: which users may read or write which MIB objects.
  • Remote configuration of these security settings.
U5 · S7 · Q18LQ
What is ASN.1 and what does it provide?

Abstract Syntax Notation One (ASN.1) is an ISO standard language for describing data types and values independently of any machine, used by SMI to define MIB objects.

It provides presentation-layer services: together with the Basic Encoding Rules (BER) it specifies exactly how each value is encoded for transmission, as a type-length-value (TLV) triple, so that machines with different internal representations (byte order, word size) can interpret the bytes identically.

Chapter 5 problems

13 of 14 assigned problems fit a 20-minute exam slot
LEFT OUT (1) - WHY
  • P4 - Six full Dijkstra tables (sources t, u, v, w, y, z) take well over 20 minutes by hand; any single part is the same exercise as P3, which is included.
U5 · Ch5 · P3~10 min

Consider the following network. With the indicated link costs, use Dijkstra's shortest-path algorithm to compute the shortest path from x to all network nodes. Show how the algorithm works by computing a table similar to Table 5.1.

Dijkstra's algorithm keeps a set N' of nodes whose least cost is final. Initialization: \(D(n)=c(x,n)\) for each neighbour of x and \(\infty\) otherwise. Each step adds the node w not in N' with the smallest D(w), then for each neighbour n of w not in N' sets \(D(n)=\min(D(n),\,D(w)+c(w,n))\), recording p(n) = w when it improves.

Table (each cell is D,p; the bold entry is the node added to N' at that step and is final; ties are broken alphabetically, which does not change any cost):

stepN'D(t),p(t)D(u),p(u)D(v),p(v)D(w),p(w)D(y),p(y)D(z),p(z)
0x∞∞3,x6,x6,x8,x
1xv7,v6,v3,x6,x6,x8,x
2xvu7,v6,v6,x6,x8,x
3xvuw7,v6,x6,x8,x
4xvuwy7,v6,x8,x
5xvuwyt7,v8,x
6xvuwytz8,x

Reading the predecessors p() back to x gives the shortest paths:

DestinationShortest pathCost
tx-v-t7
ux-v-u6
vx-v3
wx-w6
yx-y6
zx-z8

Least costs from x: v = 3 (x-v), u = 6 (x-v-u), w = 6 (x-w), y = 6 (x-y), t = 7 (x-v-t), z = 8 (x-z).

Check: the alternatives x-w-u = 6 + 3 = 9, x-y-t = 6 + 7 = 13 and x-v-w = 3 + 4 = 7 are all worse, so the table is consistent.

U5 · Ch5 · P5~12 min

Consider the network shown below, and assume that each node initially knows the costs to each of its neighbors. Consider the distance-vector algorithm and show the distance table entries at node z.

(In the textbook figure two extra short lines leave z toward unnamed nodes; they play no part.)

U5 · Ch5 · P7~6 min

Consider the network fragment shown below. x has only two attached neighbors, w and y. w has a minimum-cost path to destination u (not shown) of 5, and y has a minimum-cost path to u of 6. The complete paths from w and y to u (and between w and y) are not shown. All link costs in the network have strictly positive integer values.

  1. Give x's distance vector for destinations w, y, and u.
  2. Give a link-cost change for either c(x,w) or c(x,y) such that x will inform its neighbors of a new minimum-cost path to u as a result of executing the distance-vector algorithm.
  3. Give a link-cost change for either c(x,w) or c(x,y) such that x will not inform its neighbors of a new minimum-cost path to u as a result of executing the distance-vector algorithm.

a. Bellman-Ford at x, whose neighbours are w and y (c(x,w) = 2, c(x,y) = 5, c(w,y) = 2):

\[ D_x(w)=\min\{c(x,w),\,c(x,y)+D_y(w)\}=\min\{2,\,5+2\}=2 \]
\[ D_x(y)=\min\{c(x,y),\,c(x,w)+D_w(y)\}=\min\{5,\,2+2\}=4 \]
\[ D_x(u)=\min\{c(x,w)+D_w(u),\,c(x,y)+D_y(u)\}=\min\{2+5,\,5+6\}=7 \]

\(D_x(w)=2,\ D_x(y)=4,\ D_x(u)=7\) (u via w)

b. x's least-cost path to u uses w, so a change to c(x,w) changes \(D_x(u)=\min\{c(x,w)+5,\;11\}\). For example, c(x,w) changes from 2 to 1: \(D_x(u)=\min\{1+5,\,11\}=6\), a new minimum cost, so x sends its new distance vector to its neighbours. (Any new c(x,w) changes \(D_x(u)\); from 6 upward x switches to the path via y with cost 11.)

c. Change c(x,y), the link x does not use for u. For example, c(x,y) changes from 5 to 10: \(D_x(u)=\min\{2+5,\,10+6\}=7\), unchanged, so x has no new minimum-cost path to u to announce. (Any c(x,y) of 1 or more leaves \(D_x(u)=7\), because \(c(x,y)+6\ge 7\); a value of 1 makes a tie at 7 and the cost is still unchanged. Raising c(x,y) also leaves \(D_x(y)=4\) and \(D_x(w)=2\), so x sends nothing at all.)

U5 · Ch5 · P9~4 min

Consider the count-to-infinity problem in the distance vector routing. Will the count-to-infinity problem occur if we decrease the cost of a link? Why? How about if we connect two nodes which do not have a link?

No, in both cases.

Count-to-infinity arises when a cost goes up: a node then trusts a neighbour's old, now-invalid smaller distance (which may route back through the node itself), and the two keep raising their estimates in small steps.

When a link cost decreases, the new cost is a real path that is immediately at least as good as the old values. Every node that hears of it computes \(D=\min(\text{old value},\ \text{new cheaper path})\), adopts the cheaper real path at once and passes it on; no node relies on stale larger information, so the network converges within a number of iterations bounded by the network's diameter ("good news travels fast").

Connecting two nodes that had no link is the same as lowering that link's cost from \(\infty\) to a finite value, so it is also a decrease: the new link can only create cheaper paths, and there is no count-to-infinity.

U5 · Ch5 · P10~8 min

Argue that for the distance-vector algorithm in Figure 5.6, each value in the distance vector D(x) is non-increasing and will eventually stabilize in a finite number of steps.

Assume, as in Figure 5.6, that the link costs stay fixed while the algorithm runs.

Non-increasing. Initially \(D_x(y)=c(x,y)\) for a neighbour y and \(\infty\) otherwise, and every neighbour's first vector is likewise its own link costs or \(\infty\). Each update computes \(D_x(y)=\min_v\{c(x,v)+D_v(y)\}\). Induction on time: suppose every value any node has sent so far is non-increasing. Then each term \(c(x,v)+D_v(y)\) can only stay the same or go down (c(x,v) is constant and \(D_v(y)\) is non-increasing), so their minimum can only stay the same or go down. The first computed value is no larger than the initial one, because the direct link (term with \(D_y(y)=0\)) is among the options, and \(\infty\) is larger than anything. Hence every \(D_x(y)\) is non-increasing.

Bounded below. Every finite \(D_x(y)\) is the cost of some real walk from x to y (it is built by adding link costs along actual links), so \(D_x(y)\ge d_x(y)\), the true least cost, which is at least 0.

Finitely many values. With strictly positive integer link costs, a non-increasing sequence of integers bounded below by \(d_x(y)\) can strictly decrease only finitely many times (at most (first finite value) minus \(d_x(y)\) times). More generally, in a finite network only finitely many path costs can appear.

So each \(D_x(y)\) decreases only a finite number of times and then stops changing. Once no vector changes, no node sends any more updates, and the algorithm has stabilized (at the least costs, which satisfy the Bellman-Ford equation).

U5 · Ch5 · P11~18 min

Consider Figure 5.7. Suppose there is another router w, connected to router y and z. The costs of all links are given as follows: c(x,y) = 4, c(x,z) = 50, c(y,w) = 1, c(z,w) = 1, c(y,z) = 3. Suppose that poisoned reverse is used in the distance-vector routing algorithm.

  1. When the distance vector routing is stabilized, router w, y, and z inform their distances to x to each other. What distance values do they tell each other?
  2. Now suppose that the link cost between x and y increases to 60. Will there be a count-to-infinity problem even if poisoned reverse is used? Why or why not? If there is a count-to-infinity problem, then how many iterations are needed for the distance-vector routing to reach a stable state again? Justify your answer.
  3. How do you modify c(y,z) such that there is no count-to-infinity problem at all if c(y,x) changes from 4 to 60?

a. Stable least costs to x:

\[ D_y(x)=\min\{4,\;1+5,\;3+6\}=4,\qquad D_w(x)=\min\{1+4,\;1+6\}=5,\qquad D_z(x)=\min\{50,\;3+4,\;1+5\}=6 \]

So y routes directly to x, w routes via y, and z routes via w. With poisoned reverse a router tells the neighbour it routes through that its distance is \(\infty\):

FromToAnnounced distance to xReason
yw4y routes directly to x
yz4y routes directly to x
wy\(\infty\)w routes via y (poisoned)
wz5true value
zy6true value (z routes via w, not y)
zw\(\infty\)z routes via w (poisoned)

y tells w 4 and z 4; w tells y \(\infty\) and z 5; z tells y 6 and w \(\infty\).

b. Yes, count-to-infinity still occurs. When c(x,y) becomes 60, y computes \(D_y(x)=\min\{60,\;1+\infty,\;3+6\}=9\) via z. But z's 6 is the path z-w-y-x, which goes back through y, so the loop y to z to w to y forms. Poisoned reverse only stops loops between two adjacent routers; this loop has three. Each router in the loop then builds on the stale value of the next one, so the costs climb by the loop cost 3 + 1 + 1 = 5 per lap.

Trace with synchronous rounds (every router recomputes from the vectors it received in the previous round; round 1 is y's reaction), computed by simulation:

4141110z / w / y

The climb stops when z's cost via w exceeds its direct link of 50. z's value via w goes 6, 11, ..., 46, rising 5 every 3 rounds, and the next value 51 > 50, so after \((51-6)/5=9\) laps of 3 rounds, in round 27, z switches to its direct link (50). Then y = 3 + 50 = 53 via z (round 28), w = 1 + 50 = 51 via z (round 29), and y = 1 + 51 = 52 via w (round 30); after that nothing changes.

About 30 iterations with this counting (z escapes the loop in round 27). Final: \(D_z(x)=50\) direct, \(D_w(x)=51\) via z, \(D_y(x)=52\) via w. These are the true least costs after the change (y-w-z-x = 1 + 1 + 50 = 52). Other counting conventions (for example, counting only the laps) give a different number, so state the convention you use.

c. The loop starts only because y prefers z's announced 6 over its new direct cost, that is \(c(y,z)+6<60\). To prevent it we need \(c(y,z)+6\ge 60\): set \(c(y,z)\ge 54\) (55 or more to avoid a tie at 60), or remove the y-z link (\(c(y,z)=\infty\)). Then y keeps its direct link at 60, w's poisoned \(\infty\) prevents the y-w loop, and the network converges without counting.

U5 · Ch5 · P13~4 min

Will a BGP router always choose the loop-free route with the shortest AS-path length? Justify your answer.

No.

The Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) applies policy before path length. The first route-selection rule is the highest local preference attribute, which the AS's administrator sets according to policy (for example, prefer routes learned from paying customers over routes from peers or providers). Only among routes with the same local preference does BGP pick the shortest AS-PATH, followed by hot potato routing and other tie-breakers.

So a router may choose a longer AS-path because it has a higher local preference. Policy also filters routes before selection: a neighbour may never advertise the shortest path (a customer AS does not offer transit, a peer does not advertise routes learned from another peer), and an AS may refuse to import some routes, so the shortest loop-free path may not even be available.

Loop-freedom is handled separately: a router rejects any advertisement whose AS-PATH already contains its own AS number.

U5 · Ch5 · P14~5 min

Consider the network shown below. Suppose AS3 and AS2 are running OSPF for their intra-AS routing protocol. Suppose AS1 and AS4 are running RIP for their intra-AS routing protocol. Suppose eBGP and iBGP are used for the inter-AS routing protocol. Initially suppose there is no physical link between AS2 and AS4.

  1. Router 3c learns about prefix x from which routing protocol: OSPF, RIP, eBGP, or iBGP?
  2. Router 3a learns about x from which routing protocol?
  3. Router 1c learns about x from which routing protocol?
  4. Router 1d learns about x from which routing protocol?

Prefix x is attached to router 4a in AS4. Reachability to a prefix in another AS crosses an AS boundary by external BGP (eBGP) between gateway routers, and is spread to the routers inside an AS by internal BGP (iBGP). The intra-AS protocols (OSPF, RIP) only carry routes to destinations inside their own AS.

  1. 3c is AS3's gateway connected to 4c in AS4, and it hears the advertisement "AS4, x" over an eBGP session: eBGP.
  2. 3a has no link to AS4, so it learns the route from 3c, inside its own AS, over iBGP: iBGP.
  3. 1c is AS1's gateway connected to 3a, and 3a advertises "AS3 AS4, x" to it over eBGP: eBGP.
  4. 1d is an internal router of AS1 and learns the route from 1c over iBGP: iBGP. (1d uses RIP only to find its path to 1c, which decides the interface, I1 or I2, it installs for x.)
U5 · Ch5 · P16~5 min

Consider the following network. ISP B provides national backbone service to regional ISP A. ISP C provides national backbone service to regional ISP D. Each ISP consists of one AS. B and C peer with each other in two places using BGP. Consider traffic going from A to D. B would prefer to hand that traffic over to C on the West Coast (so that C would have to absorb the cost of carrying the traffic cross-country), while C would prefer to get the traffic via its East Coast peering point with B (so that B would have carried the traffic across the country). What BGP mechanism might C use, so that B would hand over A-to-D traffic at its East Coast peering point? To answer this question, you will need to dig into the BGP specification.

C can use BGP's MULTI_EXIT_DISC (MED, multi-exit discriminator) attribute.

C advertises D's prefix to B at both peering points, but attaches a lower MED value to the advertisement sent at the East Coast peering point and a higher MED to the one sent at the West Coast. The MED tells a neighbouring AS which of several links into the advertising AS is preferred: when B's routers compare routes to the same prefix learned from the same neighbouring AS (C), the route with the lowest MED wins, and this comparison comes before the intra-AS cost (hot potato) rule.

So B's routers choose the East Coast exit and carry the A-to-D traffic across the country themselves. This works only if B honours the MED, which is normally written into the peering agreement; B could override it with its own local preference, which is checked first.

(A blunter alternative is for C to advertise D's prefix only at the East Coast peering point, but then the West Coast link is no longer a backup for this traffic.)

U5 · Ch5 · P17~10 min

In Figure 5.13, consider the path information that reaches stub networks W, X, and Y. Based on the information available at W and X, what are their respective views of the network topology? Justify your answer. The topology view at Y is shown below.

Figure 5.13 (a simple BGP policy scenario): provider networks A, B and C all connect to each other; W is a customer of A, X is a customer of both B and C, and Y is a customer of C.

A stub's view is just the union of the AS-paths it is told. The rules from Section 5.4.5: a stub (access ISP) advertises only itself; a provider sends a neighbour only its one best path per destination; and a provider advertises to a peer only routes to its own customers (no free transit).

Best paths at the providers: A: AW, ACY, and ABX or ACX (a 2-hop tie broken by a lower-level rule). B: BAW, BX, BCY. C: CAW, CX, CY. Check against the given view: Y hears only from C, receiving YCAW and YCX, which is exactly the 4 links shown, with no B.

W's view. W hears only from A: ACY and one of ABX or ACX. W sees W-A, A-C, C-Y, plus either A-B-X (if A's tie-break chose B) or A-C-X (if it chose C). W never learns of the B-C link or of X's second connection; if A chose C, W's view has the same shape as Y's.

X's view. X is multi-homed and hears from both providers: from B the paths BAW and BCY, from C the paths CAW and CY. So X receives XBAW, XBCY, XCAW and XCY, which together contain X-B, B-A, A-W, B-C, X-C, C-A and C-Y. X sees the complete topology of Figure 5.13 (all 7 links).

Justification: the views differ because each stub sees only the best paths advertised to it; a single-homed stub gets one path per destination, while X, with two providers, gets two different paths per destination.

U5 · Ch5 · P19~8 min

In Figure 5.13, suppose that there is another stub network V that is a customer of ISP A. Suppose that B and C have a peering relationship, and A is a customer of both B and C. Suppose that A would like to have the traffic destined to W to come from B only, and the traffic destined to V from either B or C. How should A advertise its routes to B and C? What AS routes does C receive?

Traffic toward a prefix flows opposite to the advertisement of that prefix, so "traffic for W comes from B only" means C must not learn a route to W directly through A.

A advertises to B the routes A-W and A-V; A advertises to C only A-V (it withholds W from C).
