(c) b to k are 0x62 to 0x6B:

  0x6263 + 0x6465 = 0xC6C8
  0xC6C8 + 0x6667 = 0x12D2F -> wrap: 0x2D2F + 1 = 0x2D30
  0x2D30 + 0x6869 = 0x9599
  0x9599 + 0x6A6B = 0x10004 -> wrap: 0x0004 + 1 = 0x0005
  sum      = 0x0005 = 0000 0000 0000 0101
  checksum = 0xFFFA = 1111 1111 1111 1010

Checksum 0xFFFA = 1111 1111 1111 1010.

U6 · Ch6 · P5~8 min

Consider the 5-bit generator, G = 10011, and suppose that D has the value 1010101010. What is the value of R?

G has r + 1 = 5 bits, so r = 4: append four 0s to D and divide by G in modulo-2 arithmetic (subtraction is XOR); R is the 4-bit remainder.

10011 ) 10101010100000
        10011
        00110010100000   (running value)
          10011
        00010100100000   (running value)
           10011
        00000111100000   (running value)
             10011
        00000011010000   (running value)
              10011
        00000001001000   (running value)
               10011
        00000000000100   (running value)
                  0100   = R (last r bits)

The last four bits are the remainder: R = 0100. The transmitted bits D,R = 10101010100100 are then exactly divisible by 10011.

U6 · Ch6 · P6~15 min

Consider the previous problem (G = 10011), but suppose that D has the value

  1. 1000100101.
  2. 0101101010.
  3. 0110100011.

As in P5, r = 4: append 0000 to D, divide by 10011 using XOR, and keep the 4-bit remainder.

(a) D = 1000100101:

10011 ) 10001001010000
        10011
        00010001010000   (running value)
           10011
        00000010010000   (running value)
              10011
        00000000001000   (running value)
                  1000   = R (last r bits)

R = 1000

(b) D = 0101101010:

         10011
        00010110100000   (running value)
           10011
        00000101100000   (running value)
             10011
        00000001010000   (running value)
               10011
        00000000011100   (running value)
                 10011
        00000000001111   (running value)
                  1111   = R (last r bits)

R = 1111

(c) D = 0110100011:

10011 ) 01101000110000
         10011
        00100100110000   (running value)

                10011

U6 · Ch6 · P11~8 min

Suppose four active nodes - nodes A, B, C and D - are competing for access to a channel using slotted ALOHA. Assume each node has an infinite number of packets to send. Each node attempts to transmit in each slot with probability p. The first slot is numbered slot 1, the second slot is numbered slot 2, and so on.

  1. What is the probability that node A succeeds for the first time in slot 4?
  2. What is the probability that some node (either A, B, C or D) succeeds in slot 5?
  3. What is the probability that the first success occurs in slot 4?
  4. What is the efficiency of this four-node system?

In any slot, A succeeds when it transmits and the other three do not: \(P_A = p(1-p)^3\). Some node succeeds when exactly one of the four transmits: \(P_S = 4p(1-p)^3\). Slots are independent.

(a) A fails in slots 1-3 and succeeds in slot 4:

\[ \left(1 - p(1-p)^3\right)^3\, p(1-p)^3 \]

\((1-p(1-p)^3)^3\,p(1-p)^3\)

(b) Slot 5 is like any other slot: \(4p(1-p)^3\).

(c) No success in slots 1-3, then a success in slot 4:

\[ \left(1 - 4p(1-p)^3\right)^3\, 4p(1-p)^3 \]

\((1-4p(1-p)^3)^3\,4p(1-p)^3\)

(d) Efficiency is the long-run fraction of successful slots, \(E(p) = 4p(1-p)^3\). Setting the derivative to zero: \(4(1-p)^3 - 12p(1-p)^2 = 4(1-p)^2(1-4p) = 0\), so \(p^* = 1/N = 1/4\).

\[ E(1/4) = 4\cdot\tfrac14\cdot\left(\tfrac34\right)^3 = \tfrac{27}{64} \approx 0.42 \]

The efficiency is \(4p(1-p)^3\), with maximum 27/64 = 0.42 at p = 1/4.

U6 · Ch6 · P13~6 min

Consider a broadcast channel with N nodes and a transmission rate of R bps. Suppose the broadcast channel uses polling (with an additional polling node) for multiple access. Suppose the amount of time from when a node completes transmission until the subsequent node is permitted to transmit (that is, the polling delay) is \(d_{poll}\). Suppose that within a polling round, a given node is allowed to transmit at most Q bits. What is the maximum throughput of the broadcast channel?

Throughput is highest when every node always has data, so each sends the full Q bits in every round. One polling round then carries N Q bits and lasts N transmission times plus N polling delays:

\[ T_{round} = N\frac{Q}{R} + N\,d_{poll} \]
\[ \text{Throughput}_{max} = \frac{NQ}{N\frac{Q}{R} + N d_{poll}} = \frac{Q}{\frac{Q}{R} + d_{poll}} = \frac{R\,Q}{Q + R\,d_{poll}} \]

The maximum throughput is \(\dfrac{Q}{Q/R + d_{poll}}\) bps, which is always below R and approaches R only when \(d_{poll}\) is small compared with Q/R.

U6 · Ch6 · P15~12 min

Consider Figure 6.33 (three subnets interconnected by routers: Subnet 1 holds hosts A and B, Subnet 2 holds C and D, Subnet 3 holds E and F). Now we replace the router between subnets 1 and 2 with a switch S1, and label the router between subnets 2 and 3 as R1.

  1. Consider sending an IP datagram from Host E to Host F. Will Host E ask router R1 to help forward the datagram? Why? In the Ethernet frame containing the IP datagram, what are the source and destination IP and MAC addresses?
  2. Suppose E would like to send an IP datagram to B, and assume that E's ARP cache does not contain B's MAC address. Will E perform an ARP query to find B's MAC address? Why? In the Ethernet frame (containing the IP datagram destined to B) that is delivered to router R1, what are the source and destination IP and MAC addresses?
  3. Suppose Host A would like to send an IP datagram to Host B, and neither A's ARP cache contains B's MAC address nor does B's ARP cache contain A's MAC address. Further suppose that the switch S1's forwarding table contains entries for Host B and router R1 only. Thus, A will broadcast an ARP request message. What actions will switch S1 perform once it receives the ARP request message? Will router R1 also receive this ARP request message? If so, will R1 forward the message to Subnet 3? Once Host B receives this ARP request message, it will send back to Host A an ARP response message. But will it send an ARP query message to ask for A's MAC address? Why? What will switch S1 do once it receives an ARP response message from Host B?

Notation: IP(X) and MAC(X) are host X's addresses; R1 has one interface on the subnet 1-2 side and one on Subnet 3, written MAC(R1, Subnet 3).

(a) No. E and F are on the same IP subnet (Subnet 3), which E knows by comparing F's address with its own subnet mask, so E sends the frame directly to F through the local switch, after an ARP query for F if needed.

SourceDestination
IPIP(E)IP(F)
MACMAC(E)MAC(F)

(b) No, E does not ARP for B. B is on a different IP subnet, and ARP only resolves addresses on the sender's own subnet (an ARP broadcast would not cross R1). E sends the datagram to its default router R1, so it needs (and ARPs for, if not cached) the MAC address of R1's Subnet 3 interface. The frame delivered to R1 carries:

SourceDestination
IPIP(E)IP(B)
MACMAC(E)MAC(R1, Subnet 3 interface)

(c)

  • S1 learns from the frame's source address: it adds an entry for MAC(A) and the interface the frame came in on. Because the destination is the broadcast address FF-FF-FF-FF-FF-FF, it floods the frame out of all its other interfaces.
  • R1 does receive the ARP request, since R1's interface is on the same broadcast domain (the switch floods to it). But R1 does not forward it to Subnet 3: routers do not forward link-layer broadcast frames, and the target IP is not R1's, so R1 discards it.
  • B does not need to send an ARP query for A: the ARP request already contains A's IP and MAC addresses, so B stores A's mapping in its ARP table and sends the ARP response as a unicast frame to MAC(A).
  • S1 receives the response, refreshes B's entry, finds MAC(A) in its table (learned from the request), and forwards the response only out of the interface toward A.
U6 · Ch6 · P16~10 min

Consider the previous problem, but suppose now that the router between subnets 2 and 3 is replaced by a switch. Answer questions (a)-(c) in the previous problem in this new context.

With both routers replaced by switches (call the new one S2), there is no router left: hosts A to F are all on one LAN, one broadcast domain and one IP subnet.

(a) No, E does not ask a router (there is none, and F is on the same subnet anyway). The frame carries:

SourceDestination
IPIP(E)IP(F)
MACMAC(E)MAC(F)

(b) Yes, E performs an ARP query for B, because B is now on the same subnet; E broadcasts the query, both switches flood it, and B replies with its MAC address. No router is involved, so the frame is delivered (through S2 and S1) directly to B with:

SourceDestination
IPIP(E)IP(B)
MACMAC(E)MAC(B)

(c)

  • S1 learns MAC(A) and its interface, and floods the broadcast ARP request out of all other interfaces.
  • The device that replaced R1 is now the switch S2; it receives the request and also floods it, so it does reach Subnet 3 (hosts E and F receive it and ignore it, since the target IP is not theirs). S2 also learns A's location.
  • B does not send an ARP query for A: the request already carries A's IP and MAC addresses, which B caches before unicasting its response to MAC(A).
  • S1 forwards the response only toward A, using the entry it learned from the request, and updates B's entry.
U6 · Ch6 · P18~6 min

Suppose nodes A and B are on the same 10 Mbps broadcast channel, and the propagation delay between the two nodes is 325 bit times. Suppose CSMA/CD and Ethernet packets are used for this broadcast channel. Suppose node A begins transmitting a frame and, before it finishes, node B begins transmitting a frame. Can A finish transmitting before it detects that B has transmitted? Why or why not? If the answer is yes, then A incorrectly believes that its frame was successfully transmitted without a collision. Hint: Suppose at time t = 0 bits, A begins transmitting a frame. In the worst case, A transmits a minimum-sized frame of 512 + 64 bit times. So A would finish transmitting the frame at t = 512 + 64 bit times. Thus, the answer is no, if B's signal reaches A before bit time t = 512 + 64 bits. In the worst case, when does B's signal reach A?

All times are in bit times (0.1 microseconds each at 10 Mbps), and A starts at t = 0.

  • A finishes a minimum-size frame (512 bits plus 64-bit preamble) at t = 512 + 64 = 576.
  • A's first bit reaches B at t = 325; after that B senses the channel busy and will not start. So the latest B can start is just before, at t = 324.
  • B's first bit then reaches A at t = 324 + 325 = 649.
\[ 649 > 576 \]

Yes, A can finish before it detects B: in the worst case B's signal reaches A at t = 649 bit times, 73 bit times after A stopped transmitting at t = 576, so A wrongly believes its frame got through. This is why Ethernet limits the network diameter so that the round-trip propagation time fits within the minimum frame time.

U6 · Ch6 · P19~10 min

Suppose nodes A and B are on the same 10 Mbps broadcast channel, and the propagation delay between the two nodes is 245 bit times. Suppose A and B send Ethernet frames at the same time, the frames collide, and then A and B choose different values of K in the CSMA/CD algorithm. Assuming no other nodes are active, can the retransmissions from A and B collide? For our purposes, it suffices to work out the following example. Suppose A and B begin transmission at t = 0 bit times. They both detect collisions at t = 245 bit times. Suppose KA = 0 and KB = 1. At what time does B schedule its retransmission? At what time does A begin transmission? (Note: The nodes must wait for an idle channel after returning to Step 2 - see protocol.) At what time does A's signal reach B? Does B refrain from transmitting at its scheduled time?

Use the Ethernet details: a 48-bit jam after detecting a collision, a backoff of K x 512 bit times, and 96 bit times of idle channel required before transmitting.

t (bit times)Event
0A and B both start transmitting
245Both detect the collision and start the 48-bit jam
245 + 48 = 293Both finish jamming; A (K = 0) returns to Step 2 immediately; B (K = 1) waits 512 bit times
293 + 512 = 805B's scheduled return to Step 2
293 + 245 = 538Last bit of B's jam reaches A; A senses the channel idle
538 + 96 = 634A begins transmitting
634 + 245 = 879A's first bit reaches B

At t = 805 B senses the channel idle and must wait 96 bit times, so it could transmit only at 805 + 96 = 901. A's signal reaches B at 879 < 901, so B senses the channel busy and waits.

B schedules its retransmission at t = 805; A begins at t = 634; A's signal reaches B at t = 879; B refrains, so the retransmissions do not collide.

U6 · Ch6 · P20~12 min

In this problem, you will derive the efficiency of a CSMA/CD-like multiple access protocol. In this protocol, time is slotted and all adapters are synchronized to the slots. Unlike slotted ALOHA, however, the length of a slot (in seconds) is much less than a frame time (the time to transmit a frame). Let S be the length of a slot. Suppose all frames are of constant length L = kRS, where R is the transmission rate of the channel and k is a large integer. Suppose there are N nodes, each with an infinite number of frames to send. We also assume that \(d_{prop} < S\), so that all nodes can detect a collision before the end of a slot time. The protocol is as follows:

  • If, for a given slot, no node has possession of the channel, all nodes contend for the channel; in particular, each node transmits in the slot with probability p. If exactly one node transmits in the slot, that node takes possession of the channel for the subsequent k - 1 slots and transmits its entire frame.
  • If some node has possession of the channel, all other nodes refrain from transmitting until the node that possesses the channel has finished transmitting its frame. Once this node has transmitted its frame, all nodes contend for the channel.

Note that the channel alternates between two states: the productive state, which lasts exactly k slots, and the nonproductive state, which lasts for a random number of slots. Clearly, the channel efficiency is the ratio of k/(k + x), where x is the expected number of consecutive unproductive slots.

  1. For fixed N and p, determine the efficiency of this protocol.
  2. For fixed N, determine the p that maximizes the efficiency.
  3. Using the p (which is a function of N) found in (b), determine the efficiency as N approaches infinity.
  4. Show that this efficiency approaches 1 as the frame length becomes large.

(a) A contention slot succeeds when exactly one node transmits, with probability

\[ \beta = Np(1-p)^{N-1} \]

The number of contention slots up to and including the first success is geometric with mean \(1/\beta\); the successful slot is the first of the k productive slots, so the expected number of unproductive slots is

\[ x = \frac{1}{\beta} - 1 = \frac{1-\beta}{\beta} \]
\[ \text{Efficiency} = \frac{k}{k + x} = \frac{k}{k + \dfrac{1 - Np(1-p)^{N-1}}{Np(1-p)^{N-1}}} \]

\(E = \dfrac{k}{k + \frac{1}{Np(1-p)^{N-1}} - 1}\)

(b) E is largest when x is smallest, that is when \(\beta = Np(1-p)^{N-1}\) is largest. \(\frac{d\beta}{dp} = N(1-p)^{N-2}\left[(1-p) - (N-1)p\right] = N(1-p)^{N-2}(1 - Np) = 0\) gives \(p^* = 1/N\).

(c) With p = 1/N, \(\beta = (1 - 1/N)^{N-1} \to 1/e\) as \(N \to \infty\), so \(x \to e - 1\):

\[ E \to \frac{k}{k + e - 1} \]

\(E_\infty = \dfrac{k}{k + e - 1} \approx \dfrac{k}{k + 1.718}\)

(d) A longer frame means a larger k = L/(RS), while \(e - 1\) is a constant:

\[ \lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{k}{k+e-1} = \lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{1 + (e-1)/k} = 1 \]

The efficiency approaches 1 as the frame length grows, because the fixed contention overhead becomes negligible compared with the frame time.

U6 · Ch6 · P27~8 min

In this problem, we explore the use of small packets for Voice-over-IP applications. One of the drawbacks of a small packet size is that a large fraction of link bandwidth is consumed by overhead bytes. To this end, suppose that the packet consists of P bytes and 5 bytes of header.

  1. Consider sending a digitally encoded voice source directly. Suppose the source is encoded at a constant rate of 128 kbps. Assume each packet is entirely filled before the source sends the packet into the network. The time required to fill a packet is the packetization delay. In terms of L, determine the packetization delay in milliseconds.
  2. Packetization delays greater than 20 msec can cause a noticeable and unpleasant echo. Determine the packetization delay for L = 1,500 bytes (roughly corresponding to a maximum-sized Ethernet packet) and for L = 50 (corresponding to an ATM packet).
  3. Calculate the store-and-forward delay at a single switch for a link rate of R = 622 Mbps for L = 1,500 bytes, and for L = 50 bytes.
  4. Comment on the advantages of using a small packet size.

(The textbook mixes P and L; L is the number of voice bytes per packet.)

(a) The source fills L bytes = 8L bits at 128,000 bits per second:

\[ d_{pack} = \frac{8L}{128{,}000}\ \text{s} = \frac{L}{16}\ \text{ms} \]

\(d_{pack} = L/16\) ms (L in bytes)

(b) L = 1,500: 1,500/16 = 93.75 ms, well above 20 ms, so an echo is noticeable. L = 50: 50/16 = 3.125 ms, well below 20 ms.

(c) Store-and-forward delay = L/R (the switch must receive the whole packet):

\[ \frac{8 \times 1500}{622\times 10^6} = 19.29\ \mu\text{s} \qquad \frac{8 \times 50}{622\times 10^6} = 0.643\ \mu\text{s} \]

19.29 microseconds for 1,500 bytes and 0.643 microseconds for 50 bytes (with the 5-byte header included: 19.36 and 0.707 microseconds).

(d) Small packets fill quickly, so the packetization delay stays far below the 20 ms echo threshold, and they also reduce store-and-forward and queuing delay at each switch, which makes delay low and predictable for real-time voice. The cost is overhead: 5 header bytes are 5/55 = 9.1% of a 55-byte packet but only 0.33% of a 1,505-byte packet, which is the trade-off ATM's 53-byte cells were designed around.

U6 · Ch6 · P33~8 min

Consider the hierarchical network in Figure 6.30 and suppose that the data center needs to support e-mail and video distribution among other applications. Suppose four racks of servers are reserved for e-mail and four racks are reserved for video. For each of the applications, all four racks must lie below a single tier-2 switch since the tier-2 to tier-1 links do not have sufficient bandwidth to support the intra-application traffic. For the e-mail application, suppose that for 99.9 percent of the time only three racks are used, and that the video application has identical usage patterns.

  1. For what fraction of time does the e-mail application need to use a fourth rack? How about for the video application?
  2. Assuming e-mail usage and video usage are independent, for what fraction of time do (equivalently, what is the probability that) both applications need their fourth rack?
  3. Suppose that it is acceptable for an application to have a shortage of servers for 0.001 percent of time or less (causing rare periods of performance degradation for users). Discuss how the topology in Figure 6.31 can be used so that only seven racks are collectively assigned to the two applications (assuming that the topology can support all the traffic).

(a) E-mail needs its fourth rack 1 - 0.999 = 0.001 of the time (0.1%); video, with the same pattern, also 0.001 (0.1%).

(b) For independent events the probabilities multiply:

\[ 0.001 \times 0.001 = 10^{-6} = 0.0001\% \]

Both need a fourth rack \(10^{-6}\) of the time (0.0001%).

(c) Give each application three dedicated racks and let them share one spare rack S: 3 + 3 + 1 = 7 racks. In the hierarchical topology of Figure 6.30 a rack hangs below only one tier-2 switch, so S could not be under both applications' tier-2 switches. In the highly interconnected topology of Figure 6.31, each Top of Rack (TOR) switch connects to two tier-2 switches, so place the e-mail racks under tier-2 switch X, the video racks under tier-2 switch Y, and choose S whose TOR switch connects to both X and Y; then all four racks of either application lie below a single tier-2 switch. An application is short of servers only when both need the fourth rack at the same time, which happens \(10^{-6}\) = 0.0001% of the time, below the acceptable 0.001% (\(10^{-5}\)), so seven racks with one shared spare meet the requirement with a factor of 10 to spare.

Unit 7

118 questions

Section 1: Wireless Networking

46 leading questions
U7 · S1 · Q1LQ
What electronic devices can be wireless hosts in wireless networks?

Any end device that runs applications and attaches over a radio link can be a wireless host: laptops, smartphones, tablets, smart watches and other wearables, desktop PCs with Wi-Fi cards, and Internet of Things (IoT) devices such as sensors, cameras, thermostats, and in-car systems.

A host may be mobile or stationary; "wireless" describes the link, not whether the device moves.

U7 · S1 · Q2LQ
What is a wireless communication link?

A wireless communication link is the radio channel that connects a wireless host to a base station (or to another host in ad hoc mode), or connects two network elements over the air (a backhaul link).

A link-layer multiple access protocol coordinates the hosts that share it, and different link technologies offer different transmission rates and ranges (for example, Wi-Fi for tens of metres, 4G/5G cellular for kilometres).

U7 · S1 · Q3LQ
What are the relationships between base stations and access points?

A base station is the general term for the device that relays data between wireless hosts and the wired network, and coordinates transmissions of the hosts associated with it.

An access point (AP) is the base station of an IEEE 802.11 (Wi-Fi) network; a cell tower (eNode-B in 4G, gNB in 5G) is the base station of a cellular network.

So every AP is a base station, but not every base station is an AP.

U7 · S1 · Q4LQ
What are the differences between infrastructure mode wireless networks and ad hoc wireless networks?
Infrastructure modeAd hoc mode
Base stationYes; hosts associate with an AP or cell towerNone
Network servicesAddress assignment, routing and Internet access come from the wired infrastructureHosts must provide routing, address assignment and name translation themselves
Traffic pathHost to base station to networkHost to host directly (or multi-hop through other hosts)
ExamplesHome Wi-Fi, cellularBluetooth piconet, mobile ad hoc network (MANET), vehicular ad hoc network (VANET)
U7 · S1 · Q5LQ
What are the parts of a wireless network infrastructure?

The textbook names four elements:

  • Wireless hosts: the end devices that run applications.
  • Wireless links: the radio channels connecting hosts to a base station or to each other.
  • Base stations: access points or cell towers that relay between wireless hosts and the wired network.
  • Network infrastructure: the larger wired network (and Internet) that the base stations connect to.
U7 · S1 · Q6LQ
Compare and contrast wired networks and wireless networks, emphasizing their differences.

Wireless links differ from wired ones in three main ways:

  • Decreasing signal strength (path loss): radio signals weaken with distance and when passing through walls and other matter.
  • Interference from other sources: other transmitters in the same band (other Wi-Fi networks, microwave ovens, Bluetooth) and electromagnetic noise corrupt signals.
  • Multipath propagation: reflected copies of the signal arrive at slightly different times and blur together.

As a result wireless links have much higher bit error rates, so they use stronger error detection plus link-level acknowledgments and retransmission (ARQ). The medium is shared and broadcast, so a sender cannot reliably detect collisions and suffers the hidden terminal and fading problems. Wireless also lets hosts move, which brings mobility and handoff issues that wired networks do not have, and adds security concerns because anyone in range can listen. Wired links are faster, more reliable and more secure, but fixed.

U7 · S1 · Q7LQ
How does multipath propagation occur?

Multipath propagation occurs when parts of a transmitted radio wave reflect off objects and the ground (walls, buildings, vehicles) and therefore travel paths of different lengths between sender and receiver.

The copies arrive at slightly different times, so they overlap and blur the received signal, causing inter-symbol interference and errors. Because objects (and the hosts) move, the set of paths, and so the effect, changes over time.

U7 · S1 · Q8LQ
What is the so-called hidden terminal problem?

The hidden terminal problem arises when two stations A and C can both reach a common receiver B but cannot hear each other, either because an obstacle lies between them or because their signals fade before reaching each other.

Since A cannot sense C's transmission, carrier sensing fails: both may transmit at the same time and their frames collide at B, even though each sender thought the channel was idle. Signal fading produces the same effect without any physical obstacle.

U7 · S1 · Q9LQ
What is the “chipping rate”?

In Code Division Multiple Access (CDMA), each data bit is multiplied by a code of M values (chips, each +1 or -1), so every bit time is split into M mini-slots.

The chipping rate is the rate at which these chips are sent; it is M times the data bit rate. For example, 19.2 kbps data with 128-chip codes needs a chipping rate of 2.4576 Mchips/s.

U7 · S1 · Q10LQ
How does CDMA work in wireless networks?

Code Division Multiple Access (CDMA) is a channel-partitioning protocol that partitions the code space rather than time or frequency. Each sender is assigned a unique code, and all senders transmit at the same time on the same frequencies.

The codes are chosen to be orthogonal (the chip-by-chip product of any two codes sums to zero, as with Walsh codes), so the receivers can each pull their own sender's data out of the sum of all the signals. The interfering signals simply add on the channel, and each receiver's decoding cancels out the senders whose codes are orthogonal to its own.

U7 · S1 · Q11LQ
How does CDMA encode and decode data in wireless networks?

Encode: to send data bit \(d_i\) (+1 for a 1, -1 for a 0) with code \(c = (c_1,\dots,c_M)\), the sender transmits \(Z_{i,m} = d_i \cdot c_m\) in each of the M mini-slots. A 1 sends the code; a 0 sends the code with every sign flipped.

Decode: the receiver multiplies each received chip by the matching chip of its sender's code, sums over the M chips, and divides by M: \(d_i = \frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M} Z^*_{i,m}\, c_m\).

With several senders the received chips are the sum of all senders' outputs. If the codes are orthogonal, the other senders' terms sum to zero and the receiver recovers its own bit exactly. Example: code (1,1,1,-1,1,-1,-1,-1) with bits 1, 0 gives (1,1,1,-1,1,-1,-1,-1) then (-1,-1,-1,1,-1,1,1,1).

U7 · S1 · Q12LQ
What IEEE standard is used for wireless networking?

The IEEE 802.11 family, commonly known as Wi-Fi, is the standard for wireless local area networks (LANs). Versions include 802.11b (2.4 GHz, 11 Mbps), 802.11a and g (54 Mbps), 802.11n (Wi-Fi 4), 802.11ac (Wi-Fi 5), and 802.11ax (Wi-Fi 6).

All share the same CSMA/CA medium access protocol and frame structure. For short-range personal area networks the related standard is IEEE 802.15 (Bluetooth).

U7 · S1 · Q13LQ
What is a basic service set (BSS)?

A basic service set (BSS) is the fundamental building block of the IEEE 802.11 architecture: one or more wireless stations plus one central base station, the access point (AP).

In infrastructure mode the AP connects the BSS, usually through a switch or router, to the wired network. An ad hoc BSS (an independent BSS) has no AP.

U7 · S1 · Q14LQ
What do SSID and Wi-Fi stand for?

SSID stands for Service Set Identifier: the one- or two-word network name an administrator assigns to an access point (AP), which appears in the list of available networks.

Wi-Fi is the Wi-Fi Alliance's trademark for IEEE 802.11 wireless LANs; it is usually expanded as "Wireless Fidelity", although the name was coined as a brand rather than a strict acronym.

U7 · S1 · Q15LQ
What is a distribution system in wireless networking?

In IEEE 802.11, the distribution system is the network that interconnects multiple basic service sets (BSSs), that is, their access points (APs), into one extended service set. It is usually a wired switched Ethernet LAN, often a single IP subnet.

It carries frames between APs and to the router, and it lets a station move from one AP to another in the same subnet while keeping its IP address.

U7 · S1 · Q16LQ
What is CSMA/CA?

Carrier Sense Multiple Access with Collision Avoidance (CSMA/CA) is the random access protocol of IEEE 802.11.

  1. If the station senses the channel idle, it waits a Distributed Inter-Frame Space (DIFS) and then transmits the whole frame.
  2. Otherwise it picks a random backoff value and counts down only while the channel is idle (freezing while busy).
  3. When the counter reaches zero (which happens only when the channel is idle) it transmits the entire frame and waits for an acknowledgment (ACK).
  4. If an ACK arrives, it starts step 2 for its next frame; if not, it re-enters backoff with a larger random range.

Unlike Ethernet's CSMA/CD, it does not detect collisions; it avoids them with random backoff, link-layer ACKs, and optionally the RTS/CTS reservation.

U7 · S1 · Q17LQ
What are DIFS and SIFS?

They are the idle gaps IEEE 802.11 requires between frames.

  • DIFS (Distributed Inter-Frame Space) is the longer gap a station must sense idle before starting a new transmission (50 microseconds in 802.11b).
  • SIFS (Short Inter-Frame Space) is the shorter gap a receiver waits before sending a reply within an ongoing exchange: an ACK, a Clear To Send (CTS), or data after a CTS (10 microseconds in 802.11b).

Because SIFS is shorter, replies always win the channel before any station waiting a DIFS can start, so an exchange is never interrupted.

U7 · S1 · Q18LQ
Why doesn’t the IEEE 802.11 MAC protocol implement collision detection?

The IEEE 802.11 medium access control (MAC) protocol does not use collision detection for two reasons:

  • Detecting a collision requires sending and receiving at the same time, and a station's own transmitted signal is so much stronger than a received one that building such hardware is costly.
  • Even if it could, a sender would miss many collisions because of the hidden terminal problem and fading: the collision happens at the receiver, where the sender cannot hear it.

So 802.11 transmits whole frames and relies on collision avoidance and link-layer acknowledgments instead.

U7 · S1 · Q19LQ
In an IEEE 802.11 wireless network, why can hidden terminals be problematic? How does the standard deal with the problem? What format does an IEEE 802.11 standard use for its frame?

Hidden terminals are problematic because two stations that cannot hear each other both sense the channel idle and transmit, so their frames collide at the access point (AP) without either sender noticing.

IEEE 802.11 optionally deals with this by reservation: a station sends a short Request To Send (RTS) frame giving the time needed for data plus ACK; the AP answers with a Clear To Send (CTS) frame that every station in its range hears, including hidden ones, and those stations stay silent for the reserved period. Collisions can then only involve the short RTS frames. RTS/CTS is usually used only for long frames, above a threshold.

The 802.11 frame has: Frame Control (2 bytes: protocol version, type, subtype, To AP, From AP, more frag, retry, power management, more data, WEP, rsvd), Duration (2), Address 1 (receiver, 6), Address 2 (transmitter, 6), Address 3 (router interface, 6), Sequence Control (2), Address 4 (used in ad hoc mode, 6), Payload (0-2312 bytes) and a 4-byte Cyclic Redundancy Check (CRC).

U7 · S1 · Q20LQ
In order to accommodate mobile users, what considerations should be taken when designing the network layer?

The network layer must let a mobile node keep the same address and identity (so ongoing connections survive) while it changes its point of attachment, and must still be able to route packets to it.

Key considerations: how mobile the user really is (moving within one access network vs between networks); whether the address must stay the same; what supporting infrastructure exists (home and visited networks, agents, a location database such as the Home Subscriber Server or Home Location Register); how to locate the node (letting routers advertise it does not scale, so mobility is pushed to the edge, to the home and visited networks); indirect versus direct routing and the cost of triangle routing; tunneling; signaling overhead and handoff latency; and security of the registration process.

U7 · S1 · Q21LQ
From the standpoint of network layers, how mobile is a user?

Mobility is a spectrum seen from the network layer:

  • No mobility: a user who moves only within one access network (for example, between APs on one subnet) keeps the same IP address; the network layer sees no change.
  • Moderate: a user who moves between networks but powers down while moving (laptop from office to home) can simply get a new address with DHCP; no ongoing connection must survive.
  • High: a user who moves between access networks at speed while keeping ongoing connections (a phone call or video stream in a car) requires true mobility management: tracking location, rerouting, and handover.
U7 · S1 · Q22LQ
How important is the mobile node’s address?

Very important. Transport connections (for example, TCP) are identified partly by IP addresses, so if the address changes the connection breaks. Keeping a permanent address makes mobility transparent to applications and correspondents, which keep sending to the same address.

The mobile node therefore keeps a permanent (home) address and, while visiting, also obtains a care-of address; the network maps between the two. If transparency is not needed, the node can simply take a new address with DHCP.

U7 · S1 · Q23LQ
What supporting wired infrastructure is available for supporting wireless connections?

Supporting wired infrastructure includes base stations or access points connected to wired switches and routers; the home network with its home agent (or, in 4G/5G, the Home Subscriber Server (HSS) that stores subscriber information and current location, and a home gateway router); the visited network with its foreign agent (in 4G, the Mobility Management Entity (MME) and Serving and PDN Gateways (S-GW, P-GW)); DHCP servers for address assignment; and the Internet or carrier backbone that links home and visited networks and carries the tunnels between them.

In the simplest case, the user gets no special support at all and just moves among networks with new addresses.

U7 · S1 · Q24LQ
What is ad hoc networking?

Ad hoc networking means wireless hosts communicate without any base station or wired infrastructure: they form the network themselves.

The hosts must provide the services an infrastructure normally gives, such as routing (often multi-hop, each node relaying for others), address assignment, and name translation. Examples include Bluetooth piconets, mobile ad hoc networks (MANETs), vehicular ad hoc networks (VANETs), and networks deployed in disaster areas or battlefields.

U7 · S1 · Q25LQ
What issues are involved in allowing a mobile user to maintain ongoing connections while moving between networks?

To keep ongoing connections while moving between networks, the system must:

  • Keep the node's address (its identity) constant so transport connections do not break, while it gets a temporary address in each visited network.
  • Locate the node: track which network it is in now (registration with the home network or Home Subscriber Server (HSS)).
  • Route or tunnel packets to its current location (indirect or direct routing), and avoid inefficient triangle routing where possible.
  • Perform handover between base stations or networks quickly, without losing or reordering too many packets.
  • Do all this securely (authentication) and with low signaling overhead and latency.
U7 · S1 · Q26LQ
What do the terms home network, home agent, foreign (or visited) network, foreign agent, and correspondent mean?
  • Home network: the permanent "home" of the mobile node (in cellular terms, the subscriber's own carrier), where its permanent address lives.
  • Home agent: the entity in the home network that performs mobility management for the node: it tracks the node's current location and forwards (tunnels) its traffic.
  • Foreign (visited) network: the network in which the mobile node currently resides.
  • Foreign agent: the entity in the visited network that helps the node with mobility: it provides or records the care-of address, registers it with the home agent, and delivers forwarded packets to the node.
  • Correspondent: the entity (any host) that wants to communicate with the mobile node.
U7 · S1 · Q27LQ
Why should all traffic addressed to a mobile node’s permanent address be routed to the foreign network when the node is a resident of a foreign network? How can this be done?

Because correspondents keep sending to the node's permanent address, which routes to the home network; if nothing happened, the datagrams would arrive at the home network while the node is elsewhere and be lost. Forwarding them to the foreign network keeps mobility transparent to correspondents and keeps connections alive.

How: the node registers with the foreign agent and gets a care-of address (COA), and the foreign agent (or node) tells the home agent this COA. The home agent intercepts datagrams sent to the permanent address and tunnels them, encapsulated inside a new datagram addressed to the COA, to the foreign agent, which decapsulates and delivers them (indirect routing). Alternatively, the correspondent learns the COA and tunnels directly (direct routing). Letting routers advertise the node's address as a host route would also work but does not scale to millions of mobile nodes.

U7 · S1 · Q28LQ
What roles does a foreign agent play?
  • Advertises its presence (agent advertisement) and answers agent solicitations, so arriving nodes discover it.
  • Gives the mobile node a care-of address (COA), usually its own address.
  • Relays registration: tells the home agent that the node is resident in its network with that COA, and relays the reply.
  • Receives the datagrams tunneled by the home agent, decapsulates them, and forwards them to the mobile node.
  • May act as the node's default router for outgoing traffic.
U7 · S1 · Q29LQ
What is a care-of-address?

A care-of address (COA) is the address associated with a mobile node in the foreign (visited) network, usually the foreign agent's address (or one obtained by DHCP when the node acts as its own foreign agent).

The home agent uses it as the destination of the tunnel: datagrams for the permanent address are encapsulated and sent to the COA, where they are decapsulated and delivered. The node thus has two addresses while visiting: its permanent address and its COA.

U7 · S1 · Q30LQ
What is a home address? What is a foreign address?

The home address (permanent address) is the mobile node's long-term address in its home network; correspondents always use it, and it never changes as the node moves.

The foreign address is the care-of address (COA): a temporary address in the foreign (visited) network where the node currently is, used to tunnel traffic to it. It changes each time the node moves to a new network.

U7 · S1 · Q31LQ
How does indirect routing work?
  1. The correspondent addresses datagrams to the mobile node's permanent address and sends them into the network as usual, unaware of mobility.
  2. They are routed to the home network, where the home agent intercepts them.
  3. The home agent encapsulates each datagram in a new datagram addressed to the node's care-of address and tunnels it to the foreign agent (in 4G/5G, the home gateway tunnels to the visited network's gateway).
  4. The foreign agent decapsulates and forwards the original datagram to the mobile node.
  5. The mobile node replies directly to the correspondent, using its permanent address as the source.

It is transparent to the correspondent and handles movement between visited networks by just updating the COA at the home agent.

U7 · S1 · Q32LQ
How does the mobile node to foreign agent protocol work?

When the mobile node arrives in a foreign network it discovers the foreign agent (through agent advertisement or solicitation) and registers with it, supplying its permanent address and home agent. The foreign agent assigns or records the care-of address (COA) and registers it with the home agent, which confirms.

Afterwards the foreign agent decapsulates datagrams tunneled from the home agent and delivers them to the node. When the node leaves, it registers with the new foreign agent, which informs the home agent of the new COA; the registration with the old one expires or is removed.

U7 · S1 · Q33LQ
What problem does the indirect routing approach suffer from?

With indirect routing, every datagram from the correspondent travels first to the home network and then to the foreign network, even if a much shorter path exists between correspondent and mobile node (both might even be in the same visited network). This inefficient correspondent-home-visited path is called the triangle routing problem: it adds delay and wastes bandwidth.

Direct routing solves it, at the cost of complexity.

U7 · S1 · Q34LQ
How does the direct routing approach work?
  1. A correspondent agent in the correspondent's network (or the correspondent itself) asks the home agent (in cellular, the Home Subscriber Server (HSS)) for the mobile node's current care-of address (COA).
  2. The home agent returns the COA.
  3. The correspondent agent tunnels datagrams directly to the COA, bypassing the home network.
  4. The foreign agent decapsulates and delivers them; replies go directly to the correspondent.

This avoids triangle routing, but it is not transparent to the correspondent, and it must handle movement: when the node moves, the first foreign agent (the anchor foreign agent) forwards datagrams to the new foreign agent, or the correspondent must learn the new COA.

U7 · S1 · Q35LQ
What is a correspondent agent in the direct routing approach?

A correspondent agent is the entity in the correspondent's network that, in direct routing, learns the mobile node's care-of address (COA) by querying the home agent and then tunnels datagrams directly to that COA (to the foreign agent).

The correspondent itself may play this role.

U7 · S1 · Q36LQ
What is mobile IP? Which standard defines mobile IP?

Mobile IP is the Internet architecture and set of protocols that lets a mobile node keep its permanent IP address while moving between networks. It uses home and foreign agents, care-of addresses, and indirect routing with tunneling.

It is defined by the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF): RFC 5944 for IPv4 (originally RFC 3344) and RFC 6275 for Mobile IPv6. It has seen little deployment compared with the 4G/5G mobility architecture.

U7 · S1 · Q37LQ
What are the three main pieces of the mobile IP standard?
  • Agent discovery: how a mobile node finds home or foreign agents, and how agents advertise services (agent advertisement and agent solicitation).
  • Registration with the home agent: how a mobile node or foreign agent registers and deregisters care-of addresses with the home agent.
  • Indirect routing of datagrams: how the home agent forwards datagrams to the mobile node, including tunneling (encapsulation) and error rules.
U7 · S1 · Q38LQ
What is done in the process of agent discovery?

Agent discovery is how a mobile node that has arrived in a new network learns the identity of the corresponding foreign (or home) agent, and so learns that it has moved and what care-of address (COA) it can use.

It happens in one of two ways: agent advertisement, where agents periodically broadcast their presence, or agent solicitation, where the mobile node broadcasts a request for an agent to reply.

U7 · S1 · Q39LQ
What is done in the process of agent advertisement?

A foreign or home agent periodically broadcasts an Internet Control Message Protocol (ICMP) router discovery message (type 9) on all its links, carrying a mobility agent advertisement extension.

It contains the agent's IP address, the care-of addresses (COAs) it offers, a registration lifetime, and flag bits saying whether it is a home agent (H), a foreign agent (F), whether registration is required (R), and which encapsulations are supported. A mobile node that hears it learns an agent's identity and a COA.

U7 · S1 · Q40LQ
What does agent solicitation do?

Agent solicitation lets a mobile node that does not want to wait for the next periodic advertisement ask for one: it broadcasts an Internet Control Message Protocol (ICMP) solicitation message (type 10).

Any agent receiving it immediately unicasts an agent advertisement to the node, which then proceeds as if it had received an unsolicited advertisement.

U7 · S1 · Q41LQ
What is the format of an ICMP router discovery message with mobility agent advertisement?

It is a standard Internet Control Message Protocol (ICMP) router discovery message (type 9) with a mobility agent advertisement extension appended.

  • ICMP part: type, code, checksum, and the agent's (router's) IP address.
  • Extension: type, length, a sequence number (lets a node notice that an agent rebooted), a registration lifetime (the longest registration the agent accepts), flag bits saying whether it is a home agent (H) or foreign agent (F), whether registration is required (R) or the agent is busy (B), and which encapsulations it supports, followed by one or more care-of addresses (COAs) it offers.
U7 · S1 · Q42LQ
According to the mobile IP standard, how does a mobile node register its care-of address (COA) with its home agent?
  1. After hearing an agent advertisement, the mobile node sends a registration request to the foreign agent, giving its permanent address, its home agent's address, the care-of address (COA) it will use, and a requested lifetime.
  2. The foreign agent records this and forwards the request to the home agent.
  3. The home agent checks the request, binds the node's permanent address to the COA (so it can tunnel datagrams there), and sends a registration reply with the granted lifetime.
  4. The foreign agent relays the reply to the mobile node.

The registration lasts for the granted lifetime, so the node must reregister before it expires.

U7 · S1 · Q43LQ
How do wireless stations move seamlessly from one BBS to another while maintaining ongoing TCP sessions?

If the basic service sets (BSSs) belong to the same IP subnet, joined by Ethernet switches, the station keeps its IP address, so TCP sessions continue. As the signal from AP1 weakens, the station scans, finds a stronger AP2, disassociates from AP1 and associates with AP2.

The switch is self-learning: when the station's frames start arriving through AP2, the switch updates its forwarding table to point the station's MAC address at AP2's port. AP2 can speed this up by broadcasting an Ethernet frame with the station's source MAC address right after association. Moving between different subnets would change the IP address and need mobility management (for example, Mobile IP).

U7 · S1 · Q44LQ
What are the main features of 802.15 wireless networks?

IEEE 802.15.1 (Bluetooth) defines a wireless personal area network (WPAN):

  • Short range (about 10 m), low power, and low cost; built to replace cables between personal devices.
  • Operates in the unlicensed 2.4 GHz band using TDM with 625-microsecond slots, plus frequency-hopping spread spectrum (FHSS) across 79 channels, changing channel each slot.
  • Rates up to about 3 Mbps (classic Bluetooth).
  • Ad hoc: devices form piconets of up to 8 active devices (1 master and up to 7 slaves) plus up to 255 parked devices; the master controls the clock and grants slaves the right to transmit.

IEEE 802.15.4 (Zigbee) is a related, even lower-power, lower-rate standard for sensors.

U7 · S1 · Q45LQ
How is a Bluetooth network related to 802.15 wireless networks?

Bluetooth is the commercial name of the technology that IEEE standardized as 802.15.1, so a Bluetooth network is an 802.15.1 wireless personal area network (WPAN).

Its basic unit is the piconet: an ad hoc network of up to 8 active devices, one master and up to 7 slaves, sharing a frequency-hopping channel the master controls. Other 802.15 standards (for example, 802.15.4, the basis of Zigbee) cover different low-rate personal area networks.

U7 · S1 · Q46LQ
What do FHSS and PSTN stand for?
  • FHSS = Frequency-Hopping Spread Spectrum: the transmitter changes carrier frequency in a pseudo-random pattern known to sender and receiver. Bluetooth hops among 79 channels, once per 625-microsecond slot, which spreads interference.
  • PSTN = Public Switched Telephone Network: the traditional circuit-switched landline telephone network, to which cellular networks connect for voice calls.

Section 2: Cellular Networks: 4G and 5G

27 leading questions · Textbook Section 7.4, Cellular Networks: 4G and 5G,
U7 · S2 · Q1LQ
How do TDM and FDM work?

Both are channel partitioning techniques.

  • Time Division Multiplexing (TDM) divides time into frames of fixed slots; each user gets the full bandwidth during its own slot in every frame.
  • Frequency Division Multiplexing (FDM) divides the spectrum into frequency bands; each user gets its own band all the time.

Both avoid collisions and are fair, but a user gets at most R/N even when others are idle. Cellular systems combine them: 2G GSM uses FDM channels each split into 8 TDM slots, and 4G/5G use Orthogonal Frequency Division Multiplexing (OFDM), allocating time-frequency blocks.

U7 · S2 · Q2LQ
What are the main features of GSM networks?

The Global System for Mobile Communications (GSM) is the dominant second-generation (2G) cellular standard, mainly for voice.

  • Combined FDM/TDM: 200 kHz frequency channels each divided into 8 time slots, so each channel carries 8 calls.
  • Base Transceiver Stations (BTS) serve cells and are controlled by Base Station Controllers (BSC), together the Base Station System (BSS).
  • A Mobile Switching Center (MSC) handles call setup, mobility and handoff; a gateway MSC connects to the Public Switched Telephone Network (PSTN).
  • A Home Location Register (HLR) and Visitor Location Register (VLR) track subscribers' locations; a SIM card identifies the subscriber.
  • Circuit-switched digital voice with encryption; data only at low rates (later GPRS and EDGE).
U7 · S2 · Q3LQ
What are the main features of GPRS networks?

The General Packet Radio Service (GPRS), a "2.5G" extension of GSM, added packet-switched data to the circuit-switched voice network.

  • It reuses GSM radio channels and dynamically allocates time slots to data users; data rates of tens of kbps (up to about 115 kbps).
  • Always-on connection, with users charged by data volume rather than connection time.
  • New core nodes: the Serving GPRS Support Node (SGSN), which handles packet delivery and mobility, and the Gateway GPRS Support Node (GGSN), which connects to the Internet.

It was the step toward 3G data networks (with EDGE improving rates further).

U7 · S2 · Q4LQ
What is the triangle routing problem?

With indirect routing, every datagram from the correspondent travels first to the home network and then to the foreign network, even if a much shorter path exists between correspondent and mobile node (both might even be in the same visited network). This inefficient correspondent-home-visited path is called the triangle routing problem: it adds delay and wastes bandwidth.

Direct routing solves it, at the cost of complexity.

U7 · S2 · Q5LQ
How do mobile user location protocols work?

Location protocols track which network or cell a mobile user is in so traffic can reach it.

  • When a device attaches to a visited network it registers there, and the visited network informs the home network (the Home Location Register (HLR) in GSM, the Home Subscriber Server (HSS) in 4G), which records the visited network. The visited network keeps a local record (the Visitor Location Register (VLR), or the Mobility Management Entity (MME) in 4G).
  • To reach the user, a caller's network queries the home register for the current location.
  • Within the visited network, the device reports its tracking/location area when it changes; for an idle device, the network pages all cells in that area to find it.

This is mobility at the network edge: the routers' tables do not change as users move.

U7 · S2 · Q6LQ
How do handoffs happen in GSM networks?

A handoff moves an active call from one base station to another, usually because the signal to the new one is stronger or the old cell is overloaded.

  1. The phone continually measures beacon signal strength of nearby base stations and reports to its current base station.
  2. The old Base Station System (BSS) decides to hand off and tells the Mobile Switching Center (MSC) the target.
  3. The MSC sets up a path to the new BSS, which allocates a radio channel.
  4. The new BSS signals the MSC and old BSS that it is ready; the old BSS tells the phone to switch.
  5. The phone switches to the new BSS and signals completion; the MSC reroutes the call; the old path is released.

For handoffs between MSCs, the first (anchor) MSC stays on the path and the call is extended through a chain to the new MSC.

U7 · S2 · Q7LQ
How can calls be routed to a mobile user in mobile networks?

GSM uses indirect routing for calls:

  1. The caller dials the mobile user's phone number; the leading digits identify the user's home network, so the call goes to the home network.
  2. The home Mobile Switching Center (MSC) queries the Home Location Register (HLR) and gets a roaming number (a temporary number in the visited network, like a care-of address).
  3. The home MSC sets up the second leg of the call to the visited network's MSC using the roaming number; that MSC uses its Visitor Location Register (VLR) to reach the user's current cell and rings the phone.

4G/5G data uses the same idea with tunnels from the home gateway (P-GW) to the visited serving gateway (S-GW), or the P-GW in the visited network for local breakout.

U7 · S2 · Q8LQ
What are the main components of cellular network architecture?

A 4G Long-Term Evolution (LTE) network has:

  • Mobile device (user equipment) with a SIM that holds its International Mobile Subscriber Identity (IMSI).
  • Base station (eNode-B; gNB in 5G) at the edge of the radio access network; it manages radio resources and handovers and is the device's first hop.
  • All-IP core: the Home Subscriber Server (HSS), a database of subscriber information and location; the Serving Gateway (S-GW) and Packet Data Network Gateway (P-GW), which carry user data and link to the Internet (the P-GW assigns the device's IP address); and the Mobility Management Entity (MME), which handles authentication, connection, tracking and tunnel setup.

The network is divided into cells, each covered by a base station. The control plane (signaling) is separate from the data plane, and data is tunneled from base station to S-GW to P-GW.

U7 · S2 · Q9LQ
What are some important standards and technologies for cellular networks?
  • 2G: Global System for Mobile Communications (GSM) with FDM/TDM, voice; GPRS and EDGE add packet data.
  • 3G: Universal Mobile Telecommunications System (UMTS) using Wideband CDMA (W-CDMA); CDMA2000; and HSPA for faster data.
  • 4G: Long-Term Evolution (LTE) and LTE-Advanced, all-IP, with Orthogonal Frequency Division Multiplexing (OFDM) and MIMO antennas.
  • 5G: New Radio (NR) with FR1 and FR2 (millimetre wave) frequencies, massive MIMO, and a service-based 5G Core.

These standards are produced by the 3rd Generation Partnership Project (3GPP) and approved by the International Telecommunication Union (ITU). Key technologies include channel partitioning (FDM, TDM, CDMA, OFDM), MIMO, mobility management with HSS/MME, and IP tunneling.

U7 · S2 · Q10LQ
What role or roles does a Radio Network Controller (RNC) play on a 3G cellular network?

In a 3G Universal Mobile Telecommunications System (UMTS) network, the Radio Network Controller (RNC) controls a group of base stations (Node Bs) and connects the radio access network to the core.

  • It manages radio resources: channel allocation, power control, and admission control.
  • It handles mobility: it performs handovers between its base stations.
  • It connects to both core networks: the circuit-switched voice core (MSC) and the packet-switched data core (the Serving GPRS Support Node, SGSN).

In 4G Long-Term Evolution (LTE) the RNC is gone; its functions moved into the eNode-B base stations and the core.

U7 · S2 · Q11LQ
What are the differences between 3G cellular network and 4G LTE cellular network?
3G4G Long-Term Evolution (LTE)
CoreSeparate circuit-switched voice and packet-switched data coresAll-IP core (voice carried as Voice over LTE)
Radio accessNode B base stations controlled by Radio Network Controllers (RNCs)No RNC; smarter eNode-B base stations
Radio technologyWideband CDMA (W-CDMA)OFDM downlink with time-frequency resource blocks, MIMO antennas
Data rates, latencyA few Mbps, higher latencyTens to hundreds of Mbps, lower latency
Control vs dataLargely combinedClear separation of control plane (MME, HSS) and data plane (S-GW, P-GW)
U7 · S2 · Q12LQ
What are the three sublayers of the mobile device’s link layer on a 4G LTE cellular network? What role does each sublayer play?
  • Packet Data Convergence Protocol (PDCP): header compression to save radio bandwidth, and encryption and decryption of the IP datagrams.
  • Radio Link Control (RLC): fragmentation and reassembly of datagrams that are too large for link-layer frames, and link-layer reliable delivery with ACK/NAK-based ARQ.
  • Medium Access Control (MAC): requests and uses transmission slots (radio resources) allocated by the base station, and performs extra error detection and correction.
U7 · S2 · Q13LQ
What is the 4G Core network?

The 4G core, the Evolved Packet Core (EPC), is the all-IP network behind the base stations that ties the radio access network to the Internet and handles subscribers and mobility. Its elements:

  • Home Subscriber Server (HSS): database of subscriber profiles, authentication keys, and current location.
  • Mobility Management Entity (MME): control plane; authenticates devices with the HSS, sets up data tunnels, tracks location, handles handover and paging.
  • Serving Gateway (S-GW): data plane; the mobility anchor for the device's tunnels as it changes base stations.
  • Packet Data Network Gateway (P-GW): data plane; assigns the device's IP address, and is the gateway to the Internet, enforcing policy and charging.
U7 · S2 · Q14LQ
What does the 4G Core network do?

The 4G core (Evolved Packet Core) authenticates subscribers (MME with HSS), allocates IP addresses (P-GW), sets up and manages GTP tunnels for user data from the base station through the Serving Gateway (S-GW) to the Packet Data Network Gateway (P-GW), and forwards the data to and from the Internet.

It also performs mobility management (location tracking, paging idle devices, handover by redirecting tunnels), roaming with other carriers' networks, quality of service, and charging. Its control plane (MME, HSS) is separate from its data plane (S-GW, P-GW).

U7 · S2 · Q15LQ
On an LTE cellular network, what is network attachment?

Network attachment is the process by which a device connects to an LTE network when it powers on or arrives.

  1. Base station association: the device listens for primary synchronization signals that base stations broadcast every 5 ms, then reads the broadcast information (bandwidth, configuration, carrier), picks a base station (usually the strongest signal from its own or a partner carrier) and associates with it.
  2. Mutual authentication: the base station contacts the local Mobility Management Entity (MME), which uses the device's IMSI and the home Home Subscriber Server (HSS) to authenticate the device, and the device authenticates the network.
  3. Data-plane path setup: the MME and gateways set up the tunnels from the base station through the Serving Gateway to the Packet Data Network Gateway (P-GW), and the device gets its IP address.
U7 · S2 · Q16LQ
On an LTE cellular network, what does power management do?

Power management saves a battery-powered device's energy, mainly by turning the radio off whenever possible (sleep modes). The device reaches a lower-power state after a period of inactivity and wakes up periodically to check for traffic.

This trades latency for energy: the deeper the sleep, the longer it takes to resume communication. Transmit power is also controlled so devices send only as strongly as needed.

U7 · S2 · Q17LQ
What are sleep modes of wireless devices on an LTE cellular network?

LTE has two sleep states that save battery by keeping the radio off most of the time.

  • Light sleep (discontinuous reception): entered after several hundred milliseconds of inactivity. The device and base station agree in advance on periodic wake-up times (typically several hundred milliseconds apart) when the device listens for downstream transmissions; between them the radio sleeps. The device stays associated with its base station.
  • Deep sleep (Idle state): entered after 5 to 10 seconds of inactivity. The radio wakes even less often, and the device may move to a new cell without telling the network. On waking it must re-associate with a (possibly new) base station to check for paging messages, which the Mobility Management Entity (MME) broadcasts through base stations near the one the device last used.
U7 · S2 · Q18LQ
What is the global cellular network? How does it work?

The global cellular network is a "network of networks": the many carriers' cellular networks (mostly 4G and 5G), each with its own base stations, core, and gateways, interconnected with each other and with the public Internet.

Carriers connect their gateway routers through Internet Exchange Points (IXPs) or a dedicated IP exchange (IPX) network, and roaming agreements let a subscriber attach to a partner's visited network. The visited network authenticates the user with the home network's Home Subscriber Server (HSS) and tunnels the user's traffic back to the home network (or breaks it out locally), so a phone works almost anywhere in the world.

U7 · S2 · Q19LQ
What defines the ultimate wide-area data service?

The textbook defines it as a service with ubiquitous gigabit connection speeds, extremely low latency, and no practical limit on the number of users and devices supported in any region.

Such a service would enable pervasive augmented and virtual reality, wireless control of autonomous vehicles and factory robots, and fixed wireless Internet replacing DSL and cable. 5G is expected to be a big step towards it.

U7 · S2 · Q20LQ
What services are 5G cellular networks expected to provide?

5G consists of three co-existing standards (service types):

  • Enhanced Mobile Broadband (eMBB): higher download and upload speeds and somewhat lower latency than 4G Long-Term Evolution (LTE), for rich media such as mobile augmented and virtual reality and 4K and 360-degree video.
  • Ultra-Reliable Low-Latency Communications (URLLC): latencies of about 1 ms for highly latency-sensitive uses such as factory automation and autonomous driving.
  • Massive Machine-Type Communications (mMTC): narrowband, low-power access for huge numbers of Internet of Things (IoT) devices doing sensing, metering, and monitoring.

Overall, 5G is predicted to give roughly 10 times the peak bit rate, one tenth the latency, and 100 times the traffic capacity of 4G.

U7 · S2 · Q21LQ
What are the two major drawbacks of FR2 frequencies (millimeter wave frequencies) used some 5G networks?

FR2 (millimetre wave, 24-52 GHz) frequencies allow much faster data speeds but have two major drawbacks:

  1. Much shorter range from base station to receivers, which makes them unsuitable for rural areas and requires denser deployments of base stations in urban areas.
  2. High susceptibility to atmospheric interference: nearby foliage and rain cause problems outdoors, and the signals are easily blocked by buildings.
U7 · S2 · Q22LQ
What are the priorities for the design of 5G networks?

5G design prioritizes:

  • Higher speed and capacity: roughly 10 times the peak bit rate and 100 times the traffic capacity of 4G (peak downloads of 1 Gbps or more).
  • Lower latency: roughly a tenfold decrease, down to about 1 ms for Ultra-Reliable Low-Latency Communications (URLLC).
  • Lower barriers for Internet of Things (IoT) devices: support for very many devices with reduced power requirements.
  • Flexibility to serve varied applications: a virtualized, software-based core with complete control/user-plane separation and network slicing.
U7 · S2 · Q23LQ
Why are small cell stations needed for 5G networks using FR2 frequencies?

Millimetre-wave (FR2) signals have short range and are easily blocked by buildings and trees, so a few large base stations leave coverage gaps.

Small cell stations fill the gaps between base stations and users; in a highly populated region two small cells may be only 10 to 100 metres apart. The higher cell density also raises capacity (capacity = cell density x available spectrum x spectral efficiency), which is how 5G aims for about 100 times 4G's capacity.

U7 · S2 · Q24LQ
What is the 5G Core network?

The 5G Core (5GC) is the core network of 5G systems: a cloud-native, service-based architecture in which network functions run as software (virtualized, microservice-like) and talk through standard service interfaces rather than fixed point-to-point links.

It completely separates the control plane from the user plane. Its main functions include the Access and Mobility Management Function (AMF), Session Management Function (SMF), User Plane Function (UPF), Authentication Server Function (AUSF), Unified Data Management (UDM), Policy Control Function (PCF), and Network Exposure and Repository functions (NEF, NRF).

U7 · S2 · Q25LQ
What does the 5G Core network do?

The 5G Core connects devices to data networks and manages everything except the radio. It authenticates and authorizes users (AUSF, UDM), handles registration, reachability and mobility (AMF), creates and manages data sessions and assigns IP addresses (SMF), and forwards user data to and from the Internet (UPF).

It also enforces quality-of-service and charging policies (PCF) and supports network slicing: running several logical networks with different properties (for eMBB, URLLC, mMTC) over shared infrastructure.

U7 · S2 · Q26LQ
What are the main functions of the 5G Core network? Compared to 4G Core network, what are new in 5G Core network?

Main functions of the 5G Core:

  • AMF (Access and Mobility Management Function): connection, registration and mobility management, like the 4G MME's mobility role.
  • SMF (Session Management Function): session setup, IP address allocation, and UPF control.
  • UPF (User Plane Function): forwarding of user data, like the 4G S-GW/P-GW data plane.
  • AUSF and UDM: authentication and subscriber data, together replacing the HSS.
  • PCF: policy and charging; NRF, NEF: service discovery and exposure.

New compared with the 4G core: a service-based architecture of cloud-native software functions; complete control/user plane separation (CUPS), letting UPFs be placed near the edge for low latency; network slicing; support for the three service classes (eMBB, URLLC, mMTC) with very low latency and massive IoT; and access-independent design.

U7 · S2 · Q27LQ
What are differences and similarities between 4G Core network and 5G Core network?

Similarities: both are all-IP cores that authenticate users against a subscriber database, track and manage mobility, set up tunnels from base stations to gateways, assign IP addresses, enforce policy and charging, and connect to the Internet; both separate control functions from data-carrying functions.

4G Core (EPC)5G Core (5GC)
ArchitectureFixed network elements with point-to-point interfacesService-based, cloud-native software functions
Control/user splitPartial (S-GW and P-GW mix both)Complete: SMF controls, UPF forwards
ElementsMME, HSS, S-GW, P-GWAMF, SMF, UPF, AUSF, UDM, PCF, NRF, NEF
Slicing, latencyNo network slicingNetwork slicing; UPF at the edge for very low latency

Section 3: Mobility Management

37 leading questions
U7 · S3 · Q1LQ
In order to accommodate mobile users, what considerations should be taken when designing the network layer?

The network layer must let a mobile node keep the same address and identity (so ongoing connections survive) while it changes its point of attachment, and must still be able to route packets to it.

Key considerations: how mobile the user really is (moving within one access network vs between networks); whether the address must stay the same; what supporting infrastructure exists (home and visited networks, agents, a location database such as the Home Subscriber Server or Home Location Register); how to locate the node (letting routers advertise it does not scale, so mobility is pushed to the edge, to the home and visited networks); indirect versus direct routing and the cost of triangle routing; tunneling; signaling overhead and handoff latency; and security of the registration process.

U7 · S3 · Q2LQ
From the standpoint of the network layers, what defines mobility of a network device?

Mobility is a spectrum seen from the network layer:

  • No mobility: a user who moves only within one access network (for example, between APs on one subnet) keeps the same IP address; the network layer sees no change.
  • Moderate: a user who moves between networks but powers down while moving (laptop from office to home) can simply get a new address with DHCP; no ongoing connection must survive.
  • High: a user who moves between access networks at speed while keeping ongoing connections (a phone call or video stream in a car) requires true mobility management: tracking location, rerouting, and handover.
U7 · S3 · Q3LQ
How important is the mobile node’s address?

Very important. Transport connections (for example, TCP) are identified partly by IP addresses, so if the address changes the connection breaks. Keeping a permanent address makes mobility transparent to applications and correspondents, which keep sending to the same address.

The mobile node therefore keeps a permanent (home) address and, while visiting, also obtains a care-of address; the network maps between the two. If transparency is not needed, the node can simply take a new address with DHCP.

U7 · S3 · Q4LQ
What supporting wired infrastructure is available for supporting wireless connections?

Supporting wired infrastructure includes base stations or access points connected to wired switches and routers; the home network with its home agent (or, in 4G/5G, the Home Subscriber Server (HSS) that stores subscriber information and current location, and a home gateway router); the visited network with its foreign agent (in 4G, the Mobility Management Entity (MME) and Serving and PDN Gateways (S-GW, P-GW)); DHCP servers for address assignment; and the Internet or carrier backbone that links home and visited networks and carries the tunnels between them.

In the simplest case, the user gets no special support at all and just moves among networks with new addresses.

U7 · S3 · Q5LQ
What is ad hoc networking?

Ad hoc networking means wireless hosts communicate without any base station or wired infrastructure: they form the network themselves.

The hosts must provide the services an infrastructure normally gives, such as routing (often multi-hop, each node relaying for others), address assignment, and name translation. Examples include Bluetooth piconets, mobile ad hoc networks (MANETs), vehicular ad hoc networks (VANETs), and networks deployed in disaster areas or battlefields.

U7 · S3 · Q6LQ
What issues are involved in allowing a mobile user to maintain ongoing connections while moving between networks?

To keep ongoing connections while moving between networks, the system must:

  • Keep the node's address (its identity) constant so transport connections do not break, while it gets a temporary address in each visited network.
  • Locate the node: track which network it is in now (registration with the home network or Home Subscriber Server (HSS)).
  • Route or tunnel packets to its current location (indirect or direct routing), and avoid inefficient triangle routing where possible.
  • Perform handover between base stations or networks quickly, without losing or reordering too many packets.
  • Do all this securely (authentication) and with low signaling overhead and latency.
U7 · S3 · Q7LQ
What do the terms home network, home agent, foreign (or visited) network, foreign agent, and correspondent mean?
  • Home network: the permanent "home" of the mobile node (in cellular terms, the subscriber's own carrier), where its permanent address lives.
  • Home agent: the entity in the home network that performs mobility management for the node: it tracks the node's current location and forwards (tunnels) its traffic.
  • Foreign (visited) network: the network in which the mobile node currently resides.
  • Foreign agent: the entity in the visited network that helps the node with mobility: it provides or records the care-of address, registers it with the home agent, and delivers forwarded packets to the node.
  • Correspondent: the entity (any host) that wants to communicate with the mobile node.
U7 · S3 · Q8LQ
Why should all traffic addressed to a mobile node’s permanent address be routed to the foreign network when the node is resident of a foreign network? How can this be done?

Because correspondents keep sending to the node's permanent address, which routes to the home network; if nothing happened, the datagrams would arrive at the home network while the node is elsewhere and be lost. Forwarding them to the foreign network keeps mobility transparent to correspondents and keeps connections alive.

How: the node registers with the foreign agent and gets a care-of address (COA), and the foreign agent (or node) tells the home agent this COA. The home agent intercepts datagrams sent to the permanent address and tunnels them, encapsulated inside a new datagram addressed to the COA, to the foreign agent, which decapsulates and delivers them (indirect routing). Alternatively, the correspondent learns the COA and tunnels directly (direct routing). Letting routers advertise the node's address as a host route would also work but does not scale to millions of mobile nodes.

U7 · S3 · Q9LQ
What roles does a foreign agent play?
  • Advertises its presence (agent advertisement) and answers agent solicitations, so arriving nodes discover it.
  • Gives the mobile node a care-of address (COA), usually its own address.
  • Relays registration: tells the home agent that the node is resident in its network with that COA, and relays the reply.
  • Receives the datagrams tunneled by the home agent, decapsulates them, and forwards them to the mobile node.
  • May act as the node's default router for outgoing traffic.
U7 · S3 · Q10LQ
What is a care-of-address?

A care-of address (COA) is the address associated with a mobile node in the foreign (visited) network, usually the foreign agent's address (or one obtained by DHCP when the node acts as its own foreign agent).

The home agent uses it as the destination of the tunnel: datagrams for the permanent address are encapsulated and sent to the COA, where they are decapsulated and delivered. The node thus has two addresses while visiting: its permanent address and its COA.

U7 · S3 · Q11LQ
What is a home address? What is a foreign address?

The home address (permanent address) is the mobile node's long-term address in its home network; correspondents always use it, and it never changes as the node moves.

The foreign address is the care-of address (COA): a temporary address in the foreign (visited) network where the node currently is, used to tunnel traffic to it. It changes each time the node moves to a new network.

U7 · S3 · Q12LQ
How does indirect routing work?
  1. The correspondent addresses datagrams to the mobile node's permanent address and sends them into the network as usual, unaware of mobility.
  2. They are routed to the home network, where the home agent intercepts them.
  3. The home agent encapsulates each datagram in a new datagram addressed to the node's care-of address and tunnels it to the foreign agent (in 4G/5G, the home gateway tunnels to the visited network's gateway).
  4. The foreign agent decapsulates and forwards the original datagram to the mobile node.
  5. The mobile node replies directly to the correspondent, using its permanent address as the source.

It is transparent to the correspondent and handles movement between visited networks by just updating the COA at the home agent.

U7 · S3 · Q13LQ
Suppose a mobile user is watching a streamed HD video on a smartphone connected to a visited 4G/5G network. What major steps are taken when the mobile user moves from one cell to another?
  1. Measure and report: the phone continually measures the signal strength of nearby base stations and reports to its current (source) base station.
  2. Decide: the source base station decides to hand over (weaker signal, or load) and picks a target base station.
  3. Prepare: the source sends a handover request to the target, which reserves radio resources, sets up its tunnel, and replies with an acknowledgment and the information the phone needs.
  4. Command: the source tells the phone to move to the target, and forwards any datagrams still arriving for the phone to the target, which buffers them.
  5. Switch: the phone associates with the target base station.
  6. Redirect the tunnel: the target informs the Mobility Management Entity (MME), which tells the Serving Gateway (S-GW) to switch the tunnel endpoint from the source to the target base station.
  7. Release: the target tells the source that the handover is complete, and the source releases its resources.

The visited network's S-GW stays the mobility anchor, so the tunnel from the home or visited Packet Data Network Gateway (P-GW) and the phone's IP address are unchanged; the video stream continues with at most a brief pause, smoothed by the player's buffer.

U7 · S3 · Q14LQ
How does the mobile node-to-foreign agent protocol work?

When the mobile node arrives in a foreign network it discovers the foreign agent (through agent advertisement or solicitation) and registers with it, supplying its permanent address and home agent. The foreign agent assigns or records the care-of address (COA) and registers it with the home agent, which confirms.

Afterwards the foreign agent decapsulates datagrams tunneled from the home agent and delivers them to the node. When the node leaves, it registers with the new foreign agent, which informs the home agent of the new COA; the registration with the old one expires or is removed.

U7 · S3 · Q15LQ
What problem does the indirect routing approach suffer from?
